### [1] [Global Convergence and Reliable Stopping in a Safeguarded Modified Anderson--Björck Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14193)
*Latchezar Tomov,Nedelcho Ganchovski*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper provides a rigorous global convergence proof for the modified Anderson-Bjorck (modAB) root-finding method, fixes discrepancies noted in prior versions, and adds floating-point safeguards for numerical robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The modAB method (Ganchovski & Traykov, 2023; improved 2026) showed strong empirical performance but lacked formal mathematical analysis and a convergence proof. Additionally, discrepancies were noted by L. Tomov: premature AB correction on the first AB step due to retained endpoint-update records from preceding bisection, and use of corrected endpoint ordinates rather than true residuals in symmetry factor calculation.

Method: The paper (1) constructs rigorous proofs of global convergence in exact arithmetic; (2) removes the two identified discrepancies (premature AB correction and incorrect symmetry-factor residuals); (3) introduces floating-point safeguards for interpolation, switching, and stopping, with explicit handling of infinite/NaN values, underflow, overflow, and round-off errors; and (4) enables early bisection fallback when AB fails to reduce bracket width or residual, while bounding consecutive exceptions to the width condition.

Result: Global convergence is formally established in exact arithmetic. The discrepancies are resolved and floating-point safeguards added, though these safeguards do not certify the accuracy of supplied function values. Early bisection fallback yields additional performance improvement under a finite bound on consecutive exceptions.

Conclusion: The enhanced modAB method combines proven global convergence, corrected prior flaws, and robust floating-point handling, offering improved reliability and performance for solving nonlinear equations in structural mechanics.

Abstract: The modified Anderson-Bjorck (modAB) method was first proposed in 2023 by Ganchovski and Traykov for solving nonlinear equations in structural mechanics. It was improved further in 2026 by introducing better switching criteria with account to function symmetry, and bisection fallback based on measuring the bracket reduction rate. Although the algorithm demonstrated superior performance across a great variety of functions, it lacked formal mathematical analysis and proof of convergence. In addition, some discrepancies were noted by L. Tomov in the latest version that need to be addressed: premature application of the Anderson-Bjorck (AB) correction on the first AB step, caused by retaining the endpoint-update record from the preceding bisection step, and using the corrected endpoint ordinates in symmetry factor calculation instead of the true residuals. The purpose of the present paper is to provide rigorous mathematical proof of global convergence in exact arithmetic, remove the above discrepancies, and provide floating-point safeguards for interpolation, switching and stopping, with explicit treatment of infinite and NaN values, underflow, overflow and round-off errors. These safeguards do not certify the accuracy of the supplied function values. Additional performance improvement was introduced by allowing for early bisection fallback when AB fails to make progress on reducing either the bracket width or the residual, while retaining a finite bound on consecutive exceptions to the prescribed width condition.

</details>


### [2] [Solution of a system of Hamilton-Jacobi equations on a network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14271)
*Emily R. Hammett,Maria Teresa Chiri,Kenneth D. Czuprynski,Ludmil T. Zikatanov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper extends Hamilton-Jacobi equations on networks from a single equation to a weakly coupled system of two equations on a star graph, and develops a numerical scheme to compute and analyze solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work studied Hamilton-Jacobi equations on networks for a single equation; extending to weakly coupled systems on star graphs is a natural but unaddressed generalization, requiring new numerical and analytical tools.

Method: Instead of prescribing boundary values and imposing junction conditions, the solution is computed branchwise from outer vertices toward the junction. Right-hand sides are replaced by cell averages, problems are solved exactly per cell, and the resulting iteration is solved in closed form. Junction values become affine functions of boundary values via a 2x2 row-stochastic matrix with factorable determinant.

Result: Admissible boundary values yielding continuous-at-junction and branchwise monotone solutions are characterized. When matrices are nonsingular, one branch's admissible boundary values form a convex polygon bounded by difference conditions and positivity conditions; other branches follow. The singular case is also treated and illustrated numerically.

Conclusion: The scheme yields closed-form characterizations of boundary values for continuous, monotone solutions of weakly coupled Hamilton-Jacobi systems on star graphs, with numerical experiments illustrating both nonsingular and singular cases.

Abstract: We extend the study of Hamilton-Jacobi equations on networks from a single equation to a weakly coupled system of two equations on a star graph, and we develop a numerical scheme for it. Instead of prescribing the boundary values and imposing a junction condition, we compute the solution on each branch from its outer vertex toward the junction and determine the boundary values for which these branchwise solutions agree at the junction. On each branch, we replace the right-hand sides by their cell averages and solve the resulting problems exactly on every cell. This gives an iteration whose limits we compute in closed form. From these limits, the values of the discrete solution at the junction are affine functions of the boundary values on the branch, with a $2 \times 2$ matrix whose rows sum to one and whose determinant factors into explicit contributions of the cells. We use these formulas to characterize the boundary values that give solutions continuous at the junction and monotone on every branch: when the matrices are nonsingular, the admissible boundary values of one branch form a convex polygon, bounded by conditions on the difference of the two components and by positivity conditions, and the boundary values on the other branches follow from them. We also treat the singular case, and we illustrate the results with numerical experiments.

</details>


### [3] [Optimal strong convergence of a splitting mixed finite element method for a stochastic Keller--Segel system](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14390)
*Liet Vo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A splitting mixed finite element method with fully implicit Euler time discretization is developed for a stochastic Keller-Segel system with polynomial logistic growth and multiplicative noise, achieving optimal strong convergence rates in full expectation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional mixed finite element methods for such systems require the LBB inf-sup condition and solving a coupled nonlinear system at each time step; strong convergence analysis is also hampered by the interaction between nonlinear chemotactic coupling and multiplicative noise, typically needing localization arguments.

Method: A splitting mixed finite element spatial discretization combined with fully implicit Euler time stepping, which avoids the LBB condition and decouples the nonlinear system. Analysis uses well-posedness of the variational solution, new high-moment/higher-order regularity/exponential stability estimates, and a stochastic Gronwall framework to derive error estimates directly in full expectation without localization.

Result: Strong convergence in high-moment norms with optimal rates: order k^{1/2} in time and h in space in relevant L2- and H1-based error norms. Claimed first optimal strong convergence analysis in full expectation for a splitting mixed FEM of this class of stochastic Keller-Segel systems.

Conclusion: The proposed splitting mixed finite element method provides a practical, condition-free, and provably optimally convergent scheme for stochastic Keller-Segel systems, with error estimates established in full expectation.

Abstract: In this work, we develop and analyze a splitting mixed finite element method, combined with a fully implicit Euler time discretization, for a stochastic Keller--Segel system with general polynomial logistic growth and multiplicative noise. The proposed splitting formulation allows the use of standard finite element spaces without requiring the LBB inf--sup condition associated with traditional mixed finite element methods and, at each time step, avoids the need for solving a coupled nonlinear system. We first establish the well-posedness of the variational solution and derive new high-moment, higher-order regularity, and exponential stability estimates. These estimates provide the key analytical foundation for the strong convergence analysis of the numerical method. A principal contribution of this work is the derivation of error estimates directly in full expectation, without the use of localization arguments. By combining the new stability and regularity estimates with a stochastic Gronwall framework, we overcome the difficulties arising from the interaction between the nonlinear chemotactic coupling and the multiplicative noise. As a result, we establish strong convergence in high-moment norms and obtain optimal convergence rates with respect to both the temporal and spatial discretizations. In particular, the proposed method achieves the optimal rates of order $k^{\frac{1}{2}}$ in time and $h$ in space in the relevant $L^2$- and $H^1$-based error norms. These results provide, to our knowledge, the first optimal strong convergence analysis in full expectation for a splitting mixed finite element approximation of this class of stochastic Keller--Segel systems.

</details>


### [4] [Symmetric two-level preconditioning for the method of reflections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14447)
*Jongho Park*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A symmetric two-level preconditioner is developed for the method of reflections for Laplace problems in perforated domains, with a number- and size-independent condition number bound under a geometric separation condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The method of reflections suffers from slow convergence when solving Laplace problems in perforated domains with many holes; a scalable preconditioner is needed to make it efficient and robust.

Method: A coarse correction is defined via a symmetric coercive dual formulation using constant boundary modes, then combined with parallel reflection corrections to yield a symmetric positive definite preconditioner for the dual system, solved by conjugate gradient.

Result: Under a geometric separation condition, a condition number bound is proven independent of the number and sizes of the holes and the ambient domain diameter; geometry-dependent convergence estimates for one-level methods are also derived.

Conclusion: The proposed two-level preconditioner makes the method of reflections scalable and robust, and the analysis explains why a coarse correction is essential.

Abstract: We develop and analyze a symmetric two-level preconditioner for the method of reflections for Laplace problems in perforated domains. Using constant boundary modes, we define a coarse correction through a symmetric coercive dual formulation. Combining this coarse correction with parallel reflection corrections gives a symmetric positive definite preconditioner for solving the dual system by the conjugate gradient method. Under a geometric separation condition, we prove a condition number bound independent of the number and sizes of the holes and the diameter of the ambient domain. We also derive geometry-dependent convergence estimates for the one-level methods that explain the need for a coarse correction.

</details>


### [5] [Convergence of Least Squares Methods for PDEs on R^d with Shallow ReLU^k Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14457)
*Tong Mao,Lei Shi,Hanfei Zhou*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves inverse inequalities for shallow ReLU^k networks in Gaussian Sobolev norms and uses them to establish convergence of weighted least squares PDE discretizations on R^d, with near-optimal rates in terms of network width.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shallow ReLU^k networks are promising trial spaces for solving PDEs on unbounded domains like R^d, but their theoretical foundations are incomplete. In particular, sharp inverse (Bernstein) inequalities in Gaussian Sobolev norms were lacking, which are needed to control network norms via sampling and to establish convergence guarantees for least squares discretizations. The role of parameter separation and bias magnitude in stability was also unclear.

Method: The authors establish inverse inequalities in Gaussian Sobolev norms for shallow ReLU^k trial spaces, identifying the sharp regularity threshold and quantifying dependence on parameter separation and bias magnitude. They then use these estimates to prove convergence of weighted least squares discretizations of linear PDEs on R^d using shallow networks with prescribed inner parameters and finitely many residual samples in an expanding ball, under stability and regularity assumptions. They also construct parameter sets with antipodal separation comparable to the fill distance.

Result: Under suitable stability and regularity assumptions, they obtain an error bound of order h^{k+(d+1)/2-r} δ_±^{-(S-r)} in the Gaussian Sobolev norm of order S (up to logarithmic factors), where r is the PDE order, h is the parameter fill distance, and δ_± is the antipodal separation. The results cover deterministic and random sampling and extend to fixed bounded regions by restriction. Constructed parameter sets with δ_± ≍ h yield a convergence rate in terms of network width matching a lower bound up to logarithmic factors, and numerical experiments support the theory.

Conclusion: The paper provides a rigorous framework for shallow ReLU^k network discretizations of PDEs on R^d, delivering sharp inverse inequalities and near-optimal convergence rates, and offers practical guidance on balancing accuracy and stability.

Abstract: We establish inverse inequalities in Gaussian Sobolev norms for shallow $\mathrm{ReLU}^k$ trial spaces. The estimates identify the sharp regularity threshold and quantify the dependence on parameter separation and bias magnitude. Based on these estimates, we prove convergence of weighted least squares discretizations of linear PDEs on $\mathbb R^d$ using shallow networks with prescribed inner parameters and finitely many residual samples in an expanding ball. Under suitable stability and regularity assumptions, we obtain an error bound of order $h^{k+(d+1)/2-r}δ_\pm^{-(S-r)}$ in the Gaussian Sobolev norm of order $S$, up to logarithmic factors, where $r$ is the PDE order, $h$ is the parameter fill distance, and $δ_\pm$ is the antipodal separation. The convergence results cover deterministic and random sampling and extend to fixed bounded regions by restriction. We construct parameter sets with $δ_\pm\asymp h$, yielding a convergence rate in terms of the network width that matches a lower bound up to logarithmic factors. Numerical experiments support the theoretical results and provide practical guidance on balancing accuracy and stability.

</details>


### [6] [Memory-Augmented Phase-Field Modeling: Onsager Formulation, Internal-Variable Lifting, and Structure-Preserving Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14466)
*Peng Ding,Qingxia Liu,Zhiping Mao,Zhen Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a memory-augmented phase-field (MAPF) framework via Onsager's variational principle, unifying non-Markovian phase-field dynamics, interpreting fractional models as power-law memory kernels, and providing structure-preserving numerical schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-field models with memory effects (e.g., fractional models) typically lack thermodynamic consistency and a unified framework. The authors aim to build a general, thermodynamically consistent modeling and computational framework for non-Markovian phase-field dynamics.

Method: They incorporate memory via a general relaxation kernel within Onsager's variational principle. An equivalent internal-variable representation converts the nonlocal-in-time dynamics into an extended Markovian gradient system preserving energy dissipation and mass conservation. They construct an energy-stable scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) scheme with a fast rank-one implementation exploiting the relaxation-spectrum coupling structure.

Result: Fractional models are shown to be reduced descriptions for specific power-law kernels. The scheme is energy-stable, mass-conserving, and computationally efficient with reduced storage. Numerical experiments confirm accuracy, stability, and efficiency for long-time memory phase-field dynamics.

Conclusion: The framework systematically connects memory-dependent phase-field modeling, internal-variable thermodynamics, and structure-preserving numerical computation, offering a unified and efficient approach for non-Markovian phase-field dynamics.

Abstract: In this work, we develop a general memory-augmented phase-field (MAPF) framework based on Onsager's variational principle, in which memory effects are incorporated through a general relaxation kernel while preserving the thermodynamic consistency of the underlying system. Rather than treating fractional models as fundamental formulations, we show that they can be interpreted as reduced descriptions associated with specific power-law memory kernels. The proposed MAPF equations provide a unified modeling framework for non-Markovian phase-field dynamics. To facilitate analysis and computation, we further introduce an equivalent internal-variable representation that transforms the original nonlocal-in-time dynamics into an extended Markovian gradient system. This formulation preserves key structural properties, including energy dissipation and mass conservation, and enables the development of efficient structure-preserving numerical schemes. In particular, we construct an energy-stable scalar auxiliary variable approach combined with a fast rank-one implementation that exploits the relaxation-spectrum-induced coupling structure, resulting in a computationally efficient algorithm with reduced storage requirements. The proposed framework establishes a systematic connection between memory-dependent phase-field modeling, internal-variable thermodynamics, and structure-preserving numerical computation. Numerical experiments demonstrate the accuracy, stability, and efficiency of the proposed methods for phase-field dynamics with long-time memory effects.

</details>


### [7] [A new convergence proof for an iterative scheme for elliptic Cauchy problems based on alternating orthogonal projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14600)
*Abhijit Guchhait,Adrian Muntean,Martin Lind*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes convergence of an alternating Dirichlet–Neumann iterative method for the ill-posed elliptic Cauchy problem for the Poisson equation, using a Hilbert space framework where each iteration is an orthogonal projection, yielding a geometric proof via von Neumann's alternating projections theorem and a convergence rate based on the Friedrichs angle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The elliptic Cauchy problem prescribes Dirichlet and Neumann data only on part of the boundary and none on the rest, making it severely ill-posed. Existing iterative approaches lack a transparent convergence analysis, motivating a rigorous framework to explain why the alternating method converges.

Method: The authors reformulate the alternating Dirichlet–Neumann scheme in a Hilbert space setting, showing each iteration corresponds to an orthogonal projection onto a closed subspace. Convergence is then proven using von Neumann's classical theorem on alternating orthogonal projections, and a general convergence rate is derived in terms of the Friedrichs angle between the two subspaces.

Result: The projection interpretation gives a transparent geometric view of the algorithm and a concise convergence proof. The Friedrichs-angle-based rate is shown, via an example, to degenerate for the elliptic Cauchy problem, reflecting its ill-posed nature.

Conclusion: The alternating projections framework clarifies convergence of the iterative method and provides a rate estimate, but this estimate degenerates for the elliptic Cauchy problem, indicating the need for alternative or regularized approaches.

Abstract: We consider an elliptic Cauchy problem for the Poisson equation in which both Dirichlet and Neumann boundary data are prescribed only on a portion of the boundary, while no boundary conditions are available on the remaining part of the boundary. Such problems are severely ill-posed. In this work, we study the convergence of an iterative method based on the alternating imposition of Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions. We formulate the scheme within a Hilbert space framework, where a yet another alternation becomes apparent: each iteration can now be interpreted as an orthogonal projection onto an appropriate closed subspace. This perspective provides a transparent geometric interpretation of the algorithm and yields a concise convergence proof based on von Neumann's classical result on alternating (orthogonal) projections. In addition to elucidating the convergence of the method, this alternating projections perspective also offers a general rate of convergence in terms of the Friedrichs angle (between two closed subspaces). We point out by means of an example that such convergence rate estimate degenerates for the elliptic Cauchy problem under consideration.

</details>


### [8] [Dimension-independent eigenvalue approximation for sparse matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14620)
*Natalia Hajlasz,Christopher Musco,Jonathan Weare,Robert J. Webber*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the sparsified power method for sublinear-time eigenvalue computation, proving dimension-independent runtime under certain conditions, while showing worst-case failure for sparsified submatrix selection methods despite their practical effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many science and engineering applications require solving extremely large sparse Hermitian eigenvalue problems where even linear-time algorithms are too slow. Sparsified iterative methods and submatrix selection methods work well in practice but lack theoretical understanding.

Method: The authors analyze the sparsified power method theoretically and contrast it with a prototypical sparsified submatrix selection method, providing worst-case analysis of the latter.

Result: The sparsified power method approximates a leading eigenpair to prescribed accuracy in iterations comparable to the standard power method; retained nonzeros depend on target accuracy, initial overlap, eigenvector compressibility, and relative spectral gap. When these and column sparsity are dimension-independent, runtime is dimension-independent. Conversely, submatrix selection can produce a vector orthogonal to the leading eigenvector in a single iteration, causing unrecoverable failure even with substantial initial overlap. Experiments on SuiteSparse and quantum chemistry matrices confirm practical effectiveness of submatrix selection despite worst-case limitations.

Conclusion: The sparsified power method enjoys favorable theoretical guarantees for sublinear-time eigenvalue computation, whereas submatrix selection lacks comparable worst-case guarantees yet performs well in practice across various domains.

Abstract: Many applications in science and engineering require solving sparse Hermitian eigenvalue problems that are so large that even algorithms whose runtime scales linearly with the matrix dimension are unacceptably slow. Responding to this challenge, there is a growing body of work on sparsified iterative methods and submatrix selection methods that often perform well in practice. However, theoretical understanding of these methods is limited.
  In this paper, we take a step toward better understanding sublinear-time eigenvalue computation by analyzing the sparsified power method. We prove that the method approximates a leading eigenpair to a prescribed accuracy in a number of iterations comparable to the standard power method. The required number of retained nonzeros depends explicitly on the target accuracy, the initial overlap with the leading eigenvector, the compressibility of the leading eigenvector, and the relative spectral gap. When these quantities and the column sparsity are independent of dimension, the runtime is independent of dimension.
  In contrast, we show that a comparable worst-case guarantee cannot hold for a prototypical sparsified submatrix selection method. Submatrix selection is the approach of choice in many quantum chemistry applications. However, we show that, even with substantial initial eigenvector overlap, a single iteration of the method can produce a vector that is orthogonal to the leading eigenvector, after which the method cannot recover. Nevertheless, experiments on matrices from the SuiteSparse matrix collection and quantum chemistry problems illustrate the practical effectiveness of submatrix selection methods in a variety of domains despite these worst-case limitations.

</details>


### [9] [Learning Optimal Robust-Pathwise Control of Elliptic Equations with Random Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14658)
*Qi Sun,Yanhan Cui,Ling Guo,Lili Ju*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A neural operator-based approach is proposed for distributed elliptic optimal control problems with high-dimensional random inputs, achieving both robustness and pathwise adaptivity via data-driven and physics-informed training algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional control approaches under uncertainty face a trade-off: deterministic controls are robust but lose pathwise accuracy, while stochastic controls adapt to individual realizations but suffer from the curse of dimensionality. The goal is to achieve both robustness and adaptivity for high-dimensional random inputs.

Method: A neural operator-based approach is developed for distributed elliptic optimal control problems with high-dimensional random inputs. First, a data-driven training algorithm learns the mapping from coefficient realizations directly to their optimal pathwise controls. Then, a physics-informed training algorithm bypasses the need for training data pairs, recast as weakly supervised learning using pseudo label targets produced by standard adjoint-based control updates.

Result: Numerical experiments with rough coefficients validate the effectiveness and efficiency of the robust-pathwise controls. The method outperforms the benchmark robust deterministic control on unseen coefficient realizations, owing to the generalization capability and fast inference of the trained models.

Conclusion: The neural operator-based approach successfully delivers robust-pathwise controls for distributed elliptic optimal control under high-dimensional uncertainty, offering a practical alternative that combines robustness and adaptivity without the curse of dimensionality.

Abstract: Conventional control approaches to optimization problems under uncertainty entail a practical compromise: deterministic controls offer robustness at the expense of pathwise accuracy while stochastic controls can adapt to individual realizations but suffer from the curse of dimensionality. To achieve both robustness and adaptivity, a neural operator-based approach is proposed in this paper for distributed elliptic optimal control problems with high-dimensional random inputs. A data-driven training algorithm is first established to learn the mapping from coefficient realizations directly to their optimal pathwise controls. To bypass the burden of generating training data pairs, a physics-informed training algorithm is then proposed and recast as weakly supervised learning using pseudo label targets produced by standard adjoint-based control updates. Numerical experiments with rough coefficients are conducted to validate the effectiveness and efficiency of our robust-pathwise controls, which outperforms the benchmark robust deterministic control on unseen coefficient realizations owing to the generalization capability and fast inference of trained models.

</details>


### [10] [Stable and effective restarting of the SS--RR and SS--RRR methods for computing partial eigenpairs of Hermitian matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14669)
*Zhongxiao Jia,Tianhang Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper identifies numerical instability in common restart schemes for SS-RR/SS-RRR eigensolvers and proposes a "combination restart" and hybrid scheme to restore backward stability and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The commonly used restart scheme (and its orthonormalized variant), termed the "natural restart," in CJ-SS-RR and CJ-SS-RRR methods is shown to be numerically unstable, lowering attainable accuracy and causing some of the desired n_ev eigenpairs to be missed.

Method: The authors prove the instability of natural restart schemes, then introduce a "combination restart" that forms the n×ℓ starting matrix as the product of the selected n_ev approximate eigenvectors and an n_ev×ℓ Gaussian random matrix. They further design a hybrid restart scheme combining natural and combination restarts, yielding CJ-SS-RR and CJ-SS-RRR algorithms with hybrid restart.

Result: The combination restart is proven to be mathematically and numerically superior to natural restarts. Numerical experiments show the new algorithms are numerically backward stable to working precision and more efficient than the CJ-SS-RRR algorithm with natural restart.

Conclusion: The proposed combination and hybrid restart schemes fix the instability of natural restarts and can be straightforwardly extended to SS-RR and SS-RRR methods for general standard and generalized eigenvalue problems.

Abstract: We consider stable and effective restarts of the SS--RR and SS--RRR methods for computing the eigenpairs of a large Hermitian matrix $A\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}$ with the $n_{ev}$ eigenvalues in a given interval. Taking the CJ--SS--RR and CJ--SS--RRR methods for instance, we prove that the commonly used restart scheme and its orthonormalized variant, named the natural restart schemes, are not numerically stable, thereby lowering attainable accuracy of approximate eigenpairs and missing some of the $n_{ev}$ desired eigenpairs. To cure these deficiencies, we propose a new restart scheme, called the combination restart, that takes the $n\times \ell$ starting matrix to be the product of the matrix, which consists of the selected $n_{ev}$ approximate eigenvectors, and an $n_{ev}\times \ell$ Gaussian random matrix. We prove the remarkable mathematical and numerical superiority of the combination restart over the natural restarts, then present a hybrid restart scheme that fully exploits the advantages of the natural restart and combination restart, and finally develop CJ--SS--RR and CJ--SS--RRR algorithms with the hybrid restart. Numerical experiments confirm that the new algorithms are numerically backward stable to the working precision and more efficient than the CJ--SS--RRR algorithm with the natural restart. The combination restart and hybrid restart schemes can be straightforwardly extended to the SS--RR and SS--RRR methods for general standard and generalized eigenvalue problems.

</details>


### [11] [Stochastic Geometrically Exact Beams: Continuous-Time Geometric Principles and Variational Discretizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14717)
*Minghang Du,François Gay-Balmaz,Tianzhi Li,Donghua Shi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents variational principles for stochastic geometrically exact beams in both continuous and discrete time, yielding stochastic geometric integrators that discretize time and space in parallel and generalize deterministic schemes like staggered leapfrog and Yee's scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometrically exact beams model flexible structures such as soft robotic manipulators and DNA, but no geometric integrators existed for stochastic geometrically exact beams in a variational formulation.

Method: Introduces stochastic perturbations through stochastic potentials and stochastic kinematics, deriving equations of motion from a variational viewpoint, then discretizing time and space in parallel to obtain stochastic geometric integrators.

Result: The proposed integrators are shown to be stochastic extensions of the deterministic staggered leapfrog scheme for the wave equation and Yee's scheme for one-dimensional computational electromagnetism, with numerical results confirming effectiveness.

Conclusion: Stochastic geometric integrators for geometrically exact beams are developed, providing a distributed computational framework for complex beam systems and generalizing well-known deterministic schemes.

Abstract: Geometrically exact beams naturally arise in physical phenomena and engineering practice for modeling flexible structures, such as soft robotic manipulators and DNA structures. To the best of our knowledge, there appear to be no geometric integrators for stochastic geometrically exact beams in a variational formulation. In this paper, we introduce variational principles for the dynamics of stochastic geometrically exact beams, in both continuous- and discrete-time settings. By introducing perturbations via stochastic potentials and stochastic kinematics, we derive the equations of motion from a variational viewpoint. The resulting stochastic geometric integrators simultaneously discretize time and space in a parallel manner, which provides a distributed computational framework for solving the stochastic dynamics of complex beam systems. In particular, the proposed integrators are shown to be a stochastic extension of the well-known deterministic staggered leapfrog scheme for the wave equation, and also a stochastic extension of Yee's scheme for spatially one-dimensional computational electromagnetism. Numerical results are shown to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approaches.

</details>


### [12] [Stability and Error Estimates with Optimal $\varepsilon$-Dependence for Strang Splitting of the Allen--Cahn Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14867)
*Rongfu Luo,Chaoyu Quan,Zhijun Tan,Yanyao Wu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves long-time Sobolev stability and optimal ε-dependent error estimates for the Strang splitting method applied to the Allen–Cahn equation with periodic boundary conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establishing uniform-in-time stability and error estimates with optimal dependence on the small parameter ε is important for reliably solving stiff phase-field equations like the Allen–Cahn equation over long times.

Method: The analysis combines a modified energy dissipation law with Hölder–Gagliardo–Nirenberg product estimates to derive uniform-in-time H^r-seminorm bounds and a second-order error estimate for the Strang splitting scheme.

Result: The numerical solution admits uniform-in-time H^r-seminorm bounds O(ε^{-r}+1), matching the exact solution's Sobolev bounds, and a second-order error estimate O(ε²τ²) on finite time intervals. Numerical experiments verify the optimal ε-dependence.

Conclusion: The Strang splitting method for the Allen–Cahn equation achieves optimal ε-dependent long-time stability and second-order accuracy, confirmed by numerical experiments.

Abstract: In this work, long-time Sobolev stability and error estimates with optimal $\varepsilon$-dependence are established for the Strang splitting method applied to the Allen--Cahn equation with periodic boundary conditions. By combining the modified energy dissipation law, and Hölder-Gagliardo-Nirenberg product estimates, the numerical solution is proven to admit uniform-in-time $H^r$-seminorm bounds $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-r}+1)$, consistent with the corresponding Sobolev bounds of the exact solution. A second-order error estimate $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^2τ^2)$ is further established on any prescribed finite time interval, exhibiting quadratic dependence on $\varepsilon$. Numerical experiments are presented to verify the optimal $\varepsilon$-dependence of both the stability and error estimates.

</details>


### [13] [Are implicit schemes needed in adaptive time stepping for PDEs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14884)
*Mikhail A. Botchev*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether unconditionally stable implicit time stepping is necessary when accuracy—not stability—governs the time step in adaptive simulations, and quantifies the time step penalty of switching to stabilized explicit schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implicit schemes are standard for avoiding CFL stability limits in nonstationary PDEs, but within adaptive time-accurate integration, accuracy requirements often restrict the time step anyway. This raises the question of whether expensive implicit stepping is needed, relevant for large-scale parallel computing where such switches are common.

Method: Numerical tests across two adaptive time-accurate settings: (i) nonstationary convection-diffusion equations and (ii) nonlinear heat equations, comparing implicit versus stabilized explicit schemes.

Result: The paper provides numerical comparisons of time step sizes achievable with implicit versus stabilized explicit schemes under accuracy-governed adaptive stepping (details in full text).

Conclusion: The study addresses whether unconditionally stable implicit stepping is truly needed when accuracy limits the step size, and quantifies the associated time step loss when using stabilized explicit methods.

Abstract: Implicit time integration is the standard way to overcome the tight CFL time step restriction when solving nonstationary PDEs numerically. However, within an adaptive time accurate integration, the time step size selection is governed not only by stability but also by accuracy requirements. Therefore, an eligible question to ask is: is (unconditionally) stable implicit time stepping really needed if the time step is restricted by accuracy considerations anyway? And how much do we then lose (in terms of the time step size) when switching from the expensive implicit time stepping to stabilized explicit schemes? An answer to this question is also important because such a switch is often done when solving large scale nonstationary PDEs on massively parallel computers.
  This paper attempts to answer these questions by numerical tests for two particular adaptive time accurate settings: (i)~nonstationary convection-diffusion equations and (ii)~nonlinear heat equations.

</details>


### [14] [A Domain-specific adaptation network for elliptic interface problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14887)
*Jiaqi Luo,Hongmei Kang,Xingye Yue,Shixin Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: DSAN: a shared backbone with lightweight subdomain-specific adapters solves elliptic interface problems with accuracy comparable to or better than fully decomposed multi-network methods but far fewer parameters, scaling well as subdomains grow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning offers a flexible mesh-free framework for elliptic interface problems, but existing domain-specific neural approaches use separate networks per subdomain, causing model complexity to grow rapidly as the number of subdomains increases.

Method: Proposes Domain-Specific Adaptation Network (DSAN), combining a shared backbone with lightweight subdomain-specific adapters for local representation flexibility without replicating full networks. Provides stability and error analysis showing continuous physics-informed residual controls solution error, decomposed into approximation, optimization, and sampling components.

Result: On 2D and 3D problems with multiple regular and irregular interfaces, DSAN achieves accuracy comparable to or better than fully decomposed multi-network methods with substantially fewer trainable parameters; parameter savings grow with more subdomains.

Conclusion: DSAN addresses the scalability limitation of domain-specific neural methods for interface problems, demonstrating the effectiveness of domain-specific adaptation with a shared backbone for growing subdomain counts.

Abstract: Deep learning provides a flexible mesh-free framework for solving elliptic interface problems. However, domain-specific neural approaches often rely on separate networks for individual subdomains, causing the model complexity to grow rapidly as the number of subdomains increases. To address this limitation, we propose a Domain-Specific Adaptation Network (DSAN), which combines a shared backbone with lightweight subdomain-specific adapters to provide local represen- representation flexibility without replicating a complete network for each subdomain. We further establish a stability and error analysis showing that the continuous physics-informed residual controls the solution error, and decompose the resulting error into approximation, optimization, and sampling components. Numerical experiments on two- and three-dimensional problems with multiple regular and irregular interfaces demonstrate that DSAN achieves accuracy comparable to or better than fully decomposed multi-network methods while requiring substantially fewer trainable parameters. More- over, the parameter savings become increasingly pronounced as the number of subdomains grows, demonstrating the scalability of the proposed domain-specific adaptation framework.

</details>


### [15] [Shape Holomorphy of the Helmholtz Calderón Projector and the Maxwell EFIE on $C^{1,1}$ Surfaces and Screens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14888)
*Fernando Henríquez*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves shape holomorphy of boundary integral operators (Helmholtz Calderón projector, Maxwell EFIE) on closed C^{1,1} surfaces and open surfaces/screens, enabling dimension-robust UQ for scattering with shape-parametric uncertain geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To support analytic convergence of interpolation/quadrature in high-dimensional UQ of scattering problems, one needs shape holomorphy of the underlying boundary integral operators. Existing results relied on affine-parametric representations of boundary deformations, which limits the generality of admissible shape variations.

Method: BIOs are transported to fixed reference spaces; the induced maps are shown to have bounded holomorphic extensions to complex neighborhoods of compact sets of admissible boundary transformations. The proof extends prior Schauder-space results to fractional Sobolev spaces. For EFIE, the single layer representation and surface Piola transformation yield results in Maxwell trace spaces; for open surfaces/screens, suitable restriction and extension-by-zero operators are introduced. No affine-parametric representation is assumed.

Result: Established holomorphic dependence of the Helmholtz Calderón projector on closed C^{1,1} surfaces and of the Maxwell EFIE operator on both closed and open surfaces. Shape holomorphy of the shape-to-solution map for EFIE is obtained, under interior electric Maxwell nonresonance on closed surfaces but with no such condition on screens.

Conclusion: Under suitable summability and stability assumptions, these shape holomorphy results provide an analytic basis for dimension-robust convergence of interpolation and quadrature methods in computational UQ of scattering problems with high-dimensional shape-parametric uncertain geometry.

Abstract: We establish the holomorphic dependence of the Helmholtz Calderón projector on closed $C^{1,1}$ surfaces and of the operator associated with the Maxwell electric field integral equation (EFIE) on closed and open surfaces of the same regularity. More precisely, after transporting the boundary integral operators (BIOs) to fixed reference spaces, we prove that the resulting maps admit bounded holomorphic extensions to complex neighborhoods of compact sets of admissible boundary transformations, a property referred to as shape holomorphy. A key step in our proof consists in extending the results in [Lanza de Cristoforis and Rossi, Analytic Methods of Analysis and Differential Equations: AMADE 2006 (2008), pp. 193--220] and [Dalla Riva, Luzzini, and Musolino, Inverse Problems 38 (2022), 055004] from Schauder to fractional Sobolev spaces.
  For the EFIE, the single layer representation and the surface Piola transformation give the result in Maxwell trace spaces, whereas the result for open surfaces or screens is obtained by introducing suitable restriction and extension by zero operators. As opposed to existing results, we do not rely on an affine-parametric representation of the boundary deformations, thus allowing for more general parametric shape variations.
  Finally, we establish shape holomorphy of the shape-to-solution map for the EFIE, subject to interior electric Maxwell nonresonance on closed surfaces but with no such condition on screens. Under suitable summability and stability assumptions, these shape holomorphy results provide an analytic basis for the dimension-robust convergence of interpolation and quadrature methods used in computational uncertainty quantification (UQ) of scattering problems with high-dimensional, shape-parametric uncertain geometry.

</details>


### [16] [A single LU~factorization approach to model reduction for linear system families with a quadratic matrix pencil](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14902)
*Mikhail A. Botchev,Leonid Knizhnerman,Nikolai L. Zamarashkin,Dmitry A. Zheltkov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes ODB (orthonormal derivative basis method) for solving families of large linear systems whose matrix depends quadratically on a parameter, using accumulated solution derivatives to build a subspace basis with a single LU factorization or preconditioner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional methods solve each member of a parametric linear system family separately, incurring repeated expensive factorizations/setups. A more efficient approach is needed when the system matrix depends quadratically on a family parameter.

Method: Solutions' derivatives w.r.t. the parameter are used. Accumulated higher-order derivatives, evaluated at one specially chosen parameter value, yield successively better solution-subspace approximations for the whole family. Derivatives are computed via a recurrence requiring, per step, solving a linear system with the same matrix. An orthonormal basis of the derivative span is constructed to build an efficient solver.

Result: A single sparse LU factorization (or a single preconditioner setup in an iterative scheme) suffices to solve the entire linear system family. Numerical tests show the ODB method drastically reduces computational costs versus conventional solution methods.

Conclusion: The ODB method exploits solution parameter-derivatives to efficiently solve parameter-dependent linear system families, achieving major computational savings by reusing one factorization/preconditioner.

Abstract: To solve a family of large linear systems where the system matrix depends on the family parameter quadratically, we propose a new solution approach. Our method employs the derivatives of solutions with respect to the parameter. It turns out that the accumulated solution derivatives of increasing order, evaluated at a single specially chosen parameter value, provide successively better approximations to solution subspaces for the whole linear system family.
  We show that the derivatives can be computed by a recurrence, involving, at each step, solving a linear system with the same matrix. Constructing an orthonormal basis for the subspace spanned by the derivatives then leads to an efficient solver where just a single (sparse) LU factorization (or a single preconditioner setup in an iterative scheme) suffices to solve the whole linear system family. We call the new method ODB (orthonormal derivative basis method). As shown in numerical tests, compared to conventional solution methods, the ODB method allows to drastically reduce computational costs.

</details>


### [17] [Continuous meets discrete: geometry dependence of the performance of iterative solutions for nonsymmetric PDEs in terms of Laplace eigenvalues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14946)
*F. Francesconi,J. Karátson,B. Lados,J. Rowlett*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper links spectral geometry to iterative solver performance: preconditioned GMRES/GCR convergence bounds, expressed via Laplacian eigenvalues, depend continuously on the domain and are thus stable under domain deformations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classically, Dirichlet Laplacian eigenvalues depend continuously on domain geometry under suitable restrictions. The authors aim to show this abstract theoretical property has a concrete numerical manifestation—namely stability of iterative solver convergence rates for convection-diffusion problems under domain deformations.

Method: They derive a superlinear estimate for preconditioned GMRES/GCR bounding relative residual errors by an explicit expression of eigenvalues of the Laplacian (or a symmetric positive elliptic operator in divergence form), and prove this bound depends continuously on the domain, uniformly in iteration index, under boundary regularity assumptions or a topological constraint in dimension two, plus discrete-level analogues.

Result: Continuity of the convergence bound is established, and two limiting examples are given: domains bounded by a fractal curve, where continuity still holds, and perforated domains, where it fails. Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical results, including both rough-domain examples.

Conclusion: Spectral-geometric continuity of Laplacian eigenvalues translates into stable iterative solver convergence, though this stability can break down for certain rough domains such as perforated ones.

Abstract: It is a classical fact in spectral geometry that the eigenvalues of the Dirichlet Laplacian depend continuously on the geometry of the underlying domain, under suitable geometric restrictions. We show that this theoretical property is reflected in a concrete numerical situation: the convergence rate of iterative solvers for convection-diffusion problems remains stable under deformations of the domain. The connection is a superlinear estimate for preconditioned GMRES/GCR in which the relative residual errors are bounded by an explicit expression of the eigenvalues of the Laplacian, or of a symmetric positive elliptic operator in divergence form. We prove that this bound depends continuously on the domain, uniformly in the iteration index, under either regularity hypotheses on the boundary or a topological constraint in dimension two. We also establish corresponding statements on the discrete level. Two examples delimit the theory: domains bounded by a fractal curve, where continuity still holds, and perforated domains, where it fails. The theoretical results, including both examples with rough domains, are confirmed by numerical experiments.

</details>


### [18] [Homotopy for Rectangular Multiparameter Eigenvalue Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15040)
*Bor Plestenjak,Christof Vermeersch*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A homotopy-based numerical method for solving rectangular multiparameter eigenvalue problems (rMEPs) with low memory requirements that can compute all eigenvalues and is naturally parallelizable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing numerical methods for rMEPs require substantial memory and are unsuitable for problems with a large number of eigenvalues.

Method: A novel numerical approach based on the homotopy method, which is naturally parallelizable, enabling simple and efficient acceleration.

Result: The homotopy method has low memory requirements and is able to compute all eigenvalues of an rMEP, with parallelization providing computational speedup.

Conclusion: The proposed homotopy approach offers a practical alternative for rMEPs, with an implementation and test examples available in a public repository.

Abstract: The rectangular multiparameter eigenvalue problem (rMEP) is a recently introduced form of an eigenvalue problem, in which we are looking for the combinations of parameters where the multivariate rectangular matrix loses full column rank. Existing numerical methods for rMEPs typically require substantial memory and are not suitable for problems with a large number of eigenvalues. We propose a novel numerical approach based on the homotopy method that has low memory requirements and can compute all eigenvalues of an rMEP. Moreover, the homotopy method is naturally parallelizable, providing a simple and efficient way to accelerate computations. An implementation, together with a collection of test examples, is available in a public repository.

</details>


### [19] [When Is Local Time Stepping Worthwhile? A Cost Analysis for Nested Adaptive Mesh Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15143)
*James M. Hyman*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Subcycling (local time stepping) in AMR saves work determined by the dimension of the refined feature, not the ambient space. The overhead budget β=1-1/S is derived; for a δ-dimensional feature it tends to (r-1)/(r^{δ+1}-1).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how much update work subcycling avoids in deep AMR hierarchies, and under what overhead budget subcycling outperforms global stepping. The authors argue the relevant quantity is the dimension of the refined feature, not the space dimension.

Method: Derive an overhead budget β=1-1/S (S = ideal speedup from cell counts). For each level following a feature of box-counting dimension δ with geometric counts, budget tends to (r-1)/(r^{δ+1}-1). Validate with published DG and octree local-time-stepping comparisons, and paired AMReX runs on one core using a cost model fitted on points and filled regions.

Result: Budget ≈1 for points, 1/7 (r=2) and 1/21 (r=4) for 3D surfaces, consistent with Dursi & Zingale planar estimates. Published overheads are 0.1–5%, below 1/7, comparable to 1/21. Cost model predicted speedups for curves/surfaces with 7% median error (22% on saving). Measured overhead was negative in all 17 configs, but computed from published-style speedups ranged −0.11 to 0.19; none near break-even.

Conclusion: When update costs are equal under both schedules, a single profiled run can demonstrate that subcycling pays. The overhead budget framework links dimensional feature structure to practical subcycling benefit.

Abstract: In deep hierarchies of adaptive mesh refinement, the update work that subcycling avoids is set mainly by the dimension of what is refined, not of the space. Subcycling (local time stepping) does less work than global stepping, advancing each level with the finest step, provided its extra coordination cost stays below an overhead budget $β=1-1/\Sid$ as a fraction of the global-stepping work. Here $\Sid$ is the ideal speedup that the cell counts give for equal update costs. When each level follows a feature of box-counting dimension $δ$ and the counts grow geometrically, the budget of a code that advances every cell tends to $(r-1)/(r^{δ+1}-1)$ for refinement ratio r. A point leaves a budget near one, while a surface in three dimensions leaves 1/7 at r=2 and 1/21 at r=4, values implied by the planar estimates of Dursi and Zingale. Published comparisons in discontinuous Galerkin and octree codes with local time stepping imply overheads of 0.1 to 5 percent of the work, below 1/7 and comparable with 1/21. In paired runs of one AMReX code on one core, a cost model fixed in advance and fitted on points and filled regions predicted the speedups of curves and surfaces with a median error of 7 percent (22 percent on the saving); post hoc, the update counts alone did as well except for points. The overhead, as defined here, was negative in all 17 configurations, but when computed from speedups like the published values, it ranged from -0.11 to 0.19, and no configuration came near break-even. When an update costs the same under both schedules, one profiled run can show that subcycling pays.

</details>


### [20] [Interpolation in path-connected metric spaces using splines on embedded graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15172)
*E. Fuselier,J. P. Ward,J. Zhao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A local graph-based interpolation framework using variational graph splines on small neighborhoods produces continuous-domain extensions of graph data, analyzed via energy, Lipschitz continuity, and stability, and compared to kNN interpolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph data embedded in a metric space often needs continuous-domain extensions, and simple baselines like kNN lack theoretical grounding in smoothness and stability.

Method: Local interpolants built from variational graph splines defined on small neighborhoods of graph vertices, with theoretical analysis via energy estimates, Lipschitz continuity, and stability properties; benchmarked against kNN interpolation.

Result: Energy, Lipschitz, and stability analyses characterize the local schemes; numerical experiments on the manifold of symmetric positive definite matrices show relative performance versus kNN across varying smoothness and noise.

Conclusion: The local graph-spline interpolation framework offers a theoretically grounded, continuous alternative to kNN, with experimentally demonstrated trade-offs in smoothness and noise robustness.

Abstract: We study a local graph-based interpolation framework for data defined on graph vertices embedded in a metric space. The resulting interpolants are constructed from variational graph splines defined on small neighborhoods, yielding continuous-domain extensions of the graph data. We analyze their behavior through energy estimates, Lipschitz continuity, and stability properties, and compare them with $k$-Nearest Neighbor ($k$NN) interpolation as a natural baseline due to its simplicity and widespread use. Numerical experiments on the manifold of symmetric positive definite matrices illustrate the relative performance of the proposed local interpolation schemes and $k$NN interpolation across varying levels of smoothness and noise.

</details>


### [21] [An adaptive Galerkin neural-network method for minimum-norm control of wave equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15220)
*Nguyen Gia Hien*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An adaptive Galerkin neural-network method for computing minimum-L²-norm interior controls of wave equations with spatially varying coefficients, with stability and convergence analysis plus numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing minimum-L²-norm interior controls for wave equations with spatially varying coefficients is challenging; existing approaches lack adaptive, error-controlled schemes with rigorous stability guarantees, especially when adjoint approximations (neural-network bases) introduce errors into the control reconstruction.

Method: Within the Hilbert Uniqueness Method, adjoint waves are approximated in space-time via least-squares Galerkin projections onto trial spaces enriched by residual-trained neural-network basis functions. Linear Galerkin solves give adjoint approximations, and a separate linear system reconstructs the control. The least-squares objective serves as an adaptive enrichment/stopping indicator; experiments use a Ritz-based terminal-data version requiring an eigenpair consistency estimate.

Result: Assuming observability, sufficient stability conditions are derived involving a worst-case factor √n (n = retained terminal modes) multiplying the maximal adjoint error. A fully discrete error estimate separates terminal-space truncation, adjoint approximation, and assembly errors, giving convergence to the minimum-norm control. For spectral terminal bases, additional regularity and uniform contraction yield quantitative rates, showing O(log n) accepted enrichment steps per mode suffice. Experiments on a Cassini oval, a square with a low-speed lens, and a 3D ball with graded coefficient illustrate the method.

Conclusion: The method computes minimum-norm controls reliably with provable convergence: when truncation error, the √n-weighted adjoint error, and assembly errors vanish as n grows, the scheme converges, and spectral bases admit quantitative rates with efficient adaptive enrichment.

Abstract: We develop an adaptive Galerkin neural-network method for computing minimum-$L^2$-norm interior controls for wave equations with spatially varying coefficients. Within the Hilbert Uniqueness Method, adjoint waves are approximated directly in space-time by least-squares Galerkin projections onto trial spaces enriched by neural-network basis functions selected through residual-based training. Linear Galerkin solves determine the adjoint approximations, and a separate linear system reconstructs the control. The least-squares objective provides an indicator for adaptive enrichment and stopping; the experiments use a Ritz-based terminal-data version whose continuous-error interpretation additionally requires an eigenpair consistency estimate. The main difficulty is that adjoint errors perturb this reconstruction system. Assuming observability of the continuous equation, we derive sufficient stability conditions involving a worst-case factor $\sqrt{n}$ multiplying the maximal adjoint error, where $n$ is the number of retained terminal modes. A fully discrete error estimate separates terminal-space truncation, adjoint approximation, and assembly errors, yielding convergence to the minimum-norm control when the truncation error, the $\sqrt{n}$-weighted adjoint error, and the assembly errors tend to zero as $n$ grows. For spectral terminal bases, additional regularity and uniform contraction assumptions yield quantitative convergence rates and show that $O(\log n)$ accepted enrichment steps per mode suffice to balance the neural and truncation errors. Numerical experiments on a nonconvex Cassini oval, a square containing a low-speed lens, and a three-dimensional ball with a graded coefficient illustrate the method.

</details>


### [22] [Impact of matrix topological properties on the convergence of the conjugate gradient method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15393)
*Alexander Prolubnikov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Round-off error propagation in CG is governed by the matrix graph's topology: larger graph diameters (diameter, effective diameter, pseudodiameter) slow error accumulation, keeping errors local longer and enabling more low-precision iterations before convergence stagnation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Matrix graph structure is known to affect Krylov-subspace iterative method convergence, but the link between graph topology and round-off error propagation/accumulation in the conjugate gradient method is not well understood, especially for mixed-precision computation.

Method: The authors propose a model for propagation and accumulation of round-off errors in CG, analyzing how updates to vector components (search direction, residual, approximate solution) propagate according to the matrix graph topology, with error locality bounded by the current iteration number and vertex distances.

Result: Larger topological characteristics (diameter, effective diameter, pseudodiometer) of the matrix graph correlate with slower round-off error accumulation. Errors remain local—affecting only components within distance not exceeding the current iteration—until propagation becomes global, causing loss of residual orthogonality and search-direction conjugacy.

Conclusion: For matrices with larger graph diameter (effectively, effective diameter), more CG iterations can be performed in lower-precision mode before convergence stagnation, offering practical guidance for mixed-precision iterative solvers.

Abstract: It is well known that the matrix structure affects the convergence of iterative methods, particularly those utilizing Krylov subspaces. The matrix structure, determined by its graph, dictates the propagation order of updates to the vector components that are recursively computed during the conjugate gradient method iterations. Along with the propagation of these component updates, round-off errors also propagate. We present a model for the propagation and accumulation of round-off errors and demonstrate that the larger the values of topological characteristics of the matrix graph, such as its diameter, effective diameter, and pseudodiameter, the slower the round-off error accumulates during the method's iterations, and the longer the propagation of round-off errors remains local when computing the components of the search direction, residual, and approximate solution vectors. Round-off errors arising during the computation of these components affect only those components whose corresponding vertices in the matrix graph are at a distance not exceeding the current iteration number. As the iteration number increases, the propagation of round-off errors between the vertices of the matrix graph becomes global, leading to the loss of orthogonality of residuals and conjugacy of search directions. In the context of mixed-precision computations, this implies that for a matrix with a larger graph diameter (or, more practically, effective diameter), a greater number of method iterations can be performed in a lower-precision mode before the onset of convergence stagnation.

</details>


### [23] [Construction and analysis of an explicit fourth-order symmetric low-regularity integrator for the semilinear Klein-Gordon equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15397)
*Bin Wang,Zhen Miao,Yaolin Jiang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes and analyzes an explicit fourth-order symmetric low-regularity trigonometric integrator for the semilinear Klein-Gordon equation with non-smooth solutions in 1–3 dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semilinear Klein-Gordon equations with non-smooth (low-regularity) solutions are challenging for standard integrators, which often lose accuracy or require high regularity. There is a need for a high-order scheme that remains accurate under weak regularity assumptions.

Method: The integrator is built using Duhamel's formula, a twisted function tailored for trigonometric integrals, and a symmetrization procedure for trigonometric integrators, yielding an explicit fourth-order symmetric scheme. Robust error analysis establishes fourth-order accuracy in the energy space under weak regularity in H^{2+max(μ,1)} × H^{1+max(μ,1)} with μ > d/2.

Result: The scheme achieves fourth-order accuracy in the energy space under weak regularity requirements. Numerical experiments show it is much more accurate than well-known exponential integrators for the Klein-Gordon equation with non-smooth solutions.

Conclusion: The proposed explicit fourth-order symmetric low-regularity trigonometric integrator offers superior accuracy for semilinear Klein-Gordon equations with non-smooth solutions, outperforming standard exponential integrators.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose and analyse a novel explicit fourth-order symmetric low-regularity trigonometric integrator for the semilinear Klein-Gordon equation with non-smooth solution in the $d$-dimensional space, where $d=1,2,3$. The integrator is constructed based on the full use of Duhamel's formula, the employment of a twisted function tailored for trigonometric integrals and the symmetrization procedure of trigonometric integrators. Robust error analysis is conducted, demonstrating that the proposed scheme achieves fourth-order accuracy in the energy space under a weak regularity requirement in $H^{2+\max(μ,1)}(\mathbb{T}^d)\times H^{1+\max(μ,1)}(\mathbb{T}^d)$ with $μ> \frac{d}{2}$. A numerical experiment shows that the proposed low-regularity integrator is much more accurate than some well-known exponential integrators for approximating the Klein-Gordon equation with non-smooth solutions.

</details>


### [24] [On the convergence of doubly stochastic block gradient descent method for linear inverse problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15399)
*Yuxin Xia,Zehui Zhou*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a doubly stochastic block gradient descent (DSBGD) method with early stopping for linear inverse problems, combining SGD and randomized block coordinate descent, with proven regularization properties and convergence rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SGD scales well with data size and RBCD scales well with signal size, but each alone has limitations. The authors aim to combine both advantages to solve large-scale linear inverse problems efficiently in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

Method: DSBGD integrates SGD and RBCD: each iteration randomly selects one data pair for a stochastic gradient estimate, then updates one randomly selected block coordinate. Theoretical analysis covers a priori stopping rules, source conditions, and a discrepancy principle for early stopping.

Result: The regularizing property of DSBGD is proven, convergence rates are derived under source conditions, the discrepancy principle stops the iteration in finitely many steps with high probability, and the mean squared error decreases monotonically before stopping. Numerical experiments support the theory.

Conclusion: DSBGD is the first randomized coordinate descent-type iterative method with convergence rate results from a regularization theory perspective for inverse problems, offering scalability in both data and signal size.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and randomized block coordinate descent (RBCD) belong to two fundamentally different classes of stochastic iterative regularization methods, and are promising and popular for solving large-scale inverse problems, due to their excellent scalability with respect to data size and signal size, respectively. In this work, we propose a doubly stochastic block gradient descent (DSBGD) method with early stopping, by integrating the updating strategies of both SGD and RBCD, for solving linear inverse problems in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. At each iteration, the method randomly selects one data pair to obtain a stochastic gradient estimate, and then uses it to update one randomly selected block coordinate. We prove the regularizing property of DSBGD under a priori stopping rules and then derive its convergence rates with the additional source condition and a condition on the forward operator. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first convergence rate results of randomized coordinate descent-type iterative methods for inverse problems from the perspective of regularization theory. We also prove that the DSBGD iteration can be stopped by the classical discrepancy principle in finitely many steps with high probability, and that the mean squared error decreases monotonically before stopping. Several numerical experiments are provided to complement the theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [25] [Whitney--Lagrange Decomposition for the First Family of Finite Element Differential Forms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15477)
*Long Chen,Xuehai Huang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper derives polynomial bubble duality in barycentric coordinates and builds explicit nodal bases for the first family of finite element differential forms, using log coordinates and a Hessian-of-entropy metric to explain dualities and construct interpolants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit nodal bases for finite element differential forms are needed; existing dualities and Whitney–bubble decompositions lack an elementary, geometric proof and a clear construction in general dimension and order.

Method: Barycentric and logarithmic coordinates; Whitney–bubble decomposition; negative-entropy Hessian metric whose normalized Hodge star maps bubble generators to complementary-degree polynomial forms; combining Whitney forms with shifted Lagrange polynomials; construction extended to scalar nodes unisolvent on every face.

Result: A direct geometric description of the known dualities, proof of nondegeneracy of their integral pairings, and bases dual to tangential point evaluations in every dimension and order.

Conclusion: The construction yields an interpolant independent of the roots that commutes with face restriction.

Abstract: We derive polynomial bubble duality in barycentric coordinates and construct explicit nodal bases for the first family of finite element differential forms. Logarithmic coordinates give an elementary proof of the Whitney--bubble decomposition. The Hessian of negative entropy defines a metric whose normalized Hodge star maps bubble generators to complementary-degree polynomial forms. This gives a direct geometric description of the known dualities and proves nondegeneracy of their integral pairings. Combining Whitney forms with shifted Lagrange polynomials then gives bases dual to tangential point evaluations in every dimension and order. The construction extends to scalar nodes that are unisolvent on every face. The interpolant is independent of the roots and commutes with face restriction.

</details>


### [26] [Diagonal recovery from noisy Fourier--Hermite coefficients: exact orders of the error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15610)
*S. G. Solodky,Y. A. Volynets*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies recovery of functions in weighted Wiener classes W_s^μ from perturbed Fourier–Hermite coefficients in L_∞(ℝ), deriving sharp error estimates for S^θ summation methods by exploiting Hermite function behavior near turning points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovery from perturbed Fourier–Hermite coefficients requires understanding how Hermite functions behave in both oscillatory and turning-point regions, since maxima of different indices occur at different points, enabling sharper error estimates than naive supra-sum bounds.

Method: Error estimates for summation methods of class S^θ on W_s^μ, accounting for Hermite function behavior across indices; a common lower bound for all finite diagonal methods is established.

Result: For s≠4/3 and p≠4/3 the lower bound matches the upper bound giving exact error order; at critical points the gap is at most (log(e/δ))^{1/4} up to a constant. If s>4/3 or p<4/3, this order is optimal among all recovery operators using the full data sequence.

Conclusion: The exact order of recovery error in weighted Wiener classes is determined for most parameter values, with near-tight bounds at critical points and optimality among all recovery operators in certain regimes.

Abstract: We consider the recovery of functions of the weighted Wiener classes $W_s^μ$ from perturbed Fourier--Hermite coefficients in the metric of $L_\infty(\mathbb R)$. For the summation methods of the class $\mathcal S^θ$ we find error estimates on $W_s^μ$. The estimates take into account the behaviour of the Hermite functions both in the oscillatory region and near the turning point: the maxima of functions with different indices are attained at different points, and the supremum of a sum over a block of indices is substantially smaller than the sum of the suprema. Owing to this, in particular, we establish a common lower bound for all finite diagonal methods; for $s\ne4/3$ and $p\ne4/3$ it matches the upper bound and gives the exact order of the error in this class, while at the critical points the gap between the bounds does not exceed $(\log(e/δ))^{1/4}$ up to a constant. If, in addition, $s>4/3$ or $p<4/3$, then this order is also optimal among all recovery operators that use the whole sequence of data.

</details>


### [27] [Transport Polynomial Exactness for Direct ALE-DG Methods with Nonsmooth mesh motion: A Conservative Coefficient-Evolution Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15650)
*Qiqin Cheng,Chaoyi Cai,Jianxian Qiu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A new direct ALE-DG framework restores high-order accuracy under nonsmooth mesh motion by enforcing transport polynomial exactness (TPE(k)), overcoming the uniform Lipschitz constraint of existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-order direct ALE-DG methods suffer severe order reduction under nonsmooth mesh motion even though they satisfy the geometric conservation law (GCL), due to the uniform Lipschitz constraint on mesh velocity inherent in existing schemes.

Method: Proposes a direct ALE-DG framework for moving triangular/quadrilateral meshes based on transport polynomial exactness (TPE(k)), a structural property stronger than GCL that guarantees exact transport of degree-k polynomials under arbitrary motion. A coefficient-evolution (CE) formulation incorporates TPE(k), evolving coefficients with third-order SSP-RK and applying a conservative correction via Simpson's rule that vanishes for exactly transported quadratics.

Result: The scheme retains third-order accuracy when the discrete mesh-velocity Lipschitz estimate grows as O(h^{-1}), while a representative existing direct ALE-DG scheme shows order reduction. Tests with discontinuous solutions show robust, high-resolution performance under random and Lagrangian mesh motions.

Conclusion: The proposed framework overcomes the Lipschitz constraint, rigorously satisfies TPE(2), and reconciles strict local conservation with TPE(k), enabling high-order accuracy under nonsmooth mesh motion.

Abstract: High-order direct arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian discontinuous Galerkin (ALE-DG) methods can suffer severe order reduction under nonsmooth mesh motion despite satisfying the geometric conservation law (GCL). To address this limitation, we propose a new ALE-DG framework applicable to moving triangular and quadrilateral meshes. This framework successfully overcomes the uniform Lipschitz constraint inherent in existing direct ALE-DG methods, retaining high-order accuracy even when the mesh-velocity gradients are unbounded. This capability is driven by transport polynomial exactness (TPE($k$)), which is a structural property stronger than the GCL. TPE($k$) ensures the exact transport of polynomials of degree at most $k$ under arbitrary mesh motion. To incorporate TPE($k$) into a direct ALE-DG method, we introduce a coefficient-evolution (CE) formulation. Reconciling strict local conservation, an indispensable requirement for accurate shock capturing, with the preservation of the TPE(\(k\)) property constitutes the principal technical challenge. We resolve this by evolving the coefficients using the third-order strong-stability-preserving Runge--Kutta method, paired with a conservative correction executed via Simpson's rule integration.
