Crucially, this correction strictly vanishes for exactly transported quadratic polynomials, thereby ensuring that our third-order scheme rigorously satisfies the TPE($2$) property. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the proposed scheme retains third-order accuracy when the discrete mesh-velocity Lipschitz estimate grows as $\mathcal O(h^{-1})$, whereas a representative existing direct ALE-DG scheme exhibits order reduction under the same mesh motion. Tests with discontinuous solutions further demonstrate robust and high-resolution performance under random and Lagrangian mesh motions.

</details>


### [28] [A greedy double-block nonlinear Bregman-Kaczmarz method for nonlinear equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15676)
*Wendi Bao,Jing Li,Lili Xing,Weiguo Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A greedy double-block nonlinear Bregman-Kaczmarz method is proposed for efficiently finding sparse solutions of nonlinear equations, with established convergence rates and validated competitiveness via experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding sparse solutions of nonlinear equations efficiently is challenging; existing methods may lack convergence guarantees or strong practical performance.

Method: A greedy double-block nonlinear Bregman-Kaczmarz method that combines Bregman distance with Kaczmarz-type iteration, using a greedy double-block selection strategy.

Result: Theoretical convergence rate established under certain assumptions; numerical experiments show competitive performance and superiority.

Conclusion: The proposed greedy double-block nonlinear Bregman-Kaczmarz method is effective and superior for sparse solutions of nonlinear equations.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop a greedy double-block nonlinear Bregman-Kaczmarz method to efficiently find sparse solutions of nonlinear equations. The theoretical convergence rate of the proposed method is established under certain assumptions. Numerical experiments illustrate the competitive performance and superiority of the proposed method.

</details>


### [29] [GPU-Accelerated Nested Schur-Complement Solvers for Atmospheric Flow Simulations Using Horizontally-Explicit, Vertically-Implicit Schemes and Discontinuous Galerkin Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15775)
*Marco Artiano,Andrés Rueda-Ramírez,Oswald Knoth,Hendrik Ranocha,Gregor Gassner*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: GPU-accelerated nested Schur-complement solvers enable efficient HEVI time integration for a split-form DG spectral element atmospheric model, achieving high-order accuracy and strong performance on GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HEVI time integration removes the acoustic time-step restriction from fine vertical grids in nonhydrostatic atmospheric models but requires fast solvers for the resulting vertical linear systems, which is challenging especially on GPUs.

Method: Uses a Rosenbrock-W linearly implicit Runge-Kutta scheme with sparse, column-decoupled vertical Jacobians capturing acoustic waves and gravity on curvilinear meshes. Employs nested Schur complements: a global static-condensation step on LGL sparsity and a local Schur complement eliminating all but vertical momentum. Two condensation strategies yield banded and block-tridiagonal reduced systems. Implemented in TrixiAtmo.jl for CPUs and GPUs.

Result: Schemes conserve total mass and potential-temperature density to round-off. Verified high-order convergence on linear inertia-gravity waves; demonstrated robustness on the sphere via baroclinic instability, mountain-induced baroclinic instability, and DCMIP-2025 cases. Achieves 18.8 and 2.9 simulated years per day at 100 and 50 km resolution on one NVIDIA GH200 GPU in double precision.

Conclusion: The nested Schur-complement approach provides accurate, conservative, and GPU-efficient HEVI solvers suitable for large-scale nonhydrostatic atmospheric simulation.

Abstract: Horizontally explicit, vertically implicit (HEVI) time integration removes the acoustic time-step restriction imposed by fine vertical grids in nonhydrostatic atmospheric models, but requires fast solvers for the resulting vertical linear systems, especially on GPUs. We present GPU-accelerated nested Schur-complement solvers for these systems for a split-form discontinuous Galerkin spectral element method on Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto (LGL) nodes applied to the compressible Euler equations with gravity. In time, we use a Rosenbrock-W method, a linearly implicit Runge-Kutta scheme that retains its order and stability with approximate Jacobians. This allows a sparse vertical Jacobian that captures vertical acoustic waves and gravity on curvilinear meshes with orography and decouples into independent column systems. The solvers are nested: a global Schur complement (static condensation) exploits the LGL sparsity to condense each column onto a few element-edge unknowns, and a local Schur complement eliminates all variables but the vertical momentum within each element, exploiting that the pressure depends only on the potential-temperature density. We propose and analyze two condensation strategies, which lead to banded and block-tridiagonal reduced systems. The resulting HEVI schemes conserve total mass and potential-temperature density to round-off. The solvers are implemented for CPUs and GPUs in TrixiAtmo.jl, the atmospheric extension of Trixi.jl. We verify high-order convergence with linear inertia-gravity waves in a 2D channel and demonstrate robustness on the sphere with the baroclinic instability, a mountain-induced baroclinic instability, and the DCMIP-2025 gap-flow and vortex-shedding cases. For the baroclinic instability without orography, the scheme achieves 18.8 and 2.9 simulated years per day at 100 and 50 km resolution on one NVIDIA GH200 GPU in double precision.

</details>


### [30] [Explicit charge-conserving and modified energy-conserving PIC methods using the SAV approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16028)
*Chengyu Fan,Nicolas Crouseilles,Jingwei Hu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops explicit second-order particle-in-cell (PIC) methods for the Vlasov–Ampère and Vlasov–Maxwell systems using the scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) approach, achieving exact preservation of the discrete Gauss law and a modified total energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PIC simulations of Vlasov–Ampère and Vlasov–Maxwell systems struggle to simultaneously maintain exact discrete conservation laws (Gauss law, energy) while remaining explicit and high-order accurate. This motivates the need for structure-preserving, second-order explicit schemes.

Method: The authors apply the scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) approach to construct explicit second-order PIC schemes. For each system (Vlasov–Ampère, Vlasov–Maxwell), two variants are proposed: a predictor-based scheme and a symmetric splitting scheme using a scaled electric field.

Result: The methods exactly preserve the discrete Gauss law and a modified total energy. Numerical experiments on electrostatic and electromagnetic problems confirm the expected dynamics and the conservation of both invariants.

Conclusion: The proposed SAV-based explicit second-order PIC schemes provide a structure-preserving framework for Vlasov–Ampère and Vlasov–Maxwell simulations, combining second-order accuracy with exact conservation of the discrete Gauss law and modified total energy.

Abstract: We develop explicit second-order particle-in-cell (PIC) methods for the Vlasov--Ampère and Vlasov--Maxwell systems using the scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) approach. Two schemes are proposed for each system: a predictor-based scheme and a symmetric splitting scheme using a scaled electric field. The methods exactly preserve the discrete Gauss law and a modified total energy. Numerical experiments for electrostatic and electromagnetic problems demonstrate the expected dynamics as well as the conservation properties of both invariants.

</details>


### [31] [CLNO: Conservative Local Neural Operator for Compressible Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16040)
*Buxuan Wang,Rongzu Li,Qingsong Zou*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Conservative Local Neural Operator (CLNO) for accurate and generalizable autoregressive prediction of compressible flows, combining a conservative evolution framework with fine-scale reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate and generalizable autoregressive prediction of compressible flows is challenging. Machine learning approaches need to enforce physical consistency (discrete conservation) and generalize across physical parameters, spatial resolutions, domain sizes, and boundary conditions—goals that existing operators like LNO do not fully satisfy.

Method: CLNO builds on the locality and translation invariance of the local neural operator (LNO). It uses a conservative evolution framework on local subdomains of fixed physical size, separating local mean-state evolution from fine-scale reconstruction. The flow is encoded into subdomain-integrated conservative states and fine-scale features; a shared local network predicts interface fluxes for finite-volume updates enforcing discrete conservation. A convolutional reconstruction network recovers fine-scale structures using zero-mean basis functions to preserve updated local means. Reynolds–Mach number conditioning modulates the encoding, flux-prediction, and reconstruction networks for parameter generalization.

Result: CLNO improves generalization and achieves competitive or higher accuracy compared with LNO, including order-of-magnitude error reductions in representative cases, at approximately 3–5x inference cost on periodic benchmarks. It supports prediction across different spatial resolutions, domain sizes, and boundary conditions, and generalizes to unseen parameter combinations without retraining.

Conclusion: Accurate, generalizable, and physically consistent flow prediction is achieved through conservative local evolution and fine-scale reconstruction.

Abstract: We propose a conservative local neural operator (CLNO) for accurate and generalizable autoregressive prediction of compressible flows. Building upon the locality and translation invariance of the local neural operator (LNO), CLNO introduces a conservative evolution framework based on local subdomains of fixed physical size, separating local mean-state evolution from fine-scale reconstruction. The flow is encoded into subdomain-integrated conservative states and fine-scale features, while a shared local network predicts interface fluxes for finite-volume updates that enforce discrete conservation. A convolutional reconstruction network recovers fine-scale structures using zero-mean basis functions, preserving the updated local means and providing information for subsequent evolution. To accommodate different physical parameters, Reynolds--Mach number conditioning modulates the encoding, flux-prediction, and reconstruction networks, enabling a single model to learn parameter-dependent dynamics and generalize to unseen parameter combinations without retraining. CLNO supports prediction across spatial resolutions, computational domain sizes, and boundary conditions. Numerical experiments demonstrate improved generalization and competitive or higher accuracy compared with LNO, including order-of-magnitude error reductions in representative cases, at approximately three to five times the inference cost on periodic benchmarks. These results demonstrate accurate, generalizable, and physically consistent flow prediction through conservative local evolution and fine-scale reconstruction.

</details>


### [32] [High-order uniformly accurate fully discrete schemes with long time near-conservations for two nonlinear coupled dispersive models in the nonrelativistic regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16044)
*Lina Wang,Jiyong Li,Bin Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes fully discrete Fourier pseudospectral two-scale exponential Runge-Kutta schemes for nonlinear coupled dispersive models in the nonrelativistic regime, achieving high-order uniform accuracy and long-time near-conservation of mass and energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear coupled dispersive models appear in quantum electrodynamics, nonlinear optics, and plasma dynamics. In the nonrelativistic limit, these systems feature rapid temporal oscillations of wavelength O(ε²) governed by a small parameter ε, and Yukawa-coupled interactions demand schemes that are both uniformly high-order accurate and approximately preserve mass and energy for reliable long-time simulations.

Method: The authors design fully discrete schemes combining the Fourier pseudospectral method for spatial discretization with two-scale exponential Runge-Kutta methods for time advancement.

Result: Numerical experiments on highly oscillatory Klein-Gordon-Dirac and Klein-Gordon-Schrödinger equations in one, two, and three dimensions confirm that the schemes attain the theoretically predicted uniform accuracy and long-time near-conservation of both invariants.

Conclusion: The proposed schemes deliver higher-order uniform accuracy and approximate preservation of mass and energy over long times, making them effective for simulating nonlinear coupled dispersive models in the nonrelativistic regime.

Abstract: Nonlinear coupled dispersive models arise in numerous physical settings, including quantum electrodynamics, nonlinear optics, and plasma dynamics. In the nonrelativistic limit, such systems exhibit rapid temporal oscillations of wavelength $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$, controlled by a dimensionless parameter $0<\varepsilon\ll 1$. Moreover, owing to the Yukawa-coupled interaction terms, numerical schemes that simultaneously achieve high-order uniform accuracy and approximately preserve the mass and the energy are essential for reliable simulations. In this work, we propose a class of fully discrete schemes for nonlinear coupled dispersive models in the nonrelativistic regime that achieve higher-order uniform accuracy and approximately preserve both invariants over long times. Spatial discretization is based on the Fourier pseudospectral method, and time advancement employs two-scale exponential Runge-Kutta methods. Numerical experiments on the highly oscillatory Klein-Gordon-Dirac and Klein-Gordon-Schrödinger equations in one, two, and three dimensions confirm that the resulting schemes attain the theoretically predicted uniform accuracy and the long-time near-conservation of both invariants.

</details>


### [33] [Stable Matrix-Free Q-less QR via Preconditioning and Randomization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16101)
*Oleg Balabanov,Maksim Melnichenko,Michael W. Mahoney*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Proposes CQRRTO, a randomized-preconditioned matrix-free Q-less QR algorithm that makes a single Cholesky QR pass numerically safe for any numerically full-rank tall operator, with a componentwise stability analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cholesky QR and related Gram-matrix methods suffer stability issues, especially for matrix-free/operator settings where explicit orthogonalization is costly or unavailable, and stabilized variants like Q-less Cholesky QR2 demand stronger conditioning assumptions and more operator applications.

Method: CQRRTO applies randomized preconditioning to a single Cholesky QR pass, operating matrix-free and Q-less (avoiding forming Q explicitly). A componentwise stability analysis is developed that is modular with respect to the preconditioner, covering Q-less Cholesky QR, Cholesky QR2, shifted Cholesky QR3, and CQRRTO as special cases.

Result: CQRRTO is numerically stable for any numerically full-rank operator under substantially weaker conditioning assumptions than deterministic stabilized variants (e.g., Q-less Cholesky QR2), while requiring fewer operator applications. Implemented in the open-source RandLAPACK library.

Conclusion: CQRRTO offers a more robust, efficient matrix-free Q-less QR approach, useful for least-squares solves via normal equations, spectral approximations in low-dimensional bases, and low-rank operator inversion via the Woodbury identity.

Abstract: This article develops matrix-free Q-less QR algorithms for orthogonalizing tall linear operators. Our main algorithm, CQRRTO, uses randomized preconditioning to make a single Cholesky QR pass numerically safe for any numerically full-rank operator, overcoming the stability issues of Cholesky QR and related methods that operate on the Gram matrix. Compared with deterministic stabilized variants such as Q-less Cholesky QR2 (reorthogonalized Cholesky QR), it remains numerically stable under substantially weaker conditioning assumptions while requiring fewer operator applications. Matrix-free Q-less QR can be useful for improving the numerical robustness of least-squares solves via the normal equations, spectral approximations in a low-dimensional basis, and inversion of operators with low-rank updates via the Woodbury identity. We present a componentwise stability analysis of preconditioned Q-less Cholesky QR that applies to matrix-free operators and is modular with respect to the preconditioner, yielding guarantees for Q-less Cholesky QR, Cholesky QR2, shifted Cholesky QR3, and CQRRTO as special cases. All methods are implemented in the open-source RandLAPACK library.

</details>


### [34] [A Fully Discrete, Mass-Conservative Adaptive Discontinuous Spectral-Element Method for the One-Dimensional Richards Equation in Mixed Form](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17031)
*Tristan Montoya,Raymond J. Spiteri*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A high-order, space-time adaptive mixed-form discontinuous Galerkin spectral-element method for 1D Richards equation achieves machine-precision, fully discrete water mass conservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating unsaturated flow in land models requires resolving sharp wetting fronts while strictly satisfying water mass balance, which is challenging under spatial and temporal adaptivity.

Method: Arbitrary-order local discontinuous Galerkin spectral-element scheme with collocated Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto quadrature for space; high-order Rosenbrock-Wanner time integration. Mixed formulation advances water content (conserved) with pressure-head-based diffusion, vs. pressure-head formulation using a nonlinear capacity function.

Result: Mixed formulation satisfies water mass balance exactly (in exact arithmetic) independent of resolution, time step, and adaptivity; pressure-head form conserves only semi-discretely. SFOM confirms machine-precision conservation for mixed form; pressure-head shows above-roundoff errors. Temporal refinement: mixed form is more accurate at fixed time step.

Conclusion: High-order accuracy, space-time adaptivity, and fully discrete mass conservation make the mixed-form discontinuous spectral-element method promising for next-generation land-model unsaturated flow simulation.

Abstract: We present and analyze a spatially and temporally adaptive high-order method for the one-dimensional Richards equation describing vertical water transport in unsaturated soil, with an emphasis on resolving sharp wetting fronts while satisfying a fully discrete water mass balance. The spatial discretization uses an arbitrary-order local discontinuous Galerkin spectral-element formulation with collocated Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto quadrature, and the time integration is based on a high-order Rosenbrock-Wanner method. We focus primarily on the mixed formulation of the Richards equation, which advances water content as the conserved variable while evaluating the nonlinear diffusion operator in terms of pressure head; the resulting fully discrete scheme satisfies the water mass balance in exact arithmetic, independent of spatial resolution and time step size, including under spatial and temporal adaptivity. In contrast, the pressure-head formulation advances pressure head through a nonlinear capacity function and satisfies the corresponding conservation identity only at the semi-discrete level. Numerical experiments examine convergence through spatial and temporal refinement studies, with mass conservation assessed under spatial and temporal adaptivity using the solver flux output method (SFOM). The SFOM results confirm machine-precision mass conservation for the mixed formulation, whereas the pressure-head formulation exhibits mass balance errors above roundoff. Moreover, in the temporal refinement studies, the mixed formulation yields more accurate solutions than the pressure-head formulation for a prescribed time step size. The combination of high-order accuracy, spatial and temporal adaptivity, and fully discrete mass conservation makes the proposed mixed-form discontinuous spectral-element method a promising approach for simulating unsaturated flow in next-generation land models.

</details>


### [35] [An algebraic multiscale framework for two-level overlapping Schwarz preconditioners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16359)
*Filipe Cumaru,Hadi Hajibeygi,Cornelis Vuik,Alexander Heinlein*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A fully algebraic multiscale coarse space for two-level overlapping additive Schwarz preconditioners, built from the system matrix alone, delivers contrast-robust convergence for highly heterogeneous elliptic problems and extends to linear elasticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The robustness of two-level OAS preconditioners for highly heterogeneous elliptic problems depends critically on the coarse space choice; existing MsFEM-based coarse spaces typically require geometric/analytic coefficient information, limiting applicability to general unstructured decompositions and elasticity.

Method: An algebraic multiscale (AM) coarse space uses an algebraic formulation of MsFEM built solely from the system matrix. Coarse basis functions are energy-minimizing extensions of reduced boundary condition (RBC) solutions on subdomain interfaces, posed on slabs extending the interface to support unstructured decompositions. Embedding in an energy-minimizing framework extends the approach to linear elasticity.

Result: Existing condition number bounds for OAS with MsFEM coarse spaces carry over to the algebraic setting. For high-coefficient inclusions on coarse nodes, iteration count and condition number are unaffected by increasing contrast. In 3D log-normal diffusion, AM needs about as many iterations as GDSW while solving a coarse problem ten times smaller. For elasticity, omitting rotational rigid body mode coarse basis functions still scales well for homogeneous problems.

Conclusion: The proposed algebraic coarse space gives a practical, contrast-robust OAS preconditioner requiring only the system matrix, providing near-GDSW performance with a much smaller coarse problem and a fully algebraic elasticity variant.

Abstract: The robustness of two-level overlapping additive Schwarz (OAS) preconditioners for highly heterogeneous elliptic problems hinges on the choice of the coarse space. We propose an algebraic multiscale (AM) coarse space built from the system matrix alone through an algebraic formulation of the multiscale finite element method (MsFEM). The coarse basis functions are energy-minimizing extensions of the solutions of reduced boundary condition (RBC) problems on the subdomain interface, so that coefficient variations along the interface enter the coarse space. By posing the RBC problems on slabs that extend the interface, the construction also applies to general unstructured domain decompositions. Embedding the AM formulation in an energy-minimizing framework extends it to linear elasticity, and we show that existing condition number bounds for OAS with MsFEM coarse spaces carry over to the algebraic setting. For problems with high coefficient inclusions on the coarse nodes, the AM iteration count and condition number are unaffected by increasing the contrast. In 3D scalar diffusion with log-normal coefficients, AM needs about as many iterations as the generalized Dryja--Smith--Widlund (GDSW) coarse space, while solving a coarse problem ten times smaller. For linear elasticity, omitting the coarse basis functions associated with the rotational rigid body modes yields a fully algebraic coarse space that still scales well for homogeneous problems.

</details>


### [36] [On the cyclic limiting behavior of the restarted Arnoldi algorithm for real symmetric matrices with restart length $s=2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16492)
*Vance Faber,Jarek Liesen,Jörg Liesen,Petr Tichý*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves the ACI(s) conjecture for real symmetric matrices with restart length s=2, showing that restarted Arnoldi has a cyclic limiting behavior, which also settles the Forsythe conjecture for the conjugate gradient method in that case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The long-standing Forsythe conjecture concerns the limiting behavior of the conjugate gradient method, and the ACI(s) conjecture generalizes it to restarted Arnoldi. A rigorous, transparent proof for s=2 was lacking, and the authors aim to provide one that is instructive to human readers.

Method: The authors give an extensively rewritten and simplified proof for the case of real symmetric A with restart length s=2. The original proof was found using Codex with GPT-5.6 Sol, and the paper reformulates it for clarity, discussing its origin and evolution.

Result: The restarted Arnoldi algorithm with s=2 exhibits cyclic limiting behavior, proving the ACI(s) conjecture at s=2 and consequently the Forsythe conjecture for s=2.

Conclusion: The ACI(s) and Forsythe conjectures are resolved for the case s=2, with a transparent proof intended to benefit interested readers; related recent developments are also discussed.

Abstract: We prove that the restarted Arnoldi algorithm for real symmetric matrices with restart length $s=2$ has a cyclic limiting behavior. In the terminology established in (arXiv:2209.14579), this is a proof of the ACI($s$) conjecture for $A=A^T\in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ and $s=2$. As explained in (arXiv:2209.14579), this also proves the Forsythe conjecture (Numerische Mathematik, 11 (1968), pp. 57-76) about the limiting behavior of the conjugate gradient method and positive definite $A=A^T\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ for $s=2$. The proof we give is an extensively rewritten and simplified version of a proof found using Codex with GPT-5.6 Sol. At the end of this paper, we discuss its origin and evolution, as well as related recent developments around the Forsythe conjecture. Our motivation is to present a proof that is instructive and transparent, so that it can enhance the knowledge of interested human readers.

</details>


### [37] [Neural Network Preconditioning: A Case Study for the Solution of the Parametric Helmholtz Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16542)
*Luc Giraud,Carola Kruse,Paul Mycek,Maksym Shpakovych,Yanfei Xiang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A self-supervised U-Net is trained to approximate the inverse of the discretized 2D parametric Helmholtz operator and used as a nonlinear preconditioner in FGMRES, yielding faster and robust convergence than LU-based algebraic preconditioners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving linear systems from discretized parametric Helmholtz equations is challenging, and existing algebraic preconditioners based on LU factorizations of approximate matrices are costly. The authors aim for a learned, architecture-agnostic preconditioner that generalizes across velocity fields and domain sizes.

Method: A U-Net CNN is trained in a self-supervised manner (no reference solutions) using a loss based on the residual norm of the linear system to approximate the inverse of the discretized Helmholtz operator. The trained network is then embedded as a nonlinear preconditioner within flexible GMRES (FGMRES).

Result: The network is not accurate enough as a standalone solver, but significantly accelerates FGMRES convergence as a preconditioner. It shows robust performance and generalization to varying velocity fields and domain sizes, and outperforms algebraic LU-based preconditioners under equivalent conditions.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid neural-preconditioned FGMRES approach is more efficient than LU-based algebraic preconditioners and is not tied to a specific architecture, suggesting extension to other parametric PDEs.

Abstract: This work presents a hybrid numerical approach for solving linear systems arising from the discretization of the two-dimensional parametric Helmholtz equation. A convolutional neural network based on the U-Net architecture is trained in an self-supervised manner (i.e., without explicitly generating reference solutions) to approximate the inverse of the discretized Helmholtz operator, using a loss function involving the residual norm of the linear system. The trained network is used as a nonlinear preconditioner within the flexible GMRES (FGMRES) algorithm. Numerical experiments show that while the neural network is not accurate enough to act as a standalone solver, it significantly improves the convergence of FGMRES when employed as a preconditioner. The neural preconditioner demonstrates robust performance and generalization capabilities with respect to variations in the velocity field and the domain size. Comparisons with algebraic preconditioners, based on LU factorizations of approximations of the original system matrices, indicate the superior efficiency of the neural approach under equivalent conditions. We believe that the proposed method is not tied to a specific neural architecture and can be extended to other parametric PDEs.

</details>


### [38] [A Method for Exact Enforcement of Boundary Conditions on General Curved Triangular Domains: Algorithm and Application to Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17392)
*Suchuan Dong,Yuchuan Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A method enforces Dirichlet, Neumann, and Robin boundary conditions exactly on general curved triangular domains by combining curved-to-standard triangle mapping, TFC constrained expressions, and Barnhill transfinite interpolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extends prior work on general quadrilateral domains to triangular domains with arbitrarily curved boundaries, addressing the difficulty of exactly satisfying boundary conditions, especially corner compatibility at acute vertices and gradient-coupling on Neumann/Robin hypotenuses.

Method: Combines (i) exact mapping between a general curved triangular domain and the standard triangle, (ii) TFC constrained expressions, and (iii) Barnhill transfinite interpolations to yield closed-form trial ansatze. Corner compatibility is handled by reformulating conditional corner constraints into equivalent unconditional forms and introducing a separate TFC-style auxiliary edge construction with a linear solve on the hypotenuse. Implemented via extreme learning machine type randomized neural networks.

Result: Numerical experiments on linear/nonlinear PDEs over varied complex curved domains show Dirichlet, Neumann, and Robin conditions enforced to machine precision.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a general formulation for functions exactly satisfying Dirichlet, Neumann, or Robin conditions on curved triangular domains, achieving machine-precision boundary enforcement.

Abstract: We present a method for enforcing exactly Dirichlet, Neumann, and Robin conditions on general triangular domains with arbitrarily curved boundaries. The algorithmic constructions developed here complements those in a previous work for general quadrilateral domains. Our method combines (i) the exact mapping between a general curved triangular domain and the standard triangle, (ii) TFC constrained expressions, and (iii) Barnhill transfinite interpolations, to provide explicit closed-form trial ansatze that mathematically satisfy the imposed boundary conditions exactly. Corner compatibility induced by the boundary conditions is pivotal, and enforcing exactly the imposed boundary conditions hinges on the exact enforcement of the induced compatibility constraints. The corner compatibility associated with the acute vertices of the standard triangle and the coupling between gradient components when the hypotenuse corresponds to a Neumann/Robin boundary pose particular challenges. Two strategies are critical to resolving these issues, by reformulating conditional corner constraints into equivalent unconditional forms and by introducing a separate TFC-style auxiliary edge construction together with an associated linear solve on the hypotenuse. We analyze the corner compatibility constraints and present formulations for the general form of functions that exactly satisfy the imposed Dirichlet, Neumann, or Robin conditions on curved triangular domains. The proposed method has been implemented using extreme learning machine type randomized neural networks for solving PDE problems. We present ample numerical experiments with linear/nonlinear PDE problems on a variety of domains with complex boundary geometries. Numerical results show that the current method has enforced the Dirichlet, Neumann, and Robin conditions on curved domain boundaries to the machine precision.

</details>


### [39] [Globally Convergent Jacobi-Type Algorithms for Robust Approximate Diagonalization of Higher-Order Cumulant Tensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16649)
*Jianze Li,Zhenyu Cai,Wentao Ding,Konstantin Usevich*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces Jacobi-SubG, a Jacobi-type framework for robust approximate diagonalization of higher-order cumulant tensors via ℓ1-based contrast functions, using Riemannian subdifferential information for index-pair selection with global convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sums of absolute values of higher-order cumulants are classical ICA contrast functions, naturally leading to ℓ1-based diagonalization over orthogonal/unitary groups. Existing methods lack global convergence guarantees, particularly for general tensor models.

Method: Jacobi-SubG selects index pairs using Riemannian subdifferential information and applies plane rotations with a refinement step preserving nonzero diagonal contractions. Specialized subproblem solvers are developed for real and complex third- and fourth-order cumulant tensors.

Result: Proved global convergence of the entire iterate sequence from any initialization with nonzero diagonal contractions, given uniform sufficient-ascent subproblem solvers. Numerical experiments on ICA and tensor diagonalization show better source recovery and higher final objective values, especially at higher noise levels.

Conclusion: Jacobi-SubG provides theoretically grounded, globally convergent algorithms for cumulant tensor diagonalization, with practical advantages over existing methods in ICA and tensor diagonalization tasks.

Abstract: The sums of the absolute values of higher-order cumulants are classical contrast functions for independent component analysis (ICA) and lead naturally to robust approximate diagonalization of higher-order cumulant tensors using $\ell_1$-based diagonalization over the orthogonal or unitary group. We propose Jacobi-SubG, a Jacobi-type framework that selects index pairs using Riemannian subdifferential information and performs plane rotations with a refinement step that preserves nonzero diagonal contractions. For the general tensor model, we prove global convergence of the entire iterate sequence from any initialization with nonzero diagonal contractions, provided that the subproblem solvers satisfy a uniform sufficient-ascent estimate. For real and complex third- and fourth-order cumulant tensors, which are widely used in ICA, we develop specialized subproblem solvers satisfying these conditions. The resulting algorithms thus enjoy global convergence under the simple initialization condition that all diagonal contractions are nonzero. Numerical experiments on ICA and tensor diagonalization show that the proposed Jacobi-SubG variants achieve better source recovery and higher final objective values in several settings, particularly at the higher tested noise levels.

</details>


### [40] [Matrix algebras with orthogonal Cauchy-like transforms and Toeplitz approximation: a negative result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16723)
*Dario Fasino,Davide Furchì,Valerio Loi,Stefano Serra-Capizzano*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper tests whether Toeplitz matrices can be approximated by algebras diagonalized by Cauchy orthogonal transforms, using Korovkin theory. The test is negative, unlike the successful case for Fourier-like/trigonometric algebras.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate fast preconditioning algorithms: algebras with fixed orthogonal transforms (Fourier-like) successfully approximate Toeplitz matrices, motivating the study of new Cauchy orthogonal transform algebras.

Method: Define matrix algebras associated with Cauchy orthogonal transforms; apply Korovkin theory to test Toeplitz approximability of these algebras; use Frobenius optimal approximation and ad hoc numerical experiments.

Result: The Korovkin test is negative for Cauchy algebras—surprisingly, contrary to all Fourier-like and trigonometric algebras. The Frobenius optimal approximation fails to produce the desired clustering, confirmed by numerical experiments.

Conclusion: Toeplitz matrices cannot be approximated (in the clustering sense) by Cauchy orthogonal transform algebras via the standard optimal approximation. It remains open which structured matrices these new algebras can approximate.

Abstract: We consider newly designed matrix algebras associated with Cauchy orthogonal transforms. In this work, we are interested in checking the approximation of Toeplitz matrices using algebras of matrices, where each matrix is diagonalized by a fixed Cauchy orthogonal transform, in the spirit of fast algorithm based on preconditioning. We use the Korovkin theory for checking the Toeplitz approximability. Unfortunately and somehow surprisingly, the Korovkin test is negative, in contrast with the successful test for all the Fourier-like and trigonometric algebras, widely used in the relevant literature. More in detail, for the Cauchy algebras, the Frobenius optimal approximation fails to lead to the desired clustering, as also confirmed by ad hoc numerical experiments. It remains open to understand which kind of structured matrices can be approximated by these new algebras.

</details>


### [41] [Additive results of the dual Moore--Penrose generalized inverse with an application to rigid-body motion estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16852)
*Yup Zhao,Daochang Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper derives explicit formulas for the dual Moore–Penrose generalized inverse (DMPGI) of sums of dual complex matrices under certain conditions, and applies them to rank-deficient rigid-body motion estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dual complex matrices are useful for rigid-body motion and kinematics, but the Moore–Penrose inverse for sums of such matrices lacks explicit, usable formulas, especially in rank-deficient settings with perturbations.

Method: The DMPGI of a column-block-partitioned matrix is expressed via its blocks and associated projectors; two formulas for dual complex matrix sums are then derived, followed by worked examples and application to two-observation-point motion estimation under a rank-preserving first-order position perturbation.

Result: Explicit DMPGI formulas for dual complex matrix sums are obtained, verified by two examples and applied to recover observable motion components from velocity data in rank-deficient two-point rigid-body estimation.

Conclusion: The derived DMPGI formulas provide a practical tool for dual complex matrix analysis and enable recovery of observable motion components in rank-deficient rigid-body motion estimation.

Abstract: We derive explicit formulas for the dual Moore--Penrose generalized inverse (DMPGI) of dual complex matrix sums under some conditions. We first express the DMPGI of a matrix partitioned into column blocks in terms of these blocks and associated projectors, and then derive two formulas for matrix sums. Two examples illustrate the results. We further apply the formulas to rigid-body motion estimation with two observation points subject to a first-order perturbation of their positions that preserves the rank-deficient observation geometry. The DMPGI recovers the observable motion components from the velocity data.

</details>


### [42] [Quantitative asymptotic error analysis and Richardson extrapolation for exponential Euler approximations of stochastic wave equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16896)
*Jianbo Cui,Diancong Jin,Huagui Liu,Derui Sheng*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Quantitative weak convergence is established for the joint terminal law of a stochastic wave equation and its exponential Euler approximation's normalized error, with rate $O(m^{-(\alpha-1)})$ and a sharp Richardson extrapolation scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic wave equations lack parabolic smoothing, making weak error analysis and controlling normalized remainders challenging. Prior work lacked quantitative weak convergence rates and sharp extrapolation for multiplicative trace-class noise in this setting.

Method: Combines dimension-uniform regularity estimates for the joint Kolmogorov equation with a conditional weak expansion and backward transport of coefficient directions, for multiplicative trace-class noise and spatial regularity $\alpha\in(1,2]$.

Result: The normalized error converges to its limiting distribution at rate $O(m^{-(\alpha-1)})$, identifying the leading weak bias and giving a first-order weak-error expansion with remainder $O(m^{-\alpha})$. A Richardson extrapolation scheme achieves weak convergence order $\alpha$, shown to be sharp.

Conclusion: The analysis provides sharp weak convergence rates for exponential Euler approximation of stochastic wave equations and yields an optimal Richardson extrapolation scheme for $\alpha\in(1,2]$.

Abstract: We establish quantitative weak convergence for the joint terminal law of the solution to a stochastic wave equation and the normalized error of its exponential Euler approximation. For multiplicative trace-class noise and spatial regularity $α\in(1,2]$, the normalized error converges to its limiting distribution at rate $O(m^{-(α-1)})$, where $m$ is the number of time steps. This estimate identifies the leading weak bias and yields a first-order weak-error expansion with remainder $O(m^{-α})$. Moreover, by canceling the leading weak-error term, we construct a Richardson extrapolation scheme with weak convergence order $α$ for $α\in(1,2]$ and show that this rate is sharp. To overcome the lack of parabolic smoothing and control the remainders amplified by normalization, we combine dimension-uniform regularity estimates for the joint Kolmogorov equation with a conditional weak expansion and backward transport of coefficient directions.

</details>


### [43] [Simultaneous Matrix-Free HSS Compression and Factorization with Adaptive Ranks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16913)
*Elly Do,Chao Chen*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A fully matrix-free algorithm compresses and factorizes hierarchically low-rank matrices into HSS form using only matrix-vector products, with adaptively chosen ranks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many dense matrices in scientific computing have hierarchically low-rank off-diagonal structure but are only accessible through matvec routines (applying the matrix and its transpose/adjoint to vectors), not explicitly. This limits applicability of standard HSS compression/factorization methods that require explicit entries.

Method: The method performs fully matrix-free compression into HSS form while simultaneously factorizing, choosing off-diagonal ranks adaptively from a tolerance. It builds on fixed-rank block nullification, samples the matrix in small random batches, enlarges each local basis until a randomized error estimate drops below tolerance, and uses unitary nullspace updates giving O(k^3) local dense work.

Result: Uses O(k) matvecs per operator (k = largest off-diagonal rank); O(N) arithmetic and storage when ranks are bounded (excluding matvecs); inverse applied in O(N) time. Demonstrated on Poisson and Helmholtz Schur complements, Matérn covariance matrices, and a complex Lippmann-Schwinger equation with millions of unknowns.

Conclusion: The algorithm enables direct solving and preconditioning of problems with up to millions of unknowns using only matvec access, with cost matching known-rank cases and no separate factorization step.

Abstract: Many dense matrices in scientific computing have hierarchically low-rank off-diagonal structure but are accessible only through routines that apply the matrix and, in general, its transpose or adjoint to vectors, that is, through matrix--vector products (matvecs). We present a fully matrix-free algorithm that compresses such a matrix into Hierarchically Semi-Separable (HSS) form and factorizes it simultaneously with the off-diagonal ranks chosen adaptively from a prescribed tolerance. The method uses $\mathcal{O}(k)$ matvecs with each operator, where $k$ is the largest off-diagonal rank found. Building on fixed-rank block nullification, it samples the matrix in small random batches and enlarges each local basis until a randomized error estimate falls below the tolerance. Unitary nullspace updates give $\mathcal{O}(k^3)$ local dense work, the same asymptotic cost as when the ranks are known in advance. Because compression and elimination proceed together, no separate factorization step is required. Excluding the matvecs, the factorization uses $\mathcal{O}(N)$ arithmetic and storage when the off-diagonal ranks are bounded, and its inverse is applied in $\mathcal{O}(N)$ time. Experiments on Poisson and Helmholtz Schur complements, Matérn covariance matrices, and a complex Lippmann--Schwinger equation demonstrate the factorization as both a direct solver and a preconditioner on problems with up to millions of unknowns.

</details>


### [44] [Optimal Monte Carlo integration on matrix manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16965)
*Aleksei G. Sorokin,Yihan Guo,Lek-Heng Lim*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Develops optimal-order weighted cubature rules for Monte Carlo integration in Sobolev RKHS over Stiefel, Grassmann, flag, and Lagrangian Grassmann manifolds (over R, C, H), using i.i.d. uniform points and kernel-optimal weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical integration over matrix manifolds is needed but standard kernel-based cubature requires the kernel mean embedding (integral of kernel), which is often intractable on such manifolds. The goal is optimal cubature without needing this difficult-to-compute quantity.

Method: Constructs weighted cubature rules with i.i.d. uniform evaluation points on each manifold. Weights are optimized for RKHS worst-case error under the constraint that weights sum to one, avoiding kernel mean embedding evaluation. Uses computable Matérn kernels of Frobenius distances reproducing Sobolev RKHS with equivalent norms.

Result: Achieves optimal order up to logarithmic factors. Open-source numerical experiments validate theory, including simultaneous approximation of intrinsic volumes in the Cauchy--Kubota formula. Preliminary results extend beyond independent points via transformed low-discrepancy QMC points and jointly optimized points/weights.

Conclusion: Optimal-order weighted cubature is attainable on common matrix manifolds with a weight-sum constraint that sidesteps kernel mean embedding computation, with strong empirical support and promise for QMC and joint optimization extensions.

Abstract: This article develops optimal order (up to logarithmic factors) weighted cubature rules for Monte Carlo numerical integration in Sobolev reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces over common matrix manifolds. Specifically, we consider matrix models of the Stiefel, Grassmann, flag, and Lagrangian Grassmann manifolds each over either real numbers $\mathbb{R}$, complex numbers $\mathbb{C}$, or quaternions $\mathbb{H}$. The proposed cubature rules use evaluation points which are independent and identically distributed uniform on the manifold. The weights are chosen to be optimal with respect to the reproducing kernel Hilbert space worst-case error, under the constraint that the weights sum to one. Crucially, this constraint ensures optimal weights may be found without having to evaluate the kernel mean embedding (the integral of the kernel), which is often difficult to compute. We consider computable Matérn kernels of Frobenius distances between matrices which reproduce the Sobolev reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces with equivalent norms. Open-source numerical experiments validate our theoretical development, including an application to simultaneous approximation of intrinsic volumes in the Cauchy--Kubota formula. We also present preliminary empirical results beyond independent points including transformations of low-discrepancy quasi-Monte Carlo points from the unit cube and jointly optimized points and weights.

</details>


### [45] [Edge-preserving Bayesian neural networks via hierarchical sparsity-promoting priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17023)
*Jan Habscheid,Jan Glaubitz*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes GSBL-BNNs, Bayesian neural networks using hierarchical generalized sparse Bayesian learning priors with bias-weight coupling, a block-coordinate descent MAP algorithm, and deep-ensemble UQ for edge-preserving inverse problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering piecewise-smooth functions with jump discontinuities from noisy, indirect, and incomplete data is central to inverse problems. Heavy-tailed BNN priors are effective edge-preserving priors, but posterior inference is hard and existing methods only give point estimates via optimizing heavy-tailed log-densities.

Method: Model network parameters hierarchically with GSBL sparsity-promoting priors, recovering Laplace and Student's t (incl. Cauchy) marginals. Couple each neuron's bias to its weights via a shared hyperparameter, allowing jumps anywhere while preserving conditionally Gaussian structure. Develop a block-coordinate descent (BCD) MAP algorithm alternating reweighted l2 network training with closed-form gamma/inverse-gamma hyperparameter updates; add deep-ensemble approximate Bayesian inference and UQ.

Result: Experiments on signal denoising, deblurring, and inverting a 2D hyperbolic conservation law show accurate, edge-preserving reconstructions with built-in UQ. Versus Gaussian- and Cauchy-BNNs, GSBL-BNNs give more accurate predictive means and avoid overconfident uncertainty.

Conclusion: GSBL-BNNs offer an efficient, principled framework for edge-preserving Bayesian inverse problems, combining hierarchical heavy-tailed priors, tractable BCD inference, and reliable uncertainty quantification.

Abstract: Recovering piecewise-smooth functions with jump discontinuities from noisy, indirect, and incomplete data is a central task in computational inverse problems. Bayesian neural networks (BNNs) with heavy-tailed weight priors have emerged as effective edge-preserving priors for such problems. However, posterior inference for these models remains computationally challenging, and existing approaches settle for point estimates obtained by directly optimizing heavy-tailed log-densities. We propose GSBL-BNNs, which model the neural network parameters hierarchically using sparsity-promoting priors from generalized sparse Bayesian learning (GSBL). The hierarchical formulation recovers several popular marginal priors, including Laplace and Student's $t$ (including Cauchy). A key modeling ingredient, novel among edge-preserving BNNs and native to the hierarchical formulation, couples each neuron's bias to its weights through a shared hyperparameter, so that the induced jump discontinuities are free to occur anywhere in the domain while the conditionally Gaussian structure underlying efficient inference is preserved. Furthermore, we develop a block-coordinate descent (BCD) algorithm for maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation. This BCD approach alternates between standard network training with iteratively reweighted $\ell^2$-regularization and exact, closed-form hyperparameter updates for gamma and inverse gamma hyperpriors. Building on the BCD algorithm, we perform approximate Bayesian inference and uncertainty quantification (UQ) using deep ensembles. Computational experiments---including signal denoising, deblurring, and inverting a two-dimensional hyperbolic conservation law--- show that GSBL-BNNs deliver accurate, edge-preserving reconstructions with built-in UQ. Compared with Gaussian- and Cauchy-BNNs, they yield more accurate predictive means and avoid overconfident uncertainty estimates.

</details>


### [46] [Simultaneous energy and entropy stability of volume-penalisation immersed-boundary methods for the compressible Navier--Stokes equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17973)
*Abbas Ballout,Albert Jiménez-Ramos,Gonzalo Rubio,Esteban Ferrer*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This work analyzes volume-penalised compressible Navier-Stokes equations for energy and entropy stability and shows that entropy-stable split-form DG spectral element methods remain semi-discretely entropy stable when the source is integrated exactly in time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Immersed-boundary methods avoid body-fitted meshes, but energy or entropy stability of the base flow discretisation does not automatically carry over to the immersed treatment, motivating a rigorous stability analysis.

Method: The volume-penalised compressible Navier-Stokes equations are analyzed at continuous and semi-discrete levels. The source is formulated as a one-parameter family indexed by theta, with a momentum component relaxing fluid velocity toward the solid velocity; theta controls how much kinetic-energy loss leaves the domain versus converting to internal energy. The isolated source is integrated exactly in time.

Result: Opposing bounds on theta are found: dissipation of relative total energy requires theta >= 0, while entropy stability requires theta <= 1. Both hold if and only if 0 <= theta <= 1, with theta = 1/2 splitting kinetic-energy loss equally between domain and internal energy.

Conclusion: Any entropy-stable split-form discontinuous Galerkin spectral element method remains semi-discretely entropy stable once the source is added at the collocation nodes, for both stationary and prescribed moving bodies.

Abstract: Immersed-boundary methods avoid body-fitted meshes, but the energy or entropy stability of the underlying flow discretisation does not automatically extend to the immersed treatment. This manuscript analyses the volume-penalised compressible Navier--Stokes equations for both energy and entropy stability, on the continuous and semi-discrete levels, and integrates the isolated source exactly in time. The source is formulated as a one-parameter family indexed by $θ$, whose momentum component relaxes the fluid velocity towards the prescribed solid velocity; $θ$ sets how much of the resulting kinetic-energy loss leaves the domain rather than being converted to internal energy. The two stability requirements impose opposing bounds on $θ$, with dissipation of the relative total energy requiring $θ\geq0$ and stability of the mathematical entropy requiring $θ\leq1$. Where penalisation acts, both conditions are therefore satisfied if and only if $0\leqθ\leq1$, and the symmetric value $θ=1/2$ divides the kinetic-energy loss equally between the domain and the internal energy. We conclude that any entropy-stable split-form discontinuous Galerkin spectral element method remains semi-discretely entropy stable once the source is added at the collocation nodes, for stationary and prescribed moving bodies alike.

</details>


### [47] [Analysis of a zeroth-order multiscale sampler: strong convergence, convergence of ergodic averages and weak convergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17037)
*Antonin Della Noce,Urbain Vaes*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Generalizes and rigorously analyzes a multiscale derivative-free (zeroth-order) Bayesian sampler, providing error estimates in both continuous- and discrete-time settings across three metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many Bayesian inference applications, computing the gradient of the negative log-posterior is difficult or undesirable, motivating the study of derivative-free (zeroth-order) samplers.

Method: Generalizes and rigorously analyzes a multiscale derivative-free sampler from Pavliotis, Stuart and Vaes (2022). In discrete time, it resembles zeroth-order perturbed SGD (Jin et al., 2018) and zeroth-order Langevin Monte Carlo (Roy et al., 2022). Error estimates are derived with respect to all problem parameters.

Result: Error estimates are obtained in both continuous-time and discrete-time settings, in three different metrics: strong convergence, convergence of ergodic averages, and uniform-in-time weak convergence.

Conclusion: The multiscale derivative-free sampler is rigorously analyzed, with comprehensive error estimates across parameters, time settings, and convergence metrics.

Abstract: In many practical applications of Bayesian inference, it is difficult or undesirable to compute the gradient of the negative log-posterior, motivating the study of derivative-free samplers, also called zeroth-order samplers. The aim of this paper is to generalize and rigorously analyze a multiscale derivative-free sampler recently proposed in the literature by Pavliotis, Stuart and Vaes (2022). In the discrete-time setting, the multiscale sampler we study bears a strong resemblance to other recently proposed methods, such as the so-called zeroth-order perturbed stochastic gradient descent algorithm (Jin et al., 2018) and the zeroth-order Langevin Monte Carlo algorithm by Roy et al. (2022). We present error estimates with respect to all the parameters of the problem, both in the continuous-time and discrete-time settings. The error estimates are in three different metrics: strong convergence, convergence of ergodic averages, and uniform-in-time weak convergence.

</details>


### [48] [An accurate parallel finite difference method for the variable-order fractional Laplacian in heterogeneous media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17063)
*Shiping Zhou,Yanzhi Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes accurate finite difference discretizations for the variable-order fractional Laplacian and an FFT-accelerated scheme with near-linear cost O(MN log(2N)) and O(MN) memory, plus the first error analysis quantifying how solution regularity and spatially varying order jointly determine convergence rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anomalous diffusion in heterogeneous media is ubiquitous, and variable-order nonlocal operators naturally model heterogeneity and regime transitions. However, their position-dependent long-range interactions cause prohibitive memory costs and make constant-order numerical methods impractical, leaving few accurate and efficient schemes available.

Method: They develop finite difference discretizations for the variable-order fractional Laplacian (-Δ)^{α(x)/2} and an FFT-accelerated scheme for efficient implementation. Costs are O(MN log(2N)) computational and O(MN) memory, with N spatial points and M << N; the method is naturally parallelizable. A rigorous error analysis quantifies the local interplay between solution regularity and the spatially varying order α(x).

Result: The scheme achieves the stated computational and memory costs and is parallelizable. Error analysis establishes consistency and accuracy, being the first to quantify how solution regularity and varying order jointly determine convergence rates, verified by numerical experiments. Experiments confirm accuracy and efficiency, and applications to anomalous diffusion in composite materials capture material heterogeneity effects.

Conclusion: The proposed FFT-accelerated finite difference method provides an accurate, efficient, and parallelizable approach for variable-order nonlocal operators, with novel convergence theory and demonstrated utility for anomalous diffusion in heterogeneous composite materials.

Abstract: Anomalous diffusion in heterogeneous media is ubiquitous across physical and biological systems, where spatially varying microstructures give rise to distinct transport regimes. Variable-order nonlocal operators provide a natural modeling framework for describing heterogeneity and transitions between diffusion regimes. However, the position-dependent long-range interactions inherent in these operators impose prohibitive memory costs and make numerical methods developed for constant-order operators computationally impractical. Consequently, accurate and computationally efficient numerical methods for variable-order nonlocal operators remain scarce. In this paper, we propose accurate finite difference discretizations for the variable-order fractional Laplacian $(-Δ)^{α(\mathbf{x})/2}$ and develop a fast Fourier transform (FFT)-accelerated scheme for their efficient implementation. The computational and memory costs are $O\!\big(MN\log(2N)\big)$ and $O(MN)$, respectively, where $N$ is the total number of spatial points and $M \ll N$. The method is also naturally parallelizable. We establish its consistency and accuracy through a rigorous error analysis that quantifies the local interplay between solution regularity and the spatially varying order $α(\mathbf{x})$. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to quantify how these two factors jointly determine convergence rates, with numerical experiments demonstrating this dependence. Extensive numerical experiments confirm the accuracy and efficiency of the proposed method. Applications to anomalous diffusion in composite materials further demonstrate its ability to capture the effects of material heterogeneity.

</details>


### [49] [Analytical and numerical periodic homogenization of Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman and Isaacs equations in a $W^{2,p}$ Cordes framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17142)
*Timo Sprekeler,Hung V. Tran*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies periodic homogenization of fully-nonlinear second-order HJB and Isaacs equations with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions, where the diffusion matrix satisfies the Cordes condition, and develops finite element methods to approximate the effective Hamiltonian via the corrector.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Homogenization of fully-nonlinear second-order HJB/Isaacs equations with Dirichlet boundary conditions is challenging, especially when the diffusion matrix only satisfies the Cordes condition rather than stronger structural assumptions, and numerical approximation of the effective Hamiltonian is difficult due to irregular cell problems.

Method: Assumes a sufficiently large Cordes parameter to establish existence and uniqueness of W^{2,p} solutions to the true cell problem, proves homogenization under minimal regularity and obtains a quantitative result. Proposes and rigorously analyzes W^{2,p}-conforming and mixed finite element methods for the corrector, which are then used to approximate the effective Hamiltonian. Central tool: Campanato's concept of near operators.

Result: The paper shows existence and uniqueness of W^{2,p} solutions to the true cell problem, proves homogenization under minimal regularity, establishes a quantitative homogenization result, and rigorously analyzes W^{2,p}-conforming and mixed FEMs for the corrector, applied to approximate the effective Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: The Cordes condition with a sufficiently large parameter enables a rigorous homogenization and numerical framework for fully-nonlinear HJB and Isaacs equations with Dirichlet boundary conditions, with Campanato near-operator theory providing the key analytical foundation for both theory and FEM analysis.

Abstract: We study periodic homogenization of fully-nonlinear second-order Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman and Isaacs equations subject to a homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition and the numerical approximation of the effective Hamiltonian, where the diffusion matrix is assumed to satisfy the Cordes condition. In the first part, assuming the Cordes parameter is sufficiently large, we show existence and uniqueness of $W^{2,p}$ solutions to the true cell problem, prove homogenization under minimal regularity assumptions, and establish a quantitative homogenization result. In the second part, we propose and rigorously analyze $W^{2,p}$-conforming and mixed finite element methods for the approximation of the corrector, which are then used in the approximation of the effective Hamiltonian. The key tool used throughout this work is Campanato's concept of near operators.

</details>


### [50] [Quantum integration in Besov spaces with mixed smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17204)
*Bernd Käßemodel,Tino Ullrich*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum algorithms for high-dimensional integration of periodic functions in mixed-smoothness Besov spaces, achieving a one-order improvement over deterministic rates and proving near-optimality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional integration of multivariate periodic functions is a fundamental problem, and the deterministic convergence rates for mixed-smoothness Besov spaces are well known. The authors aim to exploit the quantum model of computation to improve upon these classical rates.

Method: They construct new quantum algorithms based on the multivariate Faber–Schauder decomposition of periodic functions from Besov spaces S^r_{p,q}B(T^d) with mixed smoothness, and analyze their convergence rates. They also derive lower bounds to establish optimality.

Result: The proposed quantum algorithms achieve a gain of one convergence order compared to the known deterministic rates of convergence. Lower bounds confirm these results are optimal up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: Quantum computation yields a provable one-order speedup for high-dimensional integration of mixed-smoothness periodic Besov functions, and the derived rates are near-optimal (up to logarithms).

Abstract: We study the complexity of high-dimensional integration of periodic functions from Besov spaces $S^r_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^d)$ with mixed smoothness in the quantum model of computation. We construct new quantum algorithms based on the multivariate Faber-Schauder decomposition of such functions and analyze their convergence rates. We show a gain of one convergence order compared to the well-known deterministic rates of convergence. We additionally obtain lower bounds showing the optimality of our results up to logarithms.

</details>


### [51] [Optical Fourier Transform-Based Preconditioning for Large-Scale Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17275)
*Chelsea Drum,Lucas Onisk,Ariana N. Brown,James G. Nagy*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This work explores using imperfect optical Fourier transforms (OFTs) as preconditioners for iterative Krylov solvers in linear inverse problems, showing via simulation that sufficiently high-quality optical systems can match digital FFT-based preconditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FFT-based preconditioners efficiently approximate inverse operators in large-scale linear inverse problems, and optical Fourier transforms could offer higher throughput and energy efficiency than digital FFTs. However, practical optical systems are imperfect and introduce errors, so OFTs only approximate digital transforms—and their impact on iterative solvers is largely unexplored.

Method: The authors digitally simulate the optical Fourier transform using the FFT and incorporate sources of error expected in practical optical systems. They then use these simulated OFT-based preconditioners within iterative Krylov subspace methods to solve discrete inverse problems.

Result: Numerical experiments show that given sufficient optical system quality, iterative methods using OFT-based preconditioners maintain reconstruction quality and convergence comparable to fully digital FFT-based preconditioners.

Conclusion: OFT-based preconditioning of Krylov subspace methods is feasible for discrete inverse problems, provided the optical system meets sufficient quality standards.

Abstract: Fast Fourier Transform (FFT)-based preconditioners provide an efficient approach for approximating inverse operators in large-scale linear inverse problems when the underlying operator structure can be exploited in the Fourier domain. Optical Fourier transforms (OFTs), the analog of digital Fourier transforms, exploit light propagation through optical systems and offer the potential for greater throughput and energy efficiency than conventional digital FFT implementations. However, practical optical systems are inherently imperfect and introduce a variety of errors into the computed output. Thus, OFTs can only provide approximations to their digital counterpart, and the implications of these inexact transforms for iterative solvers remains largely unexplored. This work investigates the feasibility of OFT-based preconditioning of iterative Krylov subspace methods for the solution of discrete inverse problems. We digitally simulate the optical Fourier transform using the FFT and incorporate sources of error expected in practical optical systems into the simulation. Numerical experiments suggest that given sufficient optical system quality, iterative methods utilizing OFT-based preconditioners can maintain reconstruction quality and convergence comparable to those with fully digital FFT-based preconditioners.

</details>


### [52] [Efficient Shallow Ritz Method: 1D Semi-Linear Diffusion-Reaction and Allen-Cahn Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17553)
*Zhiqiang Cai,César Herrera*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Extends an efficient shallow Ritz method to nonlinear diffusion-reaction equations with a cubic reaction term, developing a reduced block Newton solver and a time-stepping neural network method for the Allen-Cahn equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior shallow Ritz methods were only developed for one-dimensional linear diffusion and diffusion-reaction problems. This work aims to extend them to nonlinear settings, particularly the Allen-Cahn equation, which features nonlinearity and sharp interior/boundary layers.

Method: A shallow Ritz discretization with an established error estimate; a reduced block Newton (rBN) method with O(n) cost for solving the non-convex optimization; a convex splitting scheme for time discretization; a time-stepping neural network method combining these.

Result: Established error estimate of the shallow Ritz discretization, unconditional energy stability of the time discretization, and an L2-norm error estimate. Experiments show accurate solutions for singularly perturbed nonlinear problems, effective resolution of interior and boundary layers without oscillations, and significant outperformance over finite element methods on fixed uniform meshes.

Conclusion: The extended shallow Ritz method with the rBN solver provides an efficient (O(n)) and accurate approach for nonlinear diffusion-reaction and Allen-Cahn equations, outperforming finite element methods on uniform meshes, especially for layer-resolving problems.

Abstract: An efficient shallow Ritz method was developed in [8, 9] for one-dimensional linear diffusion and diffusion-reaction problems. In this paper, the method is extended to a nonlinear diffusion-reaction equation with a cubic reaction term: (1) a basic error estimate of the shallow Ritz discretization is established and (2) a reduced block Newton (rBN) method for solving the resulting non-convex optimization problem is developed. Again, the computational cost of the rBN method is $\mathcal{O}(n)$ for one-dimensional problems, where $n$ is the number of neurons. Combining a convex splitting scheme for time discretization with the efficient shallow Ritz method, we develop a time-stepping neural network method for solving the Allen-Cahn equation. The unconditional energy stability of the time discretization and an error estimate in the $L^2$ norm are established.
  Numerical experiments demonstrate that the neural network approach yields accurate solutions for singularly perturbed nonlinear diffusion-reaction equations and the Allen-Cahn equation. The method effectively resolves interior and boundary layers without oscillations and significantly outperforms the finite element method on fixed uniform meshes.

</details>


### [53] [Targeted Entropy-Stable Nonlinear Artificial Dissipation for High-Order Spectral-Element Summation-By-Parts Discretizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17567)
*Zelalem Arega Worku,Alex Bercik,David C. Del Rey Fernández,David W. Zingg*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces a sensor-activated nonlinear artificial-dissipation framework for summation-by-parts discretizations that improves robustness for under-resolved and shocked flows while preserving high-order accuracy for smooth solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-order methods need artificial dissipation to stay robust in under-resolved flows, but poorly controlled dissipation harms accuracy and damps physical structures. Existing spectral-element operators often lower formal order or only target top polynomial modes.

Method: A residual-correction-inspired, sensor-activated nonlinear dissipation framework for continuous and discontinuous SBP discretizations. It is modular, dimensionally consistent, element-local, and discretization-agnostic, allowing explicit control over magnitude, spatial distribution, activation, and convergence rate; it also recovers existing methods as special cases.

Result: The scheme preserves discrete conservation and semidiscrete entropy stability when the base method does. For smooth solutions with suitable mesh assumptions, parameter choices retain the base formal accuracy. Burgers and Euler tests with SBP show improved robustness for under-resolved and shocked flows while keeping high-order accuracy, using the same parameters across problems.

Conclusion: Targeted, sensor-activated dissipation can improve robustness of high-order methods without excessively damping resolved flow structures.

Abstract: Artificial dissipation improves the robustness of high-order methods for under-resolved flows, but can compromise accuracy and suppress physically relevant structures if its strength and spatial distribution are not appropriately controlled. We present a sensor-activated nonlinear artificial-dissipation framework, inspired by the residual correction method, for continuous and discontinuous summation-by-parts discretizations. In contrast to many existing spectral-element dissipation operators that either reduce the formal order of accuracy or target only the highest polynomial modes, the proposed framework permits design- and higher-order dissipation with explicit control over its magnitude, spatial distribution, activation, and convergence rate under mesh refinement. This modular construction also recovers several existing methods through appropriate choices of its components. The dissipation preserves discrete conservation and semidiscrete entropy stability whenever the base method satisfies these properties. It is dimensionally consistent, element local, and discretization agnostic, as the added term is constructed independently of the base spatial residual. For smooth solutions under suitable mesh assumptions, we establish parameter choices that retain the formal accuracy of the base discretization. Numerical experiments with summation-by-parts discretizations of the Burgers and Euler equations demonstrate substantially improved robustness for under-resolved flows and flows with shock waves while retaining high-order accuracy for smooth solutions, using the same parameter choices across several test problems. Overall, these results show that targeted dissipation can improve the robustness of high-order methods without excessive damping of resolved flow structures.

</details>


### [54] [A Structure-Preserving Effective Linear Approximation of the Quantum Boltzmann Collision Operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17568)
*Shuang Hu,Bo Lin,Zhenning Cai*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A structure-preserving effective linear approximation of the quantum Boltzmann collision operator for Bose/Fermi gases is constructed, enabling fast Fourier spectral evaluation and used as an approximate Jacobian in a Newton solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating quantum Boltzmann collisions for Bose and Fermi gases is computationally expensive, and existing simple models like quantum BGK lose accuracy; there is a need for an approximation that is both efficient and physically consistent.

Method: Construct a structure-preserving effective linear approximation of the quantum Boltzmann operator for variable hard-sphere kernels, evaluated via Fourier spectral methods at O(N_v^4 log N_v). Use the operator at local quantum equilibrium as an approximate collision Jacobian in a modified Newton solver that keeps the full nonlinear residual.

Result: The continuous operator conserves mass, momentum, and energy and satisfies a linearized H-theorem. Numerical tests show smaller errors than the quantum BGK model in homogeneous relaxation and fewer full collision evaluations for steady Couette and Fourier flows compared to a Newton solver using a quantum BGK Jacobian.

Conclusion: The proposed approximation offers an efficient, structure-preserving alternative for quantum Boltzmann simulations with improved accuracy and computational savings over quantum BGK-based approaches.

Abstract: We construct a structure-preserving effective linear approximation of the quantum Boltzmann collision operator for Bose and Fermi gases in the normal phase. For variable hard-sphere kernels, the resulting Fourier spectral evaluation costs $O(N_v^4\log N_v)$, with $N_v$ velocity nodes per direction and no angular quadrature factor. The continuous operator conserves mass, momentum and energy and satisfies a linearized $H$-theorem. We also use the operator at local quantum equilibrium as an approximate collision Jacobian in a modified Newton solver that retains the full nonlinear residual. Numerical tests show smaller errors than the quantum BGK model in homogeneous relaxation and fewer full collision evaluations for steady Couette and Fourier flows than the same Newton solver using a quantum BGK Jacobian.

</details>


### [55] [Strong convergence rate of the implicit midpoint method for stochastic KdV equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17600)
*Junjie Li,Xinyan Niu,Chun Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Proves first-order strong convergence of the implicit midpoint method for the periodic stochastic KdV equation with additive noise, resolving an open problem since Debussche–Printems, valid for all damping λ≥0.
