TL;DR: Interior Hessian estimates for $k$-convex solutions of Hessian quotient equations are proven using a unified separation-propagation method plus a compactness argument.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hessian quotient equations are central in fully nonlinear elliptic PDE, yet interior Hessian estimates for their $k$-convex solutions remain challenging, especially for the specific parameter range considered.

Method: Two-step approach: (1) apply the unified quantitative separation-propagation method of Li--Wu to derive a doubling inequality; (2) use the compactness argument of Shankar--Yuan to obtain the estimates. The method generalizes to a class of general Hessian equations.

Result: Interior Hessian estimates are established for $k$-convex solutions of $\frac{\sigma_k(D^2u)}{\sigma_\ell(D^2u)}=1$ with $2\leq k\leq n$, $0\leq \ell<k$, and $k-\ell\in\{1,2\}$.

Conclusion: The combined technique yields the desired estimates and extends to more general Hessian equations.

Abstract: We study interior Hessian estimates for $k$-convex solutions of Hessian quotient equations $\frac{σ_k(D^2u)}{σ_\ell(D^2u)}=1$ with $2\leq k\leq n$, $0 \leq \ell < k$, and $k-\ell \in\{1,2\}$. We first adopt the unified quantitative separation-propagation method of Li--Wu [LW26b] to derive a doubling inequality, and then use the compactness argument of Shankar--Yuan [SY25] to obtain the estimates. This approach also applies to a class of general Hessian equations.

</details>


### [178] [Triple Junctions in Multi-Phase Obstacle type Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18270)
*Daniela De Silva,Ovidiu Savin,Henrik Shahgholian*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper studies the regularity of the free boundary for a multi-phase obstacle-type problem tied to quadrature domains, focusing on triple junctions where at least three distinct components meet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadrature domains and multi-phase obstacle-type problems are important in free boundary theory, but the geometry at points where three or more components meet (triple junctions) is not well understood.

Method: The problem is posed as minimization of the energy J(U,F) = ∫_{B_1} (1/2|∇U|^2 + F·U)dx over vector-valued competitors U=(u^1,…,u^N) with non-negative, disjointly supported components and constant forcing terms f^i>0. The analysis examines the free boundary geometry at triple junctions.

Result: The paper analyzes the geometry of the free boundary at points where at least three distinct components meet.

Conclusion: The study contributes to understanding the regularity and structure of free boundaries in multi-phase obstacle-type problems with triple junctions.

Abstract: We study the regularity of the free boundary for a multi-phase obstacle-type problem associated to quadrature domains, which arises from the minimization of the energy $$J(U,F):= \int_{B_1} (\frac 1 2 |\nabla U|^2 + F \cdot U)dx$$ among all competitors $U=(u^1,\ldots, u^N)$ with non-negative components $u^i \geq 0$ with disjoint supports, and constant forcing terms $f^i>0.$ We focus on the geometry of the free boundary at points where at least three distinct components meet (triple junctions).

</details>


### [179] [Global well-posedness for a damped two dimensional fluid--beam system](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18310)
*Weihua Wang,Lu Yang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves global well-posedness and an exact energy dissipation identity for an irrotational incompressible fluid of infinite depth under an elastic Euler--Bernoulli beam with structural damping, for a broad class of finite-energy initial data not previously admissible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior well-posedness results for this beam--fluid system restricted admissible data to potentials having L^2 representatives, excluding physically relevant finite-energy initial potentials. The authors aim to remove this L^2 restriction and rigorously justify the energy identity at the solution's regularity.

Method: They fix the low-frequency part of the initial potential and evolve an L^2 remainder to bypass low-frequency non-integrability. Combining homogeneous Dirichlet--Neumann estimates with a high-frequency Fourier symmetrizer, they obtain local well-posedness with H^{-1} forcing, then use Continuous Fréchet differentiability of the energy in affine coordinates and a chain rule on H^1⊂L^2⊂H^{-1} to justify the dissipation identity.

Result: For every damping coefficient γ>0, they establish global well-posedness for arbitrary initial data in H^2(R)×(Ḣ^{1/2}(R)∩Ḣ^1(R)), including data where neither ψ_0 nor u_0=ψ_0+G(η_0)ψ_0 has an L^2 representative. They also prove the exact energy dissipation identity at the constructed solution regularity, with energy identity and coercivity giving uniform bounds for global continuation.

Conclusion: The energy identity and coercivity yield uniform bounds enabling global continuation via the local theory, while iterating local uniqueness and stability estimates gives global uniqueness within the stated affine solution class and Lipschitz dependence on bounded sets over finite time intervals.

Abstract: We study the normalized surface system of an irrotational incompressible fluid of infinite depth beneath an elastic Euler--Bernoulli beam with structural damping. For every fixed damping coefficient $γ>0$, we establish global well-posedness for arbitrary initial data in $H^2(\R)\times\bigl(\dot H^{1/2}(\R)\cap\dot H^1(\R)\bigr)$. The main novelty is that the admissible data include finite energy initial potentials for which neither $ψ_0$ nor $u_0=ψ_0+G(η_0)ψ_0$ has an $L^2$ representative. We also prove the exact energy dissipation identity at the regularity of the constructed solutions. The main analytical difficulties are the possible failure of $L^2$ integrability at low frequencies and the justification of energy differentiation at the solution regularity. To handle the low-frequency difficulty, we fix the low-frequency part of the initial potential and evolving an $L^2$ remainder. Homogeneous Dirichlet--Neumann estimates and a high-frequency Fourier symmetrizer yield local well-posedness with $H^{-1}$ forcing. Continuous Fréchet differentiability of the energy in these affine coordinates, together with a chain rule on $H^1\subset L^2\subset H^{-1}$, justifies the dissipation identity. The Energy identity and coercivity provide uniform bounds that yields global continuation tharough the local theory. Iterating the local uniqueness and stability estimates gives global uniqueness within the stated affine solution class and Lipschitz dependence on bounded sets over every finite time interval.

</details>


### [180] [Attainment of the sharp stability constant of Beckner conformal log-Sobolev inequality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18327)
*Lu Chen,Bohan Wang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper solves the open problem of the existence of a minimizer for the stability of the Log-Sobolev inequality, establishing sharp attained stability constants and a limiting connection to the fractional Sobolev inequality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Minimizers for the stability of the fractional Sobolev inequality and the Beckner conformal Log-Sobolev inequality (its endpoint case) were established by König and Chen-Lu-Tang, but the existence of a minimizer for the stability of the Log-Sobolev inequality remained unsolved.

Method: The authors work on the sphere $\mathbb{S}^n$ and analyze the sharp $L^2$-stability constant of the Beckner conformal log-Sobolev inequality, studying its attainment and relating it to the fractional Sobolev inequality via the limit $s \to 0^+$. They also use Hersch normalization to study sequences of $L^{p_{s_k}}$-normalized minimizers.

Result: For every $n \geq 2$, the sharp $L^2$-stability constant of the Beckner conformal log-Sobolev inequality on $\mathbb{S}^n$ is attained and is strictly less than $\frac{2\log 2}{n}$. The normalized stability constant of the fractional Sobolev inequality converges to that of the log-Sobolev inequality as $s \to 0^+$. After Hersch normalization, any sequence of $L^{p_{s_k}}$-normalized minimizers (with $s_k \to 0^+$) has a subsequence converging strongly in $L^2(\mathbb{S}^n)$ to an $L^2$-normalized minimizer of stability of the log-Sobolev inequality.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the previously open existence problem for minimizers of the Log-Sobolev inequality stability, and establishes a strong convergence link between fractional Sobolev stability minimizers and log-Sobolev stability minimizers in the endpoint limit $s \to 0^+$.

Abstract: The existence of minimizer for stability of the fractional Sobolev inequality and the stability of Beckner conformal Log-Sobolev inequality (the endpoint case of fractional Sobolev inequality) have been established by König and Chen-Lu-Tang. The existence of minimizer for stability of the Log-Sobolev inequality is unsolved. In this paper, we solve this problem. For every $n\geq2$, we prove that the sharp $L^2$-stability constant of Beckner conformal log-Sobolev inequality on $\mathbb S^n$ is attained and is strictly less than $\frac{2\log2}{n}$. Moreover, the normalized stability constant of the fractional Sobolev inequality converges to the stability constant of the log-Sobolev inequality as $s\to0^+$. After Hersch normalization, every sequence of $L^{p_{s_k}}$-normalized minimizers for stability of the fractional Sobolev inequality, with $s_k\to0^+$, has a subsequence converging strongly in $L^2(\mathbb S^n)$ to an $L^2$-normalized minimizer of stability of the log-Sobolev inequality.

</details>


### [181] [Frequency-dependent capacitance matrix formulation for Fabry--Pérot resonances. Part II: One-dimensional periodic media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18341)
*Habib Ammari,Bowen Li,Ping Liu,Yingjie Shao*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Bloch resonances in 1D high-contrast periodic resonator systems beyond the subwavelength regime, using transfer-matrix and capacitance-matrix techniques to characterize limiting wavenumbers, eigenmode splittings, bandgaps, crossings, and Zak phase quantization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding Bloch resonances of high-contrast resonators beyond the subwavelength regime is important because standard subwavelength approximations fail there; the work aims to give an exact and systematic spectral characterization of such systems.

Method: An exact transfer-matrix formulation identifies limiting wavenumbers; a periodic frequency-dependent capacitance matrix determines leading square-root splittings in the contrast parameter; a direct cyclic matching expansion proves the approximation (including multiple eigenvalues and small complex contrasts); spectral exclusion and monodromy-matrix arguments yield bandgap and crossing results; a local classification is derived, plus a weighted Zak phase for isolated bands.

Result: Limiting wavenumbers equal the union of interior Neumann and Dirichlet frequencies of separating intervals. Positive capacitance eigenvalues give all leading square-root splittings. A spectral exclusion result yields a direct bandgap statement. Uniform eigenmode approximations are obtained at common resonances, distinguishing the zero-frequency mode from nonzero double Bloch eigenvalues. Exact linear crossings correspond to monodromy proportional to identity. Complete local classification is obtained for two resonators per cell and, by band folding, for fully symmetric arrays; the weighted Zak phase quantizes under reflection symmetry.

Conclusion: The paper provides an exact, comprehensive spectral theory of 1D high-contrast Bloch resonances beyond subwavelength, linking transfer matrices, capacitance spectra, eigenmode splittings, bandgaps, crossings, mode classification, and Zak phase quantization, supported by explicit formulas and numerical illustrations.

Abstract: We study Bloch resonances of one-dimensional periodic systems of high-contrast resonators beyond the subwavelength regime. An exact transfer-matrix formulation identifies the limiting wavenumbers with the union of the interior Neumann frequencies and the Dirichlet frequencies of the separating intervals. At each fixed reference wavenumber, we introduce a periodic frequency-dependent capacitance matrix whose positive eigenvalues determine all leading square-root splittings in the contrast parameter. A direct cyclic matching expansion proves the approximation, including multiple eigenvalues and small complex contrasts. A spectral exclusion result outside the limiting set yields a direct bandgap statement. At a common resonance of all intervals, we also derive uniform eigenmode approximations and distinguish the zero-frequency mode from nonzero double Bloch eigenvalues. For positive contrast, exact linear crossings are characterized by the monodromy matrix being proportional to the identity. A complete local classification is obtained for two resonators per cell and, through band folding, for fully symmetric arrays with any number of resonators per cell. We further formulate the weighted Zak phase for isolated bands and its quantization under reflection symmetry. Explicit two-resonator formulas and numerical illustrations complement the analysis.

</details>


### [182] [Blowup of smooth solutions to 2D quasilinear wave equations with higher-order null conditions and short pulse initial data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18358)
*Bingbing Ding,Zhouping Xin,Huicheng Yin*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Studies 2D quasilinear wave equations with short pulse data; proves that for epsilon_0 >= epsilon_k*, smooth solutions blow up in finite time, establishing the critical exponent for global existence vs. blowup under the k-th order null condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continues prior work that proved global existence for epsilon_0 < epsilon_k*; now addresses the complementary blowup regime to identify the sharp threshold.

Method: Derives sharp orders of delta in both energy and pointwise estimates, using higher-order null structure, a new good unknown, and the transport equation for the difference between this good unknown and its corrector.

Result: For epsilon_0 >= epsilon_k*, the smooth solution blows up in finite time, complementing the global existence result for epsilon_0 < epsilon_k*.

Conclusion: The threshold epsilon_0 = epsilon_k* is the critical exponent characterizing global existence versus finite-time blowup for smooth short pulse solutions under the k-th order null condition.

Abstract: This paper investigates the 2D quasilinear wave equation $\displaystyle\sum_{α,β=0}^{2}g^{αβ}(\partialφ)\partial_{αβ}^{2}φ=0$ with the short pulse initial data $(φ, \partial_tφ)|_{t=1}=(δ^{2-\varepsilon_0}φ_0(\frac{r-1}δ,ω), δ^{1-\varepsilon_0}φ_1(\frac{r-1}δ,ω))$, where $0<\varepsilon_0<1$ is a fixed constant and $δ>0$ is a small parameter. In our previous work [Ding Bingbing, Xin Zhouping, Yin Huicheng, Sci. China Math., Vol. 69 (2026), No. 3, 667-736], we have established the global existence of a smooth solution $φ$ under the $k$-th order null condition ($k\ge 2$) when the $(k+1)$-th order null condition fails, provided that $0<\varepsilon_0<\varepsilon_k^*=\frac{k}{k+1}$. As a natural continuation, the present paper demonstrates that for $\varepsilon_0\ge \varepsilon_k^*$, the smooth solution $φ$ blows up in finite time. Consequently, the threshold $\varepsilon_0=\varepsilon_k^*$ is shown to be the critical exponent that precisely characterizes the global existence and finite-time blowup for smooth short pulse solutions to the aforementioned 2D quasilinear wave equation, under the assumption that the $k$-th order null condition holds while the $(k+1)$-th order null condition does not. A key novelty of our approach lies in deriving sharp orders of $δ$ for almost all quantities in both energy and pointwise estimates. This is achieved by utilizing the higher-order null structure in conjunction with a new good unknown recently introduced by us and the transport equation for the difference between this good unknown and its corrector.

</details>


### [183] [Uniform Decay for Ruggeri's Relaxed Navier-Stokes-Fourier Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18367)
*Heinrich Freistühler,Yizhou Zhou*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the linearization of Ruggeri's symmetric hyperbolic model for viscous, heat-conductive fluids around a homogeneous equilibrium in 3D, proving $L^2$ decay at rate $(1+t)^{-3/4}$ for $L^1\cap L^2$ perturbations, uniform as a relaxation-time parameter vanishes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ruggeri's symmetric hyperbolic model is a relaxation-type approximation of the Navier–Stokes–Fourier system. Understanding whether its linearized dynamics decay at the same rate as the formal Navier–Stokes–Fourier limit, uniformly in the relaxation parameter, is a natural and important consistency question.

Method: Linearize Ruggeri's classical symmetric hyperbolic system of viscous, heat-conductive fluids about a homogeneous equilibrium in three space dimensions. Analyze the linear semigroup acting on $L^1\cap L^2$ perturbations, tracking the equilibrium variables together with suitably weighted stresses and heat flux, and control the decay uniformly as a common parameter scaling the three relaxation times tends to zero.

Result: Perturbations in $L^1\cap L^2$ are shown to decay in $L^2$ at the rate $(1+t)^{-3/4}$, uniformly in the vanishing relaxation-time parameter. The estimate controls the equilibrium variables and the appropriately weighted stresses and heat flux, matching the decay rate of the formal Navier–Stokes–Fourier limit.

Conclusion: The linearized Ruggeri model exhibits the same optimal $L^2$ decay rate as its Navier–Stokes–Fourier limit, uniformly in the relaxation parameter, confirming the consistency of the hyperbolic relaxation with the parabolic limit at the linear level.

Abstract: This work studies the linearization of Ruggeri's classical symmetric hyperbolic model of viscous, heat-conductive fluids about a homogeneous equilibrium. In three space dimensions, we show that perturbations in $L^1\cap L^2$ decay in $L^2$ at the rate $(1+t)^{-3/4}$, uniformly as a common parameter scaling the three relaxation times tends to zero. The estimate controls the equilibrium variables together with the appropriately weighted stresses and heat flux, and has the same decay rate as the formal Navier--Stokes--Fourier limit.

</details>


### [184] [Dynamic viscoelastic membranes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18466)
*Martin Kružík,Riccardo Voso*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper derives a dynamic membrane theory for nonlinear viscoelastic (Kelvin-Voigt) solids from 3D bulk theory via vanishing-thickness limit, handling inertia, second-gradient elasticity, and frame-indifferent dissipation, proving existence and convergence of solutions through a two-time-scale minimizing movements scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A rigorous dimensional reduction from 3D nonlinear viscoelastic bulk models to membrane theories is lacking, particularly one that handles inertia, nonsimple elastic energies, frame-indifferent dissipation, and prevents self-interpenetration in the limit.

Method: A hyperbolic minimizing movements scheme with two time-scales recasting evolution into incremental, sequential, and time-delayed problems. Uses Gamma-convergence of incremental functionals; since the Korn constant is non-uniform in thickness, estimates rely on the time derivative of the Cauchy-Green tensor rather than the velocity gradient.

Result: Existence of solutions to the scheme for fixed thickness and their convergence to a weak solution of the continuous-time problem as time-scales vanish. Identification of membrane limit at each hierarchy level (Gamma-convergence and convergence of sequential/time-delayed problems), and convergence of membrane time-delayed solutions to a measure-valued solution of the membrane continuous-time problem as the outer time-scale vanishes.

Conclusion: A dynamic membrane theory for nonlinear viscoelastic Kelvin-Voigt solids is rigorously established as the vanishing-thickness limit of the 3D bulk theory, with orientation preservation and self-interpenetration prevention, yielding measure-valued solutions in the limit.

Abstract: We rigorously derive a dynamic membrane theory for nonlinear viscoelastic solids with Kelvin-Voigt rheology from the corresponding three-dimensional bulk theory, in the limit of vanishing thickness. The bulk model accounts for inertia, a nonsimple elastic energy depending on the second deformation gradient, and a frame-indifferent dissipation. Orientation preservation is ensured by the energy, while self-interpenetration of the limiting membrane is prevented by a uniform averaged invertibility condition. Our analysis relies on a hyperbolic minimizing movements scheme with two time-scales, which recasts the evolution into a hierarchy of incremental, sequential, and time-delayed problems. For fixed thickness, we prove the existence of solutions to the scheme and their convergence to a weak solution of the continuous-time problem as the time-scales vanish. We then identify the membrane limit at each level of the hierarchy, proving the $Γ$-convergence of the incremental functionals and the convergence of solutions to the sequential and time-delayed problems. Since the constant in the Korn inequality is not uniform in the thickness, these limits rely on estimates for the time derivative of the Cauchy-Green tensor rather than for the velocity gradient. Finally, as the outer time-scale vanishes, the membrane time-delayed solutions converge to a measure-valued solution of the membrane continuous-time problem.

</details>


### [185] [Sign-indefinite transmission conditions and near-zero eigenvalues and resonances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18483)
*Andrea Mantile,Andrea Posilicano*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies the linear operator L_{c,d} for metamaterial modeling with sign-indefinite parameters. It shows the spectrum is absolutely continuous on [0,+∞) but has non-empty point spectrum, and analyzes the splitting of a zero threshold eigenvalue under small analytic perturbations of the parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metamaterials with sign-indefinite material parameters (d negative on some components) exhibit spectral properties differing from the sign-definite case, motivating a detailed spectral analysis of such operators.

Method: Spectral analysis of the operator L_{c,d} with transmission boundary conditions at interfaces; for connected Ω, perturb parameters analytically and relate the threshold eigenvalue behavior to the Neumann-Poincaré operator eigenvalues of the interface.

Result: The spectrum contains an absolutely continuous part [0,+∞) plus a non-empty point spectrum; conditions are found for zero to be a threshold eigenvalue. Depending on ½(1+d(ε))/(1−d(ε)) relative to the nearest Neumann-Poincaré eigenvalue, the zero eigenvalue splits into two analytic branches in ε^{1/2}: either two resonance branches or one negative eigenvalue branch plus one resonance branch.

Conclusion: Metamaterial operators with sign-indefinite parameters possess rich spectral structure, including threshold eigenvalues whose splitting behavior is governed by the Neumann-Poincaré operator spectrum of the interface.

Abstract: We consider the linear operator for metamaterial modeling $$L_{c,d }=(χ_{\RE^{3}\backslash\overline Ω}+c)\nabla\!\cdot (χ_{\RE^{3}\backslash\overline Ω}+d)\nabla\,,$$ where the material parameters $c>0$ and $d$ are constant on each connected component of the bounded domain $Ω$, $d$ is negative on at least one component of $Ω$ and transmission boundary conditions hold at the interfaces. It turns out that $L_{c,d }$ has, as in the sign-definite case, an absolutely continuous spectrum equal to $[0,+\infty)$; however, contrary to the latter, it has a non-empty point spectrum; in particular, we find conditions on the parameters so that zero is a threshold eigenvalue. In the case $Ω$ is connected, we study how such a threshold eigenvalue behaves under $\varepsilon$-small analytic perturbations of the parameters. We show that, depending on the position of the number $\frac12\,\frac{1+d(\ve)}{1-d(\ve)}$ relative to the nearest eigenvalue of the Neumann-Poincaré operator of the interface, the zero eigenvalue splits into two branches (analytic in $\varepsilon^{1/2}$), consisting either of two branches of resonances, or of one branch of negative eigenvalues and one of resonances.

</details>


### [186] [Viscosity Solutions for Hamilton-Jacobi equations on Separable Banach Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18496)
*Fabian Fuchs*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper builds a viscosity-solution framework for optimal control on separable Banach spaces, proving a strict comparison principle and identifying the value function as the unique viscosity solution of an infinite-dimensional HJB equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal control problems on infinite-dimensional (Banach) spaces lack a fully developed viscosity theory, since existing notions were mainly designed for finite-dimensional or metric-space settings.

Method: The authors extend the notion of $B$-viscosity solutions to the Banach setting and connect their extension to viscosity notions used in metric spaces.

Result: They prove a strict comparison principle and characterize the value function of an optimal control problem as the unique viscosity solution to an infinite-dimensional HJB equation.

Conclusion: The framework establishes well-posedness of the infinite-dimensional HJB equation via viscosity solutions, providing a rigorous foundation for optimal control on separable Banach spaces.

Abstract: This work provides a framework to treat optimal control problems on separable Banach spaces in the viscosity sense. First, it extends the notion of $B$-viscosity solutions to the Banach setting and relates the extension to the notions of viscosity solutions used in metric space settings. As the main results, the paper proves a strict comparison principle and characterizes the value function of an optimal control problem as the unique viscosity solution to an infinite-dimensional HJB equation.

</details>


### [187] [Near-boundary rigidity and strict maximum modulus for Neumann eigenfunctions on convex planar domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18546)
*Haiyun Deng,Xuyong Jiang,Xiaoping Yang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Every nonzero second Neumann eigenfunction on a bounded convex planar domain attains its maximum modulus only on the boundary, with no smoothness or simplicity assumptions; a local distance condition at an interior maximum forces global rigidity, making the domain a rectangle and the eigenfunction a cosine.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses localization of the maximum modulus of second Neumann eigenfunctions on convex planar domains, seeking to remove boundary smoothness and eigenvalue simplicity assumptions and to understand how local geometric conditions enforce global structure.

Method: A rigidity theorem is established: for any positive Neumann eigenvalue μ, if an interior maximum-modulus point p satisfies sqrt(μ)·dist(p,∂Ω) < π/√3, then the domain must be a rectangle and the eigenfunction a cosine of one coordinate. This is used to prove the boundary-only maximum property.

Result: The maximum modulus of every nonzero second Neumann eigenfunction on a bounded convex planar domain is attained only on the boundary. Moreover, μ ≥ 4μ₂(Ω), and the factor 4 is sharp.

Conclusion: The work reveals how a local distance condition at a single interior maximum-modulus point can enforce global geometric rigidity, linking extremum localization to classification of domains and eigenfunctions.

Abstract: We prove that the maximum modulus of every nonzero second Neumann eigenfunction on a bounded convex planar domain $Ω$ is attained only on the boundary, without boundary smoothness or eigenvalue simplicity. The key is a rigidity theorem for any positive Neumann eigenvalue $μ$: an interior maximum-modulus point $p$ with $\sqrtμ\,\operatorname{dist}(p,\partialΩ)<π/\sqrt{3}$ forces the domain to be a rectangle and the eigenfunction to be a cosine of one coordinate. In particular, $μ\ge4μ_2(Ω)$, and the factor $4$ is sharp. This result reveals how a local distance condition at a single interior maximum-modulus point can enforce global geometric rigidity, thereby linking extremum localization to the classification of domains and eigenfunctions.

</details>


### [188] [Generic boundary behavior of Dirichlet eigenfunctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18619)
*Alec Payne,Zihui Zhao*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Generic $C^k$ domains have Dirichlet eigenfunction nodal sets meeting the boundary orthogonally along a $C^{k-1}$ submanifold, extending Uhlenbeck's genericity results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uhlenbeck's classical results concern generic properties of Laplacian eigenfunctions, but the behavior of nodal sets at the boundary (orthogonal intersection) was not fully established. The paper aims to extend these genericity results to the boundary behavior of Dirichlet eigenfunctions.

Method: Analyzes boundary asymptotics of Dirichlet eigenfunctions via homogeneous harmonic polynomials and compares their nodal sets. Proves genericity of simple Dirichlet eigenfunctions with zero as a regular value in the interior and zero as a regular value of the boundary normal derivative. For planar domains, a separate argument gives one-parameter families of perturbed domains.

Result: A residual set of $C^k$ domains in $\mathbb{R}^n$ ($k > n$) exists such that the nodal set of any Dirichlet eigenfunction intersects the boundary orthogonally along a $C^{k-1}$ embedded submanifold whenever the intersection is non-empty. Also, a complete proof that generic Euclidean domains have simple Dirichlet eigenfunctions with the stated regularity properties.

Conclusion: Uhlenbeck's genericity framework is successfully extended to boundary nodal-set behavior, with orthogonal intersection established generically; planar case admits explicit one-parameter families of perturbed domains.

Abstract: We extend Uhlenbeck's classical results on the generic properties of Laplacian eigenfunctions. In particular, we find a residual set of $C^k$ domains in $\mathbb{R}^n$, $k > n$, such that the nodal set of any Dirichlet eigenfunction intersects the boundary orthogonally along a $C^{k-1}$ embedded submanifold, whenever the intersection is non-empty. This is achieved by analyzing the boundary asymptotics of Dirichlet eigenfunctions via homogeneous harmonic polynomials and comparing their respective nodal sets. Along the way, we give a complete proof that generic Euclidean domains have simple Dirichlet eigenfunctions with zero as a regular value in the interior and zero as a regular value of the boundary normal derivative. In the special case of planar domains, a different argument provides one-parameter families of perturbed domains with the desired properties.

</details>


### [189] [Generalized minimizers for the capillarity energy outside convex obstacles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18673)
*Gabriele Nani,Simone Vincini*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Capillarity energy outside an unbounded convex obstacle may lack volume-constrained minimizers due to escaping sequences; the authors prove the infimum is attained by a "generalized minimizer" (finite union of regions) via Γ-convergence, with applications to the isoperimetric profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volume-constrained minimizers of capillarity energy outside an unbounded obstacle can fail to exist, since infimizing sequences may escape to infinity. The paper seeks to describe the asymptotic behavior and restore attainment in this setting.

Method: The authors study minimization outside a closed convex obstacle C. They establish a Γ-convergence result for capillarity energies outside sequences of closed convex obstacles converging in the Hausdorff sense, then use it to construct generalized minimizers.

Result: The infimum is attained by a generalized minimizer: a finite union of regions, each minimizing capillarity energy outside a limit of translations of C in its volume class, with total volume satisfying the prescribed constraint. Each generalized minimizer is approximable by an infimizing sequence.

Conclusion: The method describes the asymptotic behavior of minimizing sequences. As an application, the isoperimetric profile associated with the capillarity energy is studied, showing how the obstacle's asymptotic geometry affects the profile, the generalized minimizers, and their connectedness.

Abstract: In general, the capillarity energy outside an unbounded obstacle may fail to admit volume-constrained minimizers, as infimizing sequences may escape to infinity. We study this minimization problem outside a closed convex obstacle $C$. Via a $Γ$-convergence result for the capillarity energies outside sequences of closed convex obstacles converging in the Hausdorff sense, we prove that the infimum of the functional is attained by a generalized minimizer, namely, a finite union of regions, each of which minimizes the capillarity energy outside a limit of translations of $C$ in its volume class, with the total volume satisfying the prescribed constraint. Each generalized minimizer can be suitably approximated by an infimizing sequence, thus providing a description of its asymptotic behaviour. As an application, we study the isoperimetric profile associated with the capillarity energy and investigate how the asymptotic geometry of the obstacle affects both this profile and generalized minimizers and their connectedness.

</details>


### [190] [Optimal transport to non-convex polyhedral domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18756)
*Shibing Chen,Qi-Rui Li,Yuanyuan Li,Jiakun Liu,Xiaotian Wu*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper analyzes optimal transport maps to a non-convex polyhedral target in R^n, showing the singular set's closure is locally a smooth hypersurface away from a small (dimension ≤ n-2) set, and proves the same regularity for the source free boundary in partial transport between domains separated by a hyperplane.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the regularity and structure of singular sets in optimal transport with non-convex targets is challenging; the paper aims to control the singular set and free boundary behavior in this setting.

Method: Geometric measure theory and optimal transport techniques are applied to study optimal transport with a non-convex polyhedral target, analyzing the closure of the singular set and, for partial transport between hyperplane-separated domains, the source free boundary.

Result: The closure of the singular set is locally a smooth hypersurface outside a relatively closed set of Hausdorff dimension at most n-2, and the source free boundary exhibits the same regularity for partial transport between domains separated by a hyperplane.

Conclusion: Optimal transport to non-convex polyhedral targets has controlled singular structure, with smooth hypersurface regularity except on a small-dimensional set, extending to free boundary regularity in partial transport.

Abstract: We study optimal transport with a non-convex polyhedral target in $\mathbb{R}^n$. The closure of the singular set is locally a smooth hypersurface outside a relatively closed set of Hausdorff dimension at most $n-2$. For partial transport between domains separated by a hyperplane, the source free boundary has the same regularity.

</details>


### [191] [Sharp Global Well-Posedness for the Logarithmically Regularized $2$D Euler Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18775)
*Zachary Radke*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper proves global well-posedness for 2D Euler equations with a logarithmically regularized velocity, provided γ > 1/2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The 2D Euler equations with logarithmically regularized velocity relate to the borderline between well-posedness and ill-posedness (the Yudovich/Beale-Kato-Majda threshold). Understanding this regime clarifies how much regularization is needed for global solutions.

Method: The velocity is given by u = ∇⊥(−Δ)^(−1) ln^(−γ)(|∇|+e) ω, with initial vorticity in the homogeneous space Ḣ¹ ∩ Ḣ⁻¹(ℝ²). The proof treats this logarithmic modification of the Biot–Savart law.

Result: Global well-posedness holds whenever γ > 1/2.

Conclusion: A logarithmic correction stronger than half-power in the velocity regularization guarantees global well-posedness in the critical initial-vorticity class.

Abstract: We consider solutions to the two-dimensional Euler equations with a logarithmically regularized velocity given by $u=\nabla^{\perp}(-Δ)^{-1}\ln^{-γ}(|\nabla|+e)ω$ and initial vorticity in $\dot{H}^1 \cap \dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^2)$. We show that if $γ>1/2$, then global well-posedness holds.

</details>


### [192] [Global existence of smooth solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations for initial data with infinite energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18797)
*Alexey Cheskidov,Hedong Hou*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves global existence of smooth (in space and time at positive times) solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations for any divergence-free distributional initial data, and characterizes which distributional data admit weak solutions with sharp Besov/Triebel-Lizorkin ranges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical well-posedness for Navier-Stokes requires initial data in function spaces above certain regularity thresholds; the question of how far below these thresholds solutions can still be constructed for distributional initial data motivates this work.

Method: Construct solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations from arbitrary divergence-free distributional initial data, showing they become smooth for positive times, and analyzing the borderline cases via Sobolev W^{-1,p}, Besov, and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

Result: Global existence of solutions for any divergence-free distributional data, smoothness in time and space for positive times, weak solutions in the classical sense when data lie in W^{-1,p} (1<p<∞), and a characterization of distributional data admitting weak solutions with sharp ranges in Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

Conclusion: The work extends solution theory for Navier-Stokes to distributional initial data, identifying exactly which data yield weak solutions and providing sharp regularity-space thresholds.

Abstract: We prove the global existence of solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations for any divergence-free distributional initial data. The solutions are smooth in both time and space at positive times. When the initial data belong to the Sobolev spaces $W^{-1,p}$ for $1<p<\infty$, our constructed solutions are weak in the classical sense. We also characterize the distributional initial data admitting weak solutions and obtain the sharp ranges in Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

</details>


<div id='physics.comp-ph'></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [193] [SIREN: A nonlinear Rankine-source boundary element solver for the stationary motion of a marine vehicle in calm water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14027)
*Daniele Peri*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: SIREN is a Rankine-source panel method (in Dawson's tradition) for steady ship wave resistance, using an iterated linearisation about a base flow that treats the free surface as a rigid lid and moves panels to follow the relaxed wave elevation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical double-body base flow is only meaningful for a flat free surface and a hull cut at z=0, and requires desingularised free-surface panels. SIREN seeks a base flow valid on the actual boundary that avoids these limitations.

Method: The free-surface condition is linearised about a base flow found from a Neumann problem on the actual boundary: sources on all hull panels (wet and dry) and free-surface panels with impermeability imposed on both, so the free surface acts as a rigid lid. The linearisation is iterated non-linearly: free-surface panels are moved rigidly in the vertical to the relaxed wave elevation at their centroid, hull panels are marked wet/dry against the moved surface, and the base flow is recomputed. Impermeability applies to the whole hull; the wet/dry marking only selects panels for the force integral. Under-relaxation is confined to passes where the non-linear correction accumulates without reset. The radiation condition is imposed by a one-row upstream shift of collocation points; wave resistance is obtained both by pressure integration and by transverse wave-cut analysis.

Result: Wave resistance is computed by two independent means—pressure integration and transverse wave-cut analysis—providing a cross-check of the results.

Conclusion: SIREN replaces the classical double-body flow with a rigid-lid Neumann-problem base flow on the actual boundary, eliminating the need for desingularised free-surface panels while allowing a nonlinear iterated solution with a physically relaxed free surface.

Abstract: SIREN (Ship Inviscid Resistance and Elevation eNgine) is a Rankine-source panel solver for the steady wave resistance of a ship, in the tradition of Dawson's method. The free-surface condition is linearised about a base flow, and the linearisation is iterated. In the present formulation the base flow is the solution of a Neumann problem on the actual boundary: sources on the entire hull, wet and dry, and on the free surface panels, with impermeability imposed on both, so that the free surface acts as a rigid lid at the position it currently occupies. This replaces the classical double-body flow, whose image about z=0 is meaningful only for a flat surface and a hull cut at z=0, and it removes the need for desingularised free-surface panels. In the non-linear iteration the free-surface panels are moved rigidly in the vertical to the relaxed wave elevation at their centroid, the hull panels are marked wet or dry against the moved surface, and the base flow is recomputed on the moved geometry; impermeability applies to the whole hull regardless of this marking, which is used only to select which panels enter the force integral, and under-relaxation is confined to the passes where the non-linear correction accumulates without being reset. The radiation condition is imposed by a one-row upstream shift of the collocation points, and the wave resistance is obtained by pressure integration and, independently, by transverse wave-cut analysis.

</details>


### [194] [A Gauge-Inspired Coupled Fokker--Planck System for Multistate Stochastic Dynamics: Formalism and 1-D Numerical Implementation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14052)
*Francesco Pio De Cosmo,Gianluca Di Natale,Leandro Cieri*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The first numerical solver for an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker-Planck system in (1+1) dimensions, using operator splitting with a non-oscillatory advection scheme to ensure probability conservation, non-negativity, and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending the Fokker-Planck equation to coupled systems via non-Abelian symmetry groups provides a powerful framework for coupled multi-state stochastic systems, but translating this abstract gauge field formulation into viable numerical predictions remained an unaddressed challenge, motivated by prior work on ice crystal habit evolution.

Method: An operator-splitting scheme combined with a non-oscillatory advection solver, designed to strictly ensure probability conservation, non-negativity, and numerical stability, validated across linear and quadratic coupling regimes up to strong interactions (g <= 1.0).

Result: Non-Abelian gauge coupling acts as a cross-reactive feedback loop that fundamentally alters system dynamics: driving continuous probability exchange between states, suppressing localised density peaks, and forcing long-time spatial delocalisation.

Conclusion: This work establishes foundational computational infrastructure for integrating complex multi-state physics, providing a key step toward improving ice cloud representation in atmospheric and climate models, while the framework and engine remain general and multidisciplinary, offering a versatile paradigm for any domain requiring coupled multi-state stochastic equations.

Abstract: Extending the Fokker--Planck equation to a coupled system of equations through non-Abelian symmetry groups offers a powerful theoretical framework for coupled multi-state stochastic systems. However, translating this abstract gauge field formulation into viable numerical predictions remains an unaddressed challenge. Motivated by our previous work on ice crystal habit evolution, in this work we bridge this gap by presenting the first numerical solver for an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker--Planck system in (1+1) dimensions. Using an operator-splitting scheme combined with a non-oscillatory advection solver, our algorithm strictly ensures probability conservation, non-negativity, and numerical stability. We demonstrate that non-Abelian gauge coupling acts as a cross-reactive feedback loop that fundamentally alters system dynamics, driving continuous probability exchange between states, suppressing localised density peaks, and forcing long-time spatial delocalisation. Validated across linear and quadratic coupling regimes up to strong interactions (g <= 1.0), this work establishes the foundational computational infrastructure needed to integrate complex multi-state physics. Overall, while our approach provides a key step toward improving ice cloud representation in atmospheric and climate models, the underlying physical framework and computational engine remain fundamentally general and multidisciplinary, offering a versatile paradigm for any domain requiring coupled multi-state stochastic equations.

</details>


### [195] [acousticHelmholtzFoam: an MPI-parallel unstructured finite-volume solver for frequency-domain acoustics in heterogeneous two-phase media with perfectly matched layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14057)
*Chuanchao Xu,Jun Liu,Dieter Bothe,Tomislav Maric*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: acousticHelmholtzFoam is an OpenFOAM application for time-harmonic acoustic pressure fields in heterogeneous gas-liquid media on unstructured polyhedral meshes, solved via PETSc/MUMPS with PML boundary truncation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a documented, verified computational tool for acoustic pressure fields and acoustic-force evaluation in two-phase gas-liquid systems on general meshes.

Method: Coupled real/imaginary pressure equations discretized with cell-centred finite-volume operators, material coefficients from volume-of-fluid phase fraction, Cartesian PML truncation (setPMLFields), assembled into a distributed PETSc matrix solved with MUMPS.

Result: Layered gas-liquid-PML case with analytical solution: relative complex-pressure error 1.197e-3 on 8000 cells; serial/MPI (up to 8 ranks) agreement within 1e-12 in L2 norm; levitator height sweep and scaling measurements demonstrated.

Conclusion: The paper documents the implementation, interface, algorithm, build procedure and test infrastructure of a verified acoustic solver for two-phase media.

Abstract: acousticHelmholtzFoam is an OpenFOAM application that computes time-harmonic acoustic pressure fields in heterogeneous gas-liquid media on unstructured polyhedral meshes. The complex pressure is stored as real and imaginary volume fields, the material coefficients are constructed from a volume-of-fluid phase fraction with geometric face-fraction averaging, and open boundaries are truncated by Cartesian perfectly matched layers (PMLs) whose damping tensor is generated by the companion utility setPMLFields. The two coupled real-valued equations are discretized with the cell-centred finite-volume operators of OpenFOAM and assembled explicitly, including processor couplings and boundary contributions, into a distributed PETSc matrix that is solved by default with the MUMPS sparse direct solver. The solver writes pressure amplitude, complex velocity, acoustic radiation pressure and time-averaged momentum flux for acoustic-force evaluation. This paper documents the implemented equations, the case interface, the assembly and solution algorithm, the build procedure and the test infrastructure. A layered gas-liquid-PML case with an analytical solution serves as an end-to-end regression: on 8000 cells the whole-domain relative complex-pressure error is $1.197\times10^{-3}$, and serial and MPI solutions on up to eight ranks agree to within $10^{-12}$ in relative $L_2$ norm. A single-axis acoustic-levitator height sweep with constant-volume spheroidal drops demonstrates the two-phase material input and the force output, and strong- and problem-size-scaling measurements characterize the direct-solver workflow. The numerical formulation and its verification are presented in a companion paper (arXiv:2610.01593).

</details>


### [196] [FDTD Model of Noise Pollution from an AI Data Center](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14899)
*Gavin Buxton*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: An FDTD acoustic model assesses how topography shapes low-frequency noise pollution from an AI data center, identifying elevation features near a river as high-noise hotspots and distant areas as largely protected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of AI data centers raises environmental concerns, particularly low-frequency noise pollution that affects nearby residents not only as an annoyance but with significant health impacts. Understanding how local topography influences noise levels can guide effective mitigation.

Method: A finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) model of sound propagation over the topography near an AI data center is used to simulate and map noise levels across the terrain.

Result: Topography strongly controls noise pollution: areas of sudden elevation on either side of the river near the data center are more susceptible to high noise levels, while areas further away are largely protected by the topography.

Conclusion: Characterizing how topography governs noise pollution in specific terrains helps identify where noise mitigation strategies would be most effectively deployed.

Abstract: The sudden growth in AI data centers is raising numerous environmental concerns. Low-frequency noise pollution is a concern for local residents, that may not just be an annoyance but also carry significant health impacts. An FDTD model of sound propagation over the topography near an AI data center is presented. The topography plays a large role in the level of noise pollution, with areas of sudden elevation on either side of the river near the AI data center more susceptible to high noise levels, while areas further afield largely protected from noise pollution by the topography. Understanding the role that topography plays in noise pollution, and capturing the noise pollution in specific topographies, can help identify areas where noise mitigation strategies might be best employed.

</details>


### [197] [A quantum algorithm for meshless lattice Boltzmann method with unitary interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14943)
*Chi Zhang,Hefeng Wang,Hua Xiang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a mass-weighted meshless formulation of the shifted incompressible lattice Boltzmann method with an exactly unitary streaming step, combined with Carleman embedding for quantum implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In traditional meshless LBM, the semi-Lagrangian streaming step on scattered nodes is an interpolation that is non-unitary, making block encoding costly (requiring normalization proportional to sparsity and logarithmically many ancillas). The authors aim to overcome this non-unitarity to enable efficient quantum simulation.

Method: The interpolation is expressed as the exponential exp(a Dx + b Dy) of mimetic derivative operators. Each node is given a Voronoi mass, and a mass-weighted similarity transform makes these derivative operators skew-Hermitian via moment constraints and low-wave-number trigonometric differentiation conditions, rendering the interpolation exactly unitary. The local collision step stays unchanged up to a rescaling of its quadratic term, with the same weights defining the amplitude encoding of distribution functions.

Result: For quasi-uniform node sets, the mass-weighting diagonal stays within a unit-order normalization that degrades only mildly as the Carleman truncation order grows, mitigating block-encoding cost. Numerical experiments on the Taylor-Green vortex confirm the method reproduces full meshless LBM, with velocity error decreasing as the Carleman order increases.

Conclusion: The mass-weighted unitarization of meshless LBM interpolation yields an exactly unitary streaming step and a practical, efficient formulation that matches classical meshless LBM accuracy while enabling quantum block encoding with reduced cost.

Abstract: We present a mass-weighted meshless formulation for the shifted incompressible lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) whose streaming step is exactly unitary, combining with the Carleman embedding. In traditional meshless LBM, on a scattered node set the semi-Lagrangian streaming step is an interpolation, which is not unitary; a generic block encoding of this matrix therefore requires a normalization proportional to its sparsity and logarithmically many ancillas. To overcome the non-unitarity, we instead express the interpolation as the exponential exp(a Dx + b Dy) of mimetic derivative operators. Endowing each node with its Voronoi mass and applying a mass-weighted similarity transform renders these derivative operators skew-Hermitian by enforcing moment constraints together with low-wave number trigonometric differentiation conditions, so that the interpolation becomes exactly unitary. Such unitarization of interpolation leave the local collision step unchanged up to a rescaling of its quadratic term, where the same weights define the amplitude encoding of the distribution functions. For quasi-uniform node sets, the mass-weighting diagonal remains within a unit-order normalization that degrades only mildly as the Carleman truncation order grows, thereby mitigating the cost of block encoding. Numerical experiments on the Taylor Green vortex confirm that the method reproduces the full meshless LBM, with the velocity error decreasing as the Carleman order increases.

</details>


### [198] [Self-Adaptive Model-Order Reduction for Shock-Dominated Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15944)
*Ali Mohaghegh,Cheng Huang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This work proposes a self-adaptive model-order reduction (MOR) framework that dynamically switches between adaptive and static reduced-order models on the fly, based on evolving solution characteristics, to balance computational efficiency and accuracy for shock-dominated flows like rotating detonation engines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional static ROMs (offline/online trained) have limited predictive capability for convection-dominated problems with strongly evolving physics (e.g., shocks). Adaptive ROMs fix this by updating the low-dimensional subspace online but incur extra computational overhead, and frequent basis/sampling-point updates erode their speed advantage over static ROMs. There is a need to reconcile this accuracy-efficiency tradeoff.

Method: A self-adaptive MOR framework that dynamically constructs and deploys the most appropriate ROM formulation (adaptive or static) on the fly according to the evolving characteristics of the solution. It combines the advantages of adaptive and static ROM approaches while reducing reliance on extensive offline training.

Result: The framework achieves optimal computational efficiency while maintaining high modeling accuracy of local physics during online model execution. It is developed and evaluated for modeling rotating detonation engines (RDEs), a challenging test case involving strongly convection-dominated, transient, and multiscale reacting-flow dynamics.

Conclusion: The proposed self-adaptive MOR framework reconciles the efficiency-accuracy tradeoff by adaptively selecting between adaptive and static ROM formulations, enabling accurate and efficient prediction of shock-dominated flows.

Abstract: Projection-based model-order reduction (MOR) offers a promising approach for accelerating expensive full-order model (FOM) by constructing reduced-order models (ROMs) that retain the majority of FOM accuracy while significantly reducing the computational cost. However, conventional static ROMs, often following offline/online training strategy, can exhibit limited predictive capability for convection-dominated problems featuring strongly evolving physics such as shocks. Adaptive ROMs emerge as a promising alternative and address such limitation by dynamically updating the low-dimensional subspace during online simulation to track locally evolving system dynamics, thereby enabling accurate predictions even for shock-dominated flows. However, such online adaptation introduces additional computational overhead, and frequent basis and sampling-point updates can substantially reduce their computational advantage relative to their static counterpart. To reconcile this tradeoff, the current work introduces a self-adaptive MOR framework that dynamically constructs and deploys the most appropriate ROM formulation (either adaptive or static) on the fly according to the evolving characteristics of the solution. to achieve optimal computational efficiency while maintaining high modeling accuracy of the local physics during the online model execution. In this regard, the proposed framework seeks to exploit the advantages of both adaptive- and static-ROM approaches to achieve an optimal computational efficiency and predictive accuracy while reducing reliance on extensive offline training. The framework is developed and evaluated for modeling rotating detonation engines (RDEs), which provide a challenging test environment involving strongly convection-dominated, transient, and multiscale reacting-flow dynamics.

</details>


### [199] [Principles and Techniques for Agentic Research in Experimental Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17193)
*Niko R. Reed,Declan M. Daly,Andrew J. Beling,Jordan D. Giandrea,Ronald L. Walsworth,John W. Blanchard,Daniel Ang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes ten requirements for collaborative AI agents in experimental physics and a framework (custom LLM scaffolding plus scientific workflows) to enforce them, validated through six case studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI use in physics is growing, but few guidelines exist for its effective use, especially in collaborative, human-directed experimental settings.

Method: A principles-based, human-centered approach: agents amplify rather than replace the researcher. The authors define ten requirements across scientific norms, effective agent use, and domain expertise, then build a framework improving off-the-shelf LLM behavior via custom scaffolding, general-purpose scientific workflows and skills, and closed-loop iterative campaigns.

Result: Six experimental physics case studies (device design, hardware control, neural network optimization, physical model identification, data analysis) produced useful products: a superior neural network architecture and quantum defect photodynamic model; a fabrication-ready waveguide design; a quantum control pulse sequence improving simulated performance by over an order of magnitude; NMR spectrometer commissioning to ~1 Hz precision on live hardware; and the first reconstruction of an Ising microstate of an artificial spin-ice array from diffraction-limited imaging.

Conclusion: Collaborative, closed-loop AI agents can meaningfully amplify physics research, delivering work products that outperform prior published results.

Abstract: The use of artificial intelligence (AI) in physics research is increasingly common, yet few guidelines exist for its effective use. Starting with a principles-based approach, we outline a set of ten requirements for collaborative AI agents in experimental physics, spanning scientific norms, effective agent use, and domain expertise. We take a human-centered approach in which agents act as collaborators that amplify rather than replace the expertise of a human researcher (user) who directs the work and is responsible for its results. We then present a framework designed to enforce these requirements, detailing methods for improving off-the-shelf behavior of large language models (LLMs) with custom scaffolding and describing general-purpose scientific workflows and skills. In particular, we highlight the power of closed-loop campaigns where agents work iteratively toward a well-defined goal. We demonstrate the potential of this mode of research through six case studies in experimental physics, covering device design, hardware control, neural network optimization, physical model identification, and data analysis. All six case studies result in useful work products that add utility to our research, including a neural network architecture and solid-state quantum defect photodynamic model that substantially outperform our published work; a microwave waveguide design that extends a published design concept to a fabrication-ready device meeting our experimental constraints; a quantum control pulse sequence candidate that improves simulated performance by more than an order of magnitude; commissioning of an NMR spectrometer to $\sim$1~Hz frequency precision on live hardware; and finally a data-fitting pipeline that achieves, to our knowledge, the first reconstruction of the Ising microstate of an artificial spin-ice array from diffraction-limited magnetic imaging.

</details>


### [200] [Wisevolve Potential Engine: Machine-Learning Molecular Dynamics at 100 Million Atom-Steps per Second on a Single GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17366)
*Hekai Bu,Zheyong Fan*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: WPE accelerates GPUMD by selecting and reusing model- and stage-specific GPU execution policies, achieving large speedups across NEP, qNEP, classical, and PIMD workloads while retaining simulation control in GPUMD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose MD software must support diverse potentials, but within a simulation stage the loaded model and required calculations stay fixed as atoms evolve. This stability can be exploited for specialization, yet GPUMD lacks such tailored GPU execution.

Method: The Wisevolve Potential Engine (WPE) uses information from GPUMD to select and reuse a GPU execution policy tailored to the model and simulation stage, while GPUMD retains simulation control and data ownership.

Result: On an RTX 5090, GPUMD-WPE reaches median 109M atom-step/s across 106 NEP workloads and 142M atom-step/s for a 15.1M-atom diamond Si system. Geomean speedups vs GPUMD: 3.49x (NEP), 3.61x (qNEP), 1.24x (classical), 10.5x (PIMD). Validated on water structure, thermal conductivity, PIMD thermal expansion, and large-scale Cu shock compression.

Conclusion: Model- and stage-specific specialization is a practical way to accelerate GPU potential evaluation in general-purpose MD workflows.

Abstract: General-purpose molecular dynamics software supports diverse interatomic potentials and simulation requirements. Within a simulation stage, the loaded potential model and the required calculations typically remain fixed as the atomic configuration evolves. We present the Wisevolve Potential Engine (WPE), which uses this information from GPUMD to select and reuse a GPU execution policy tailored to the model and simulation stage. GPUMD retains simulation control and ownership of the simulation data. On a single NVIDIA RTX 5090, WPE-enabled GPUMD (GPUMD-WPE) achieves a median throughput of 109 million atom-step/s across 106 neuroevolution potential (NEP) workloads. For a 15.1-million-atom diamond Si system, it sustains 142 million atom-step/s. Relative to GPUMD, geometric-mean speedups are 3.49x across these NEP workloads, 3.61x across 16 workloads using NEP with dynamic charges (qNEP), 1.24x across 28 classical-potential workloads, and 10.5x across 56 path-integral molecular dynamics (PIMD) configurations. End-to-end calculations of water structure, lattice thermal conductivity, and PIMD thermal expansion show close agreement with corresponding GPUMD results and published simulation results. A separate 12.5-million-atom Cu shock-compression simulation demonstrates its applicability to large-scale nonequilibrium dynamics. These results support model- and stage-specific specialization as a practical way to accelerate GPU potential evaluation in general-purpose molecular dynamics workflows.

</details>


### [201] [KINTORA: a semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin drift-kinetic, unstructured mesh tokamak solver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17428)
*Mark F. Adams*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A continuum drift-kinetic solver using semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin on unstructured triangular poloidal planes with flux-coordinate-independent field-line mapping; verified against references and demonstrated on ITG and zonal flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuum gyrokinetic codes use structured field-aligned grids (limited geometry flexibility), while PIC codes support unstructured meshes and open field lines but suffer statistical noise. A method is needed that combines geometric flexibility without the noise floor.

Method: Semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin discretization on unstructured triangular poloidal planes extruded toroidally. Parallel advance is a map along the exact field line in a flux-coordinate-independent manner, so the mesh is never field-aligned. Uses adiabatic electrons and a long-wavelength polarization field solve. Fully device-resident; MPI and CPU/GPU builds.

Result: Every operator verified against independently computed references: Landau damping, geodesic-acoustic root, and a linear ITG eigenvalue problem, with measured rates within 4% except geodesic-acoustic damping within 12%. A linear toroidal ITG demonstration on canonical-Maxwellian equilibrium recovers a radially coherent mode with growth rate 0.065 c_s/a, stationary in time. The collisionless zonal-flow residual is not converged, rising from 0.72 to 0.87 of the orbit-quadrature prediction with poloidal refinement. Results reproduced between CPU and GPU and across MPI process counts. Code, data, and artifact description are public.

Conclusion: The solver bridges continuum and PIC approaches, occupying a middle ground with unstructured meshes but no statistical noise, and is verified, portable, and reproducible, though the zonal-flow residual requires further convergence study.

Abstract: Continuum gyrokinetic codes use structured, field-aligned grids on nested flux surfaces; particle-in-cell codes support unstructured meshes and open field lines but carry a statistical noise floor. We present a continuum drift-kinetic solver that occupies neither position: a semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin discretization on unstructured triangular poloidal planes extruded toroidally, whose parallel advance is a map along the exact field line in the flux-coordinate independent manner, so the mesh is never aligned with the field, with adiabatic electrons and a long-wavelength polarization field solve. Every operator is verified against an independently computed reference, from Landau damping to the geodesic-acoustic root and a linear ion-temperature-gradient eigenvalue problem, every measured rate within 4% except the geodesic-acoustic damping, within 12%. A linear toroidal ion-temperature-gradient demonstration on a canonical-Maxwellian equilibrium recovers a radially coherent mode whose growth rate, 0.065 c_s/a, is stationary in time. The collisionless zonal-flow residual is the one measurement still moving: it rises with poloidal refinement from 0.72 to 0.87 of the model's own orbit-quadrature prediction for the measured observable and has not converged. The solver is fully device resident, and every result is reproduced between CPU and GPU builds and across MPI process counts. The code, the data behind every table and an artifact description are in a public repository.

</details>


<div id='physics.plasm-ph'></div>

# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [202] [A New Degree of Freedom in Stellarator Coil Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14396)
*Sophia A. Henneberg,Annika Zettl*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Reversed toroidal field (RTF) coils add a new design freedom in stellarators: redistributing linked current (RTF compensation) lets modular-coil current exceed the net value without changing net linkage, improving coil-plasma separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stellarator coil design is constrained by fixed total plasma-linked current (Ampere's law) and tight coil-plasma separation requirements, which limit compactness. New degrees of freedom are needed to relax these constraints.

Method: Introduce reversed toroidal field (RTF) coils that compensate RTF currents, allowing modular-coil current to exceed the net linked value while preserving net linkage, thereby decoupling coil constraints and reshaping external coil geometry.

Result: The confining magnetic field is largely preserved, while achievable coil-plasma separation increases in the studied configurations.

Conclusion: RTF coils can enable more compact stellarator designs by improving coil-plasma separation without significantly degrading confinement.

Abstract: Reversed toroidal field (RTF) coils introduce a new degree of freedom in stellarator coil design. While the total plasma-linked current is fixed by Ampere's law, compensating RTF currents allow the modular-coil current to exceed this value without altering the net linkage. This redistribution decouples key coil constraints and modifies the external coil geometry without significantly compromising the confining magnetic field. As a result, the achievable coil-plasma separation can be increased in the configurations studied, potentially enabling more compact stellarator designs.

</details>


### [203] [SPIRE: Spin-Resolved Particle-in-Cell with Radiative Corrections for Extreme Laser--Plasma Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14664)
*Xian-Zhang Wu,Huai-Hang Song,Wei-Min Wang,Yan-Fei Li*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: SPIRE is a spin- and photon-polarization-resolved PIC code for self-consistent simulations of laser-plasma interactions in the strong-field QED regime, verified against benchmarks and published results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating laser-plasma interactions in the strong-field QED regime requires self-consistent treatment of spin and photon polarization effects, which existing PIC codes often lack.

Method: SPIRE combines a Yee-grid FDTD Maxwell solver, relativistic Boris pusher, Esirkepov current deposition, moving window, and a vectorial Laguerre-Gaussian injector. Its strong-field QED module uses spin- and polarization-resolved probabilities (nonlinear Compton scattering, Breit-Wheeler pair production), spin evolution with T-BMT precession and anomalous magnetic moment, and Stokes-vector photon polarization evolving through QED and vacuum effects.

Result: Verified via 1D/2D/3D benchmarks; classical PIC cross-checked against SMILEI, and QED modules reproduce published results for polarized electron generation, vacuum birefringence/dichroism, and dense polarized positron production.

Conclusion: SPIRE provides a verified, coupled framework for classical PIC, strong-field QED, spin, and photon-polarization simulations.

Abstract: We present SPIRE, a spin- and photon-polarization-resolved particle-in-cell (PIC) code for self-consistent simulations of laser-plasma interactions in the strong-field quantum electrodynamics (QED) regime. The classical PIC framework combines a Yee-grid finite-difference time-domain Maxwell solver, a relativistic Boris particle pusher, shape-function particle-grid interpolation, Esirkepov charge-conserving current deposition, laser boundary injection, and a relativistic moving window in one, two, and three spatial dimensions. A vectorial Laguerre-Gaussian laser injector based on an angular-spectrum formulation supports tightly focused, nonparaxial optical orbital-angular-momentum modes. The strong-field QED module treats nonlinear Compton scattering and nonlinear Breit-Wheeler pair production as stochastic processes using spin- and polarization-resolved probabilities in the local constant-field approximation. Electron and positron spin evolution includes stochastic transitions, no-emission corrections, and Thomas-Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi precession with a field-dependent anomalous magnetic moment. Photon polarization is represented by the Stokes vector and evolves through nonlinear Compton scattering, pair production, vacuum birefringence, and vacuum dichroism. The implementation is verified with one-, two-, and three-dimensional benchmarks. Classical PIC results are cross-checked against SMILEI, and the spin- and polarization-resolved QED modules reproduce published results for polarized electron generation, vacuum birefringence and dichroism, and dense polarized positron production. These benchmarks verify the coupled classical PIC, strong-field QED, spin, and photon-polarization modules implemented in SPIRE.

</details>


### [204] [First achievement of 100 million degree electron temperatures in a spherical torus: the EXL-50U](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14684)
*Guang Yang,Shaodong Song,Songjian Li,Xiaobing Xie,Zhaokun Wang,Xiang Gu,Jia Li,Xianli Huang,Xinchen Jiang,Yupeng Guan,Huicong Ma,Junquan Lin,Dong Guo,Jiaqi Dong,Yunfeng Liang,Yuejiang Shi,the EXL-50U Team*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: An ST plasma on EXL-50U achieves central electron temperature >8.6 keV (100 million K) using only ECRH, via sequential O-mode then X-mode 50 GHz heating at low density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve high-temperature plasmas in a spherical torus (ST) without relying on neutral beam heating, which is typically used for high-ion-temperature ST scenarios. The aim is to demonstrate electron heating via ECRH alone.

Method: Two 50 GHz ECRH systems are operated sequentially: ordinary-mode (O-mode) ECRH3 first, followed by extraordinary-mode (X-mode) ECRH4. The high-temperature phase is obtained at low density. Temperature is measured by Thomson scattering.

Result: Thomson scattering measures a central electron temperature exceeding 8.6 keV (100 million degrees Kelvin) on the EXL-50U spherical torus device. The discharge is sustained by ECRH only.

Conclusion: A high electron temperature ST plasma can be produced and sustained using solely sequential O-mode then X-mode 50 GHz ECRH at low density, distinct from neutral-beam-heated ST scenarios.

Abstract: Here,we report an ST plasma in which Thomson scattering measures a central electron temperature exceeding 8.6 keV (100 million degrees kelvin) on the EXL-50U spherical torus device.The high-temperature phase is obtained at low density by sequential operation of two 50 GHz ECRH systems: ordinary-mode (O-mode) ECRH3 followed by extraordinary-mode (X-mode) ECRH4. In contrast to neutral-beam-heated high-ion-temperature ST scenarios, the discharge is sustained by ECRH only.

</details>


### [205] [Plasma-neutral collision operators for hydrogen isotopes: kinetic and gyrokinetic descriptions, comparison to fluid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15090)
*Matthieu Protais,Guilhem Dif-Pradalier,Yanick Sarazin,Virginie Grandgirard,Yannick Marandet,Srikanth Sureshkumar,Naren Varadarajan*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives kinetic and gyrokinetic plasma-neutral interaction terms for a hydrogen plasma, showing that standard fluid models misrepresent charge exchange, ionization, and recombination dynamics at the plasma edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasma-neutral interactions strongly influence edge dynamics in fusion devices, but standard fluid models oversimplify them using Maxwellian assumptions and linearized source terms, potentially missing important kinetic effects on instabilities.

Method: The authors derive plasma-neutral interaction terms in kinetic and gyrokinetic frameworks, incorporating velocity-dependent differential cross-sections to capture inelastic collision effects on ions and electrons beyond simple energy and momentum exchange. They verify that fluid moments reduce to standard fluid source terms under Maxwellianity and linearity assumptions, then compare kinetic and fluid models.

Result: Substantial discrepancies appear even for Maxwellian distributions. For charge exchange, the kinetic model gives momentum and energy exchange rates up to twice those of fluid descriptions. A fluid treatment of ionization greatly overestimates the mean kinetic energy of produced electrons.

Conclusion: Fluid descriptions have significant limitations, and kinetic effects are essential for accurately modeling edge-plasma momentum and energy exchange.

Abstract: Plasma-neutral interactions critically affect the edge dynamics of magnetically confined fusion devices. Neutral atoms modify ion and electron velocity distributions, potentially affecting local instabilities Standard fluid models simplify these processes (namely charge exchange, ionization and recombination) using Maxwellian assumptions and linearized source terms. We revisit these assumptions for a hydrogen plasma interacting with hydrogen atoms. We derive plasma-neutral interaction terms in the kinetic and gyrokinetic frameworks. Accounting for velocity-dependent differential cross-sections, the model captures the kinetic impact of inelastic collisions on ions and electrons beyond energy and momentum exchange. We demonstrate that the fluid moments of the kinetic model reduce to standard fluid source terms under the appropriate assumptions of Maxwellianity and linearity. We compare the kinetic model with fluid models through their effects on distribution functions and fluid moments. Substantial discrepancies are observed, even for Maxwellian distributions. For charge exchange, the kinetic model yields momentum and energy exchange rates up to twice those predicted by fluid descriptions. We show that a fluid treatment of ionization greatly overestimates the mean kinetic energy of the produced electrons. These results highlight the limitations of fluid descriptions and the importance of kinetic effects in edge-plasma momentum and energy exchange.

</details>


### [206] [A reduced model for neoclassical transport in non-axisymmetric magnetic configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15561)
*Y. Ban,X. Garbet,R. Varennes,Z. Qu,K. Lim,Y. Sarazin,A. Ibrahim,Y. C. Tay,B. B. Ang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph
