TL;DR: A unified framework computes neoclassical equilibrium in tokamaks with non-axisymmetric magnetic perturbations by combining a Hamiltonian description with a variational minimum-entropy-production principle, yielding fast, tractable transport coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokamaks with non-axisymmetric magnetic perturbations require computing neoclassical equilibrium, but understanding how different trapped particle classes contribute to transport is difficult.

Method: Combine a Hamiltonian description with a variational approach based on a minimum entropy production principle; derive analytic expressions for specific collisionality regimes and devise a fast numerical procedure for the entropy production rate.

Result: Transparent identification of contributions from different classes of trapped particles in magnetic well minima, analytic expressions per collisionality regime, and tractable transport coefficients computable quickly and efficiently.

Conclusion: The extremum of entropy production yields tractable transport coefficient expressions, enabling efficient computation of neoclassical equilibrium for perturbed tokamaks.

Abstract: A unified framework is proposed to compute a neoclassical equilibrium for tokamaks with non-axisymmetric magnetic perturbations, based on a Hamiltonian description combined with a variational approach that relies on a principle of minimum entropy production. This approach allows for a transparent identification of the respective contributions from different classes of trapped particles in magnetic well minima. Analytic expressions are derived for specific collisionality regimes, and a fast and efficient numerical procedure is devised to compute the entropy production rate. Finding the extremum of the entropy production leads to tractable expressions of transport coefficients that can be computed quickly and efficiently.

</details>


### [207] [Implicit PIC Simulations of Hydrogen Plasmas with Intense Negative Ion Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16257)
*Denis Eremin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This work simulates low-pressure, high-density hydrogen plasma discharges using energy-conserving implicit PIC/MCC codes (electromagnetic and electrostatic) to study negative ion production, avoiding artificial scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-density, low-pressure hydrogen discharges are important for negative ion production but exhibit weak collisionality, requiring a kinetic nonlocal description. The standard explicit momentum-conserving PIC/MCC method is highly inefficient since it must resolve both the Debye length and the plasma period.

Method: Simulations of two types of high-density plasma discharges in hydrogen performed with electromagnetic and electrostatic energy-conserving PIC/MCC codes, using actual plasma parameters without artificial scaling.

Result: The implicit energy-conserving PIC/MCC scheme models plasmas of interest with their actual parameters, offering an effective alternative to explicit methods; simulations reveal the negative ion production behavior in the discharges.

Conclusion: The energy-conserving implicit PIC/MCC approach is an attractive alternative for modeling weakly collisional, high-density hydrogen discharges, and is used to characterize negative ion production.

Abstract: High-density plasma discharges in hydrogen operating at low pressures are of considerable interest for negative ion production used in many applications. Their weak collisionality requires a kinetic, nonlocal description, and a popular approach is to model such discharges numerically using the particle-in-cell (PIC) method combined with Monte Carlo collisions (MCC). However, the most commonly used explicit momentumconserving variant of this method is highly inefficient because it must resolve both the Debye length and the plasma period. In this context, the implicit energy-conserving PIC/MCC scheme appears to be an attractive alternative, as it can model the plasmas of interest using their actual parameters without resorting to artificial scaling techniques. This work considers simulations of two types of high-density plasma discharges performed using electromagnetic or electrostatic energy-conserving PIC/MCC codes in hydrogen, and discusses the observed negative ion production.

</details>


### [208] [Relativistic Six-Fluid Rotating-Plasma Force-Balance Equilibria for p-11B Fusion Conditions in Spherical Tori](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16304)
*Yueng_Kay Martin Peng,Akio Ishida*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a six-component axisymmetric spherical torus (ST) magnetofluid equilibrium model for p-11B plasmas with thermal and suprathermal proton, boron, and electron fluids, capturing relativistic and multi-fluid effects absent in single-fluid descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In p-11B regimes, high ion energies, strong current drive, energetic electron populations, and ion-electron temperature separation make single-fluid static-equilibrium models inadequate, so a component-resolved equilibrium framework is needed.

Method: Building on rotating-plasma equilibrium theory, the model adds relativistic thermal enthalpy corrections to electron fluids via h(T/m_ec^2) and modified canonical-angular-momentum flux functions Y_α per component. Equilibrium is governed by a Grad-Shafranov-like Ampere law for poloidal flux Psi, quasineutrality-constrained algebraic density relations, and prescribed profile functions F_α(Y_α), T_α(Y_α), K_α(Y_α), solved with a six-fluid computational method.

Result: Four numerical equilibria (two reactor-relevant ST p-11B sets, two EHL-2 ST cases) span toroidal beta 0.058–0.253, plasma current 2.697–12.59 MA, suprathermal electron temperature 287 keV–2.37 MeV, and h up to 19.1. Ampere's law accuracy, electrostatic potential accuracy, and component-resolved radial force balance show errors from 3% down to below 0.1%.

Conclusion: High-temperature p-11B ST equilibria can differ qualitatively from single-fluid tokamak equilibria, requiring analysis of component-resolved force balance, relativistic electron enthalpy, and explicit reproducibility diagnostics.

Abstract: We formulate an axisymmetric spherical torus (ST) six-component magnetofluid equilibrium model for p_11B plasmas composed of thermal and suprathermal proton, boron, and electron fluids. The model is designed for p_11B parameter regimes where ion energies, strong current drive, energetic electron populations, and ion-electron temperature separation can render a single_fluid static_equilibrium description inadequate. It generalizes rotating_plasma equilibrium theory by incorporating relativistic thermal enthalpy corrections to the electron fluids via h(T/m_ec^2) and by constructing modified flux functions Y_α associated with canonical angular momentum for each component α. The equilibrium is determined by a Grad-Shafranov-like Ampere law for the poloidal magnetic flux Psi, algebraic density relations constrained by quasineutrality, and prescribed profile functions F_α(Y_α), T_α(Y_α) and K_α(Y_α). The manuscript includes the relativistic magnetofluid equations, the axisymmetric equilibrium formulation, the six_fluid computational method, and four numerical equilibria: two reactor_relevant ST p_11B parameter sets and two EHL-2 ST cases. The examples span toroidal beta b_T = 0.058_0.253, plasma currents I_p = 2.697_12.59 MA, and suprathermal electron temperatures T_eh = 287 keV_2.37 MeV, with h up to 19.1. Over these parameter ranges, Ampere's law accuracy, electrostatic potential accuracy, and component_resolved radial force balance can have errors ranging from 3% to below 0.1%. These results indicate that high-temperature p_11B ST equilibria can differ qualitatively from the single_fluid tokamak equilibria and require analysis of equilibrium component_resolved force balance, relativistic electron enthalpy, and explicit reproducibility diagnostics.

</details>


### [209] [QUARZ: a quasi-static particle-in-cell code with non-uniform radial grids for plasma wakefields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16910)
*Alexander Pukhov,Thomas C. Wilson*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: QUARZ: a quasi-static PIC scheme for beam-driven plasma wakefields on arbitrary non-uniform radial grids in cylindrical geometry, enabling large speedups and accurate resolution of near-axis physics and cold-plasma bubble closure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform radial grids waste resolution far from the axis and cannot efficiently resolve tightly focused beams, ion motion onto the axis, or the singular closure of cold-plasma bubbles.

Method: Hat-function finite elements and conservative finite volumes on monotone radial nodes; explicit transverse magnetic field (no iterations, following Baxevanis & Stupakov); optional azimuthal m=1 mode for offsets/tilts/hosing; exact discrete Gauss law; exact uniform-plasma deposition on all nodes including axis; second-order accurate on smoothly stretched grids.

Result: For a tightly focused witness beam, a near-axis-refined grid gives the same near-axis fields as a uniform grid at the same axis resolution with 30x fewer cells and 30x less compute time; resolves the cold-plasma bubble closure that uniform grids smooth over one cell; AWAKE proton self-modulation agrees with HiPACE++.

Conclusion: The scheme is implemented in the open-source C++/Kokkos code QUARZ (CPU/GPU, MPI with pipelined co-moving decomposition). It is the first production-grade plasma-physics simulation code that is zero-touch, written entirely by an AI agent directed by a physicist.

Abstract: We present a quasi-static particle-in-cell scheme for beam-driven plasma wakefields in cylindrical geometry that works on an arbitrary non-uniform radial grid. The field equations are discretized with hat-function finite elements and conservative finite volumes on any monotone set of radial nodes, and the transverse magnetic field is obtained explicitly, without iterations, following Baxevanis and Stupakov. An optional azimuthal mode $m=1$ describes beam offsets, tilts and hosing. The discretization keeps the discrete Gauss law exact, deposits a uniform plasma exactly on every node including the axis, and remains second-order accurate on smoothly stretched grids. Resolution can therefore be placed where the physics needs it. For a tightly focused witness beam that pulls the plasma ions onto the axis, a grid that is fine only near the axis gives the same near-axis fields as a uniform grid with the same axis resolution, with thirty times fewer cells and in thirty times less computing time. Such a grid also resolves the singular closure of a cold-plasma bubble, which a uniform grid hides by smoothing it over one cell; smoothing the plasma sources over a fixed length, chosen independently of the grid, regularizes the closure and makes the wake behind it converge. The scheme is implemented in the open-source C++/Kokkos code QUARZ (Quasistatic Arbitrary-resolution RZ), which runs on CPUs and GPUs and uses MPI with a pipelined decomposition along the co-moving coordinate. Simulations of the self-modulation of the AWAKE proton bunch agree with the three-dimensional code HiPACE++. QUARZ is, to our knowledge, the first production-grade simulation code in plasma physics that is \emph{zero-touch}: all of its source code, tests and documentation were written by an AI agent directed by a physicist who did not write a single line of the code.

</details>


### [210] [Dynamic Optimization Workflow for Gyrokinetic Stability and Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17496)
*E. A. Belli,J. Larson,J. Candy,I. Sfiligoi*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A dynamic optimization workflow coupling CGYRO with libEnsemble enables derivative-free optimization of linear gyrokinetic stability, maximizing growth-rate-to-wavenumber ratio over the ion-scale spectrum for magnetic flux-surface shaping in DIII-D negative triangularity plasmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explores multi-dimensional plasma parameter spaces and addresses practical challenges in fusion plasma simulation-driven optimization: expensive objective evaluations, unavailable gradients, non-smoothness from transitions between instability branches, and simultaneous multi-core HPC simulations.

Method: Couples the gyrokinetic code CGYRO with libEnsemble for ensemble management. Uses a derivative-free, model-based optimization algorithm with manifold sampling to handle the nonsmooth composite objective structure. Objective maximizes the growth rate-to-wavenumber ratio over the ion-scale spectrum (rather than growth rate at a single wavenumber).

Result: Applied to optimization of local magnetic flux-surface shaping parameters, with an equilibrium baseline drawn from DIII-D negative triangularity experimental parameters.

Conclusion: The workflow is general and extensible, providing a foundation for future optimization studies involving nonlinear transport objectives and higher-dimensional parameter spaces.

Abstract: A dynamic optimization workflow for linear gyrokinetic stability that couples the gyrokinetic code CGYRO with the libEnsemble framework for ensemble management has been developed. The workflow is designed to efficiently explore multi-dimensional plasma parameter spaces and to address the practical challenges of simulation-driven optimization in fusion plasma physics, including expensive objective evaluations, unavailable gradient information, non-smoothness arising from transitions between dominant instability branches, and simultaneous multi-core simulations on high-performance computing resources. Rather than optimizing the growth rate at a single wavenumber, the objective maximizes the growth rate-to-wavenumber ratio over the ion-scale spectrum. A derivative-free, model-based optimization algorithm employs manifold sampling to handle the nonsmooth composite structure of the objective function. The approach is applied to the optimization of local magnetic flux-surface shaping parameters, with an equilibrium baseline drawn from DIII-D negative triangularity experimental parameters. The workflow is general and extensible and provides a foundation for future optimization studies involving nonlinear transport objectives and higher-dimensional parameter spaces.

</details>


### [211] [Effects of Coulomb Collisions on the Structures of Fast Magnetosonic Shocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17594)
*Michael B. Farnell,Fan Guo,Yi-Hsin Liu,Xiaocan Li*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Fully kinetic PIC simulations show that binary Coulomb collisions can inhibit shock rippling and reformation in fast magnetosonic shocks, producing stationary laminar fronts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the role of binary Coulomb collisions in fast magnetosonic shocks, relevant to laboratory shocks and possibly the outer heliosphere.

Method: Fully kinetic particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations incorporating binary Coulomb collisions.

Result: At sufficiently high collisional frequencies, shock rippling and reformation are inhibited, yielding stationary laminar shock fronts. Ion-ion collisions disrupt the upstream-reflected particle motion essential for cyclical reformation, while electron-ion collisions provide a Spitzer resistivity that dissipates the instability driving rippling.

Conclusion: Collisions qualitatively suppress rippling/reformation via distinct ion-ion and electron-ion mechanisms, clarifying collision effects in laboratory shocks and the outer heliosphere.

Abstract: We present fully kinetic particle-in-cell simulations to demonstrate qualitative effects of binary Coulomb collisions on fast magnetosonic shocks. We find that with sufficiently high collisional frequencies, shock rippling and reformation can be inhibited, leading to stationary, laminar shock fronts. We observe that ion-ion collisions disrupt the motion of particles reflected back upstream that is the key for cyclical reformation. Meanwhile, the electron-ion collisions provide a Spitzer resistivity that can dissipate energy from the instability that causes shock rippling. This result helps us understand the role of collisions in laboratory shocks and possibly the outer heliosphere.

</details>


### [212] [Validation of geometric proxies using negative and positive triangularity plasmas with varying elongation on TCV and multi-machine gyrokinetic simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17974)
*Alessandro Balestri,Justin Ball,Stefano Coda,Olivier Sauter,Paola Mantica,Alberto Mariani,Benoit Labit,Branka Vanovac,Joerg Hobirk,the TCV team*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Geometric proxies based on poloidally averaged magnetic drift velocity, its poloidal variation, and FLR effects reliably predict when Negative Triangularity (NT) improves confinement over Positive Triangularity (PT), validated against gyrokinetic simulations and TCV/DIII-D/AUG/JET experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NT tokamak plasmas offer H-mode-like confinement without Edge Localized Modes, but predicting in which configuration this benefit occurs requires understanding how magnetic geometry controls turbulent transport.

Method: Uses geometric proxies (poloidally averaged magnetic drift velocity, its poloidal variation, FLR effects) validated against linear/nonlinear GENE simulations (ITG- and TEM-dominated) and experimental elongation scans (TCV, DIII-D, AUG, JET), including up-down asymmetric equilibria and systematic swaps of geometric coefficients.

Result: Proxies predict NT benefit degrades below κ~0.75–0.8, confirmed by simulations; TCV shows NT with ~1.5× larger β_N and H_98y2 at κ=1.5 but no difference at κ=0.95. DIII-D, AUG, JET NT equilibria show reduced nonlinear heat fluxes (factors 1.8, 1.6, 2.1). Magnetic drifts and FLR effects dominate stabilization; parallel dynamics secondary.

Conclusion: Geometric proxies are reliable, computationally inexpensive predictors of NT transport benefits across diverse shapes and turbulence regimes.

Abstract: Negative Triangularity (NT) tokamak plasmas can achieve H-mode-like confinement while avoiding Edge Localized Modes, making them an attractive reactor scenario. Predicting in which configuration this benefit occurs requires understanding how magnetic geometry controls turbulent transport. Using a set of geometric proxies based on the poloidally averaged magnetic drift velocity, its poloidal variation, and Finite Larmor Radius (FLR) effects, this work validates their predictive power against gyrokinetic simulations and experiments. First, the proxies are used to predict how the relative benefit of NT over Positive Triangularity (PT) evolves with elongation $κ$, forecasting a detrimental effect of NT below $κ\sim0.75$--$0.8$. This prediction is confirmed by linear and nonlinear GENE simulations in both ITG- and TEM-dominated regimes. Additionally, a dedicated experimental elongation scan on TCV shows that NT plasmas at $κ^{\rm LCFS}=1.5$ have $β_N$ and $H_{98y2}$ roughly 1.5 times larger than PT counterparts, while no difference is observed at $κ^{\rm LCFS}=0.95$. The analysis is then extended to realistic ITG-dominated scenarios from DIII-D, AUG, and JET, including up-down asymmetric equilibria where conventional triangularity-based descriptions break down. In all three devices, simulations of NT equilibria exhibit substantially reduced nonlinear heat fluxes relative to PT (factors of 1.8, 1.6, and 2.1, respectively). A systematic swap of geometric coefficients between NT and PT equilibria shows that magnetic drifts and FLR effects are the dominant stabilizing mechanisms across all machines, with parallel dynamics playing a secondary role. These results establish the geometric proxies as reliable, computationally inexpensive predictors of NT transport benefits across diverse shapes and turbulence regimes.

</details>


### [213] [Controlling writhe in stellarator optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18190)
*Tobias M. Schuett,Sophia A. Henneberg*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A fast, smooth method to directly target the writhe of a stellarator plasma boundary during optimization, avoiding dependence on the MHD equilibrium solve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In stellarators, large writhe implies large helical excursion, but it has previously been either an uncontrolled optimization outcome or fixed a priori by the coordinate system choice. A controllable way to target writhe directly was missing.

Method: The method computes writhe and linking number directly from the underlying elliptical boundary of the stellarator, so it requires no properties of the MHD equilibrium solve (such as the true magnetic axis). This makes it fast to evaluate and smooth to differentiate, enabling use as an optimization target.

Result: Examples for quasi-helically symmetric stellarators show that the writhe target works well in practice.

Conclusion: Writhe can be effectively targeted during stellarator optimization using a simple boundary-based calculation, avoiding equilibrium solves and providing a practical, differentiable objective.

Abstract: The writhe of a closed curve measures its coiling, and for a ribbon along it, writhe is the contribution to the linking number not due to twist. In stellarators, large writhe implies large helical excursion and has previously been either an uncontrolled outcome in optimization, or fixed beforehand by the choice of the coordinate system. We present a simple method to target the underlying writhe of the plasma boundary during optimization. Our method calculates writhe and linking number directly from the underlying elliptical boundary of the stellarator. It therefore does not require any properties of the MHD equilibrium solve, such as the true magnetic axis, making it fast to evaluate and smooth to differentiate. Examples for quasi-helically symmetric stellarators show that this target works well in practice.

</details>

# cond-mat.soft [[Back]](#toc)

### [214] [Solubility of poorly soluble species in bulk and nanopores from grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16599)
*Russell Kajouri,Adyant Agrawal,Philipp Stärk,Alexander Schlaich,Christian Holm*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: Grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling reveals that for poorly soluble species in nanopores, the most probable particle number (grand-potential minimum) underestimates the true ensemble-averaged solute loading, so solubility should be read from ensemble averages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In small systems like nanopores, poorly soluble species can have mean populations below one. The grand-potential minimum (most probable particle number) then differs from the ensemble-averaged solute loading, and reading loading from this minimum can falsely indicate no solute. This motivates a correct, efficient way to obtain ensemble averages.

Method: Grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling (GCWLS) is used to compute the density of states Ω(N_A, N_B) for a binary Lennard-Jones mixture of solute B in solvent A, both in bulk and in slit pores. Independent grand-canonical Monte Carlo (GCMC) simulations are used to confirm the GCWLS ensemble averages.

Result: One GCWLS run covers many chemical potentials, unlike GCMC which needs one run per state point. The solvent number distribution is nearly Gaussian, while the dilute solute distribution is skewed and Poisson-like, so the most probable solute number is below the average and is zero whenever ⟨N_B⟩<1. Oversolubility O_B ≈ 12 in a 3.5-diameter pore with repulsive walls, rising to ≈13 with wall-solute attraction, dropping to ≈3.4 with wall-solvent attraction—showing wall-solvent interaction dominates the enhancement. O_B decays toward unity as pore width grows.

Conclusion: Wall-solvent interaction controls most of the solubility enhancement in nanopores, which decreases toward unity as pores widen. In bulk and nanopores alike, the solubility of poorly soluble species should be obtained from ensemble averages rather than from grand-potential minima.

Abstract: Poorly soluble species can have mean populations below one in small systems such as nanopores. In this regime, the grand-potential minimum, which identifies the most probable particle number, differs from the ensemble-averaged solute loading. We use grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling (GCWLS) to calculate the density of states $Ω(N_\mathrm{A}, N_\mathrm{B})$ for a binary Lennard-Jones mixture of solute B in solvent A, both in bulk and in slit pores. One set of GCWLS simulations gives ensemble averages over a range of chemical potentials, whereas conventional grand-canonical Monte Carlo (GCMC) simulation requires a separate simulation at each state point. Independent GCMC simulations confirm the GCWLS ensemble averages. The solvent number distribution is nearly Gaussian, whereas the dilute solute distribution is skewed and Poisson-like. The most probable solute number therefore lies below the ensemble-averaged loading $\langle N_\mathrm{B}\rangle$ and is zero whenever $\langle N_\mathrm{B}\rangle<1$. Reading the solute loading from the grand-potential minimum underestimates it and can falsely indicate that no solute is present. Defining the oversolubility as $\mathcal{O}_\mathrm{B}=x_\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{conf}}/x_\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{bulk}}$, where $x_\mathrm{B}$ is the solute mole fraction, we find $\mathcal{O}_\mathrm{B}\approx12$ in a pore of 3.5 particle diameters width when both wall interactions are repulsive. Adding wall-solute attraction moderately raises it to $\approx13$, whereas wall-solvent attraction lowers it to $\approx3.4$. This comparison shows that the wall-solvent interaction controls most of the enhancement. As the pore widens, $\mathcal{O}_\mathrm{B}$ decreases toward unity. In bulk and in nanopores alike, the solubility of poorly soluble species should be obtained from ensemble averages rather than from grand-potential minima.

</details>


### [215] [First-fracture kinetics in periodic chains: from free-energy landscapes and dynamical recrossing to absolute rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16944)
*Aristotelis P. Sgouros*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: A framework for fracture-healing rates in periodic Morse chains combines deterministic theory, equilibrium simulations, and a quasi-deterministic flux-based approach, revealing chain-length-dependent two-state kinetics, KGH validity, and near-irreversible fracture beyond coexistence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The nearly collinear configuration of highly stretched polymer chains before fracture motivates one-dimensional modeling, but at fixed extension stretching one bond redistributes length across the chain, coupling rupture to the entire chain and complicating rate description.

Method: Combines deterministic theories, equilibrium simulations, and a quasi-deterministic approach using equilibrium fluxes and barrier-top trajectories. Uses constrained-partition calculations, an intact-reservoir PMF for first-fracture surface probability, and recrossing corrections without a rate-fitted prefactor. Applied Kramers-Grote-Hynes, nonlinear reactive-flux, and equilibrium rates.

Result: Constrained-partition calculations reproduce simulated populations and intact-state tensions. KGH, nonlinear reactive-flux and equilibrium rates agree excellently within an operational chain-length boundary. Rates decrease approximately exponentially then decline more gently for longer chains (barrier growth dominates, extra fracture sites partly compensate). Beyond coexistence, long chains fracture practically irreversibly. Projected committor crossings lie near site-PMF maxima. Multiple fractures negligible up to N=50.

Conclusion: The framework identifies an operational chain-length boundary for accurate two-state kinetics and could supply state-dependent rates to mesoscopic fracture models of polymer networks.

Abstract: The nearly collinear configuration of highly stretched polymer chains before fracture motivates one-dimensional models. At fixed extension, stretching one bond redistributes length among the others, coupling rupture to the whole chain. In this work, I develop a framework for fracture-healing rates in periodic Morse chains, combining deterministic theories, equilibrium simulations and a quasi-deterministic approach using equilibrium fluxes and barrier-top trajectories. Constrained-partition calculations closely reproduce simulated populations and intact-state tensions. An intact-reservoir potential of mean force (PMF) supplies the first-fracture surface probability, while recrossing corrects the attempt flux without a rate-fitted prefactor. Independent rate tests identify an operational chain-length boundary for accurate two-state kinetics; failed-return durations decrease relative to population relaxation as chains grow. Kramers-Grote-Hynes (KGH), nonlinear reactive-flux and equilibrium rates show excellent agreement in this regime. KGH predicts rates away from coexistence and a chain-dependent local activation length dominated by the barrier response. Rates decrease approximately exponentially before declining more gently for longer chains: barrier growth dominates, additional fracture sites partly compensate, and transmission varies more weakly. Beyond coexistence, long chains can fracture practically irreversibly because healing becomes too rare to observe. Projected committor crossings, where the configuration-averaged probability of reaching the fractured core first is one half, lie near site-PMF maxima, although configurations at equal bond length retain different fracture probabilities. Multiple fractures remain negligible near coexistence through at least $N=50$. The framework could supply state-dependent rates to mesoscopic fracture models of polymer networks.

</details>

### [216] [Global Regularization of the Spatial Elliptic Restricted Three-body Problem: Quaternionic, Hamiltonian Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13969)
*Kenshiro Oguri*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: This paper develops a global regularization formulation for the spatial elliptic restricted three-body problem (ER3BP) under external perturbations, using quaternionic algebra and Hamiltonian mechanics to remove two gravitational singularities simultaneously.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Objects in space can encounter singularities from the inverse-square gravitational law, hindering analysis and propagation of particle motion. Regularization removes these singularities, but existing methods typically regularize each singularity individually. This paper addresses global regularization that simultaneously removes two singularities (e.g., Earth and Moon) for restricted three-body problems.

Method: The formulation leverages quaternionic (hyper-complex) algebra and Hamiltonian mechanics. This enables: (i) elegant handling of four-dimensional coordinates from dimension-lifting for spatial regularization, (ii) systematic derivation of the coordinate-momentum transformation preserving canonical structure while incorporating perturbing forces, and (iii) geometric insights for analyzing regularity of the transformation, resulting Hamiltonian, and dynamical flow.

Result: Numerical examples demonstrate the validity of the mathematical results and significant numerical benefits of the regularization formulation in the perturbed Earth-Moon ER3BP.

Conclusion: The proposed global regularization formulation enables analysis, propagation, and prediction of particle motion throughout the domain of R3BPs, in contrast to regularizing singularities individually, providing both theoretical framework and numerical advantages.

Abstract: Any objects in space may encounter singularity due to the inverse-square law of the gravitational force, hindering the analysis and propagation of particle motion in space. Regularization in celestial mechanics removes such gravitational singularities in equations of motion by transforming the time and coordinates. This paper addresses a class of regularization methods known as global regularization for restricted three-body problems (R3BPs), which simultaneously removes the two singularities (e.g., Earth and Moon in cislunar space). It enables analysis, propagation, and prediction of particle motion throughout the domain of R3BPs, which is in stark contrast to regularizing each singularity individually. In particular, this paper develops a formulation for global regularization of the spatial elliptic R3BP (ER3BP) under external perturbations. The presented formulation leverages mathematical tools from quaternionic (hyper-complex) algebra and Hamiltonian mechanics. This approach enables us to (i) elegantly handle the four-dimensional coordinates arising from the dimension-lifting operation for spatial regularization, (ii) systematically derive the coordinate-momentum transformation while preserving the canonical structure and incorporating perturbing forces, and (iii) provide geometric insights for analyzing the regularity of the transformation, resulting Hamiltonian, and dynamical flow. Numerical examples demonstrate the validity of the mathematical results as well as significant numerical benefits of the regularization formulation in perturbed Earth-Moon ER3BP.

</details>


### [217] [On the singular set of the geodesic flow on nonpositively curved surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16612)
*Thibault Lefeuvre*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: The singular locus of the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of a nonpositively curved surface has characteristic function in the anisotropic Sobolev space H^{5/8-}_x H^{-13/16}_θ, linking non-ergodicity to a thick zero set of curvature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish regularity of the singular locus of the geodesic flow and understand how non-ergodicity of the flow constrains the curvature's zero set.

Method: Semiclassical analysis combined with a new radial estimate for parabolic flows.

Result: The characteristic function lies in H^{5/8-}_x H^{-13/16}_θ; if the flow is non-ergodic w.r.t. Liouville measure, the curvature's zero set supports a nonzero, nonnegative function in H^{5/8-} (i.e., is "thick").

Conclusion: The singular locus gains extra regularity, and non-ergodicity forces the curvature's zero set to be thick, connecting flow dynamics to curvature degeneracy.

Abstract: We prove that the singular locus of the geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle of a nonpositively curved surface enjoys an additional regularity property: its characteristic function belongs to the geometric anisotropic Sobolev space $H^{5/8-}_xH^{-13/16}_θ$. In particular, this implies that if the geodesic flow is not ergodic with respect to the Liouville measure, then the zero set of the curvature must be thick in the sense that it supports a nonzero, nonnegative function in $H^{5/8-}$. The proof relies on semiclassical analysis and a new radial estimate for parabolic flows.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.SR'></div>

# astro-ph.SR [[Back]](#toc)

### [218] [Radial increase of the compensated interplanetary magnetic field magnitude as a result of the growth of spherically polarized Alfvén waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15741)
*Stuart D. Bale,Timothy S. Horbury,Lorenzo Matteini*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: PSP measurements show that the rise of avg|B|R² inside ~20 Rs is caused by growing large-amplitude, spherically polarized Alfvén waves (switchbacks) acting through a dynamo-like process that enhances |B| by up to 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inside ~20 solar radii, the interplanetary magnetic field magnitude does not fall off as 1/R² as expected; avg|B|R² is 15–20% below its value at ~60 Rs. The cause of this outward rise was unclear.

Method: Using PSP magnetic field measurements over 10.6–56.8 Rs, the authors separate wave contributions from global heliospheric structure and current-sheet proximity, track avg{B_R}R² conservation, measure the wave amplitude ε = B⊥,rms/avg{B∥}, and build a simple model parametrized by broadening of deflection-angle distribution.

Result: The rise is not due to global field structure or current sheet proximity but to growing spherically polarized Alfvén waves (switchbacks). avg{B_R}R² stays constant (sector-signed radial flux conserved). ε grows from 0.26 to 0.57 as R^{1/2}cosα, and avg|B|/avg{B_R} rises by 20%. The model reproduces the effect to high accuracy.

Conclusion: Growing relative wave amplitudes drive a dynamo-like process enhancing field magnitude by up to 20%, raising magnetic pressure and affecting large-scale solar wind structure in the low-beta inner heliosphere, and partially resolving the long-standing discrepancy between remote and in-situ solar magnetic field measurements.

Abstract: Measurements from the NASA Parker Solar Probe (PSP) mission demonstrate that inside $\sim20$ solar radii ($\Rs$) the magnitude of the interplanetary magnetic field does not fall off simply as $1/R^2$, with $\avg{|B|}\,R^2$ 15--20\% below its value at $\sim60\,\Rs$. Here we use PSP magnetic field measurements over $10.6$--$56.8\,\Rs$ to show that the outward rise of $\avg{|B|}\,R^2$ is not due to the global structure of the heliospheric magnetic field or proximity to the heliospheric current sheet but is the direct result of the growth of large-amplitude, spherically polarized Alfvén waves (so-called `switchbacks'). Over the same distance range, $\avg{B_R}\,R^2$ stays constant, so that the sector-signed radial flux is conserved. The wave amplitude $ε= B_{\perp,{\rm rms}}/\avg{B_\parallel}$ grows from $0.26$ to $0.57$ as $R^{1/2}\cosα$, and $\avg{|B|}/\avg{B_R}$ rises by $20\%$. A simple model parametrized by the broadening of the distribution of deflection angles reproduces the effect to high accuracy. These results demonstrate that as the relative wave amplitudes grow with increasing solar distance, a dynamo-like process operates to enhance the field magnitude by up to $20\%$. By enhancing magnetic pressure this has implications for the large scale solar wind structure in the low-beta inner heliosphere, as well as partially resolving the long-standing discrepancy between remote measurements of the solar magnetic field and those made in situ by earlier missions farther from the Sun.

</details>


<div id='gr-qc'></div>

# gr-qc [[Back]](#toc)

### [219] [Classical and quantum scattering of massive Klein--Gordon fields in asymptotically flat spacetimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17819)
*Mikhail Molodyk*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: Using microlocal analysis, the authors construct distinguished incoming and outgoing vacuum states for massive Klein-Gordon fields in non-trapping asymptotically flat spacetimes, showing they satisfy the Hadamard condition and yield a well-defined unitary quantum S-matrix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Defining vacuum states and a well-defined quantum S-matrix in curved (asymptotically flat) spacetimes is subtle; the goal is to do so rigorously, including radiative perturbations of Minkowski space, without relying on explicit asymptotic expansions of classical waves.

Method: Microlocal tools are used to define distinguished incoming and outgoing vacuum states. This rests on a description of classical field scattering that tracks the asymptotic separation between positive- and negative-frequency solutions of the Klein-Gordon equation, avoiding explicit asymptotics.

Result: The asymptotic vacuum states satisfy the Hadamard condition, and the incoming and outgoing particle representations are unitarily equivalent, i.e., the quantum S-matrix is well-defined.

Conclusion: A rigorous, microlocal, asymptotics-free framework yields well-defined Hadamard vacuum states and a unitary quantum S-matrix for massive Klein-Gordon fields in non-trapping asymptotically flat spacetimes.

Abstract: We use microlocal tools to define distinguished incoming and outgoing vacuum states of the quantum theory of massive Klein--Gordon fields in non-trapping asymptotically flat spacetimes, including radiative perturbations of Minkowski space. The asymptotic vacuum states satisfy the Hadamard condition, and the incoming and outgoing particle representations are unitarily equivalent, or in other words the quantum S-matrix is well-defined. These results are based on a description of scattering of classical fields which keeps track of the asymptotic distinction between positive- and negative-frequency solutions of the Klein--Gordon equation. Our emphasis is on developing the theory without recourse to explicit asymptotics for classical waves.

</details>


<div id='math.ST'></div>

# math.ST [[Back]](#toc)

### [220] [Sampling with collective variables: Relative error analysis for umbrella sampling under general conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18717)
*Holden Lee,Matheau Santana-Gijzen,Jonathan Weare*

Main category: math.ST

TL;DR: Non-asymptotic relative-error guarantees for EMUS-based expectation estimation in high dimensions under a log-Sobolev assumption on collective-variable fibers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating expectations over high-dimensional distributions is hard due to slow mixing. A low-dimensional collective variable (CV) map is assumed to capture all slow-mixing directions, enabling efficient estimation.

Method: Assumes fibers of the CV map satisfy a log-Sobolev inequality. Uses the eigenvector method for umbrella sampling (EMUS) with Gaussian biasing windows on the low-dimensional CV space, and analyzes a modified sparse-matrix version of EMUS.

Result: Non-asymptotic relative-error bounds for log-Lipschitz observables and indicators of convex sets. Complexity is polynomial in regularity parameters and inverse relative accuracy, exponential in CV-space dimension. Bounds apply to rare event estimation and quantify effects of mixing of conditionals and observable fluctuation along fibers.

Conclusion: EMUS gives provable relative-error guarantees for high-dimensional expectation and rare-event estimation when a low-dimensional CV map captures slow mixing directions.

Abstract: We consider the problem of estimating the expectation of a function (observable) with respect to a high-dimensional probability distribution, under the assumption that a low-dimensional collective variable map captures all the directions of slow mixing for the distribution. More precisely, under the assumption that the fibers of the collective variable map satisfy a log-Sobolev inequality, we establish non-asymptotic relative-error guarantees for this problem using the eigenvector method for umbrella sampling (EMUS). Our analysis applies to both log-Lipschitz observables and indicator of convex sets, and gives complexity bounds that are polynomial in regularity parameters and inverse relative accuracy, and exponential in the dimension of the collective variable space. As a relative error guarantee, our bounds apply to rare event estimation. We consider Gaussian biasing windows defined on a low-dimensional collective variable space and analyze a modified sparse matrix version of EMUS. The bounds quantify the effect of the map through mixing of the conditional distribution and fluctuation of the observable along the fibers.

</details>


<div id='hep-ph'></div>

# hep-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [221] [BSMScanner: a lightweight framework for beyond-the-Standard-Model global fits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15096)
*Mohamed Belfkir*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: BSMScanner is a framework where BSM parameter scans are specified declaratively via YAML rather than code. The spec is validated, compiled into a typed C++ evaluation kernel, and driven by selectable scanning engines, demonstrated on six benchmark models with 4–26 free parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional BSM parameter scans require writing bespoke programs, which is error-prone and hard to reuse. A declarative, reusable specification could streamline model scanning.

Method: Users declare free parameters, priors, mass matrices, diagonalisations, observables, theory-validity conditions, and likelihood terms in YAML. The framework validates and resolves this into a dependency graph, compiles it into a typed C++ kernel, and drives it with scanning engines (uniform sampling, two differential-evolution engines, staged basin-discovery, optional MCMC refinement). A built-in library covers flavour physics (PMNS/CKM, oscillation likelihoods, flavour observables); non-closed-form quantities (e.g. relic density) plug in via C++/Python.

Result: Demonstrated on six benchmark models with 4 to 26 free parameters, reporting for each the specification, scan configuration, best fit, and analytic cross-checks.

Conclusion: BSMScanner offers a declarative, reusable, and efficient approach to BSM parameter scanning, validated across diverse benchmark models.

Abstract: We present BSMScanner, a framework in which a beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) parameter scan is specified as a set of declarative YAML documents rather than written as a program. The user declares the free parameters and their priors, the mass matrices and the diagonalisations that act on them, the observables, the theory-validity conditions and the likelihood terms. The framework validates this specification, resolves it into a dependency graph and compiles it once into a typed C++ evaluation kernel, which is then driven by an independently chosen scanning engine: uniform sampling, two differential-evolution engines, or a staged basin-discovery orchestrator, optionally followed by Markov-chain posterior refinement. Flavour physics matrix diagonalisation, PMNS and CKM construction in fixed conventions, tabulated oscillation likelihoods and a library of common flavour observables are provided by the framework and imported by name, so that a model file contains only model-specific definitions; quantities without a closed form, such as a relic density, enter through a C++ or Python plugin interface. We describe the specification schema, the observable and likelihood library, the scanning engines and their configuration, and demonstrate the framework on six benchmark models with $4$ to $26$ free parameters, giving for each the specification, the scan configuration, the resulting best fit and analytic cross-checks of the result.

</details>


<div id='physics.geo-ph'></div>

# physics.geo-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [222] [Bayesian Inversion Beyond the Generator's Range: Reducing Non-physical Constraints from Generative Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16614)
*Pratyay Roy,Xuebin Zhao,Andrew Curtis*

Main category: physics.geo-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces an "escaping prior" distribution for geophysical inversion that uses Generative Neural Networks (GNNs) as a guide while allowing solutions beyond the GNN's low-dimensional manifold, overcoming a fundamental limitation of latent space inversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent space inversion with GNNs is fundamentally flawed because Earth structure is not intrinsically low-dimensional and GNNs carry training errors; solutions remain trapped on a low-dimensional manifold and cannot even reproduce the best higher-dimensional approximation to the true Earth.

Method: An "escaping prior" distribution gives freedom to find solutions beyond the GNN-generated manifold while still using the GNN as a guide, with a hierarchical Bayesian approach adapting prior strength to observed data and synthetic forward function a posteriori. It is illustrated on low-dimensional problems (escaping prior over a 1D manifold in 2D space) and tested on synthetic tomography problems.

Result: Compared with standard latent space and uniform-prior model space inversions across simple to geologically realistic synthetic tomography cases, with true solutions both far from and close to the manifold, the escaping prior consistently yields significantly better results, finding solutions beyond each GNN's generative capabilities and including the best possible model.

Conclusion: The escaping prior overcomes the fundamental limitation of latent space inversion, adaptively balances prior and data information, and clearly demonstrates the shortcomings of pure latent space inversion in geophysics.

Abstract: To address non-uniqueness in geophysical inversions, Generative Neural Networks (GNNs) are often used to parameterise geological or other prior information in a lower-dimensional space (the so-called latent space), thereby reducing the number of inversion parameters. However, the training samples that represent this information are then always incorrect, because Earth structure is not intrinsically low-dimensional, and all GNNs are subject to training errors. Consequently, even when solutions are mapped back to the original higher-dimensional space, they inherit this imperfection and remain restricted to a low-dimensional manifold. From a mathematical point of view, latent space inversion thus fails fundamentally: generally, it can not even reproduce the best, higher-dimensional approximation to the true Earth. To overcome this limitation, we introduce an `escaping prior' distribution, which provides the necessary freedom to find solutions beyond the manifold of GNN-generated solutions, while still using the GNN as a guide. We use a hierarchical Bayesian approach to adapt the strength of prior information to the observed data and synthetic forward function $\textit{a posteriori}$. We illustrate our method on low-dimensional problems where the escaping prior is defined over a 1D manifold in 2D space. We then compare our approach with standard latent space and model space inversions using a uniform prior distribution in synthetic tomography problems, ranging from simple to more geologically realistic cases. Furthermore, we tested scenarios where the true solution is either far from or close to the manifold. In each case, our escaping prior shows significantly better results than the alternatives. It results in solutions beyond each GNN's generative capabilities, solutions which include the best possible model, and clearly demonstrates the limitations of latent space inversion.

</details>


<div id='eess.SY'></div>

# eess.SY [[Back]](#toc)

### [223] [$H_2$-optimal model order reduction using hyperbolic geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16249)
*Andrea Angino,Tamás Dózsa,Matthias Voigt*

Main category: eess.SY

TL;DR: A framework using Malmquist-Takenaka expansions and hyperbolic geometry computes H2-optimal reduced-order models for SISO LTI systems while guaranteeing ROM stability, unlike IRKA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IRKA is widely used for H2-optimal model order reduction, but the stability of the resulting ROM is not generally guaranteed. This work aims to enforce stability during H2-optimal approximation.

Method: Uses Malmquist-Takenaka orthogonal expansions in H2-spaces and their connection to hyperbolic geometry to define an equilibrium function. The poles of an H2-optimal ROM are the zeros of this function. The resulting nonlinear root-finding problem is solved via a Newton-type method on products of Poincare disk manifolds, with updates along hyperbolic geodesics ensuring stability at every iteration.

Result: Numerical experiments on benchmark systems for discrete- and continuous-time LTI MOR show the method preserves stability throughout iterations, including cases where IRKA produces unstable reduced models.

Conclusion: The proposed hyperbolic-geometry-based framework yields stable H2-optimal ROMs for SISO LTI systems, overcoming IRKA's stability limitations.

Abstract: We revisit the problem of approximating a linear time-invariant LTI model by a reduced-order model (ROM) in an $H_2$-optimal sense. A widely used method for computing such ROMs is the iterative rational Krylov algorithm (IRKA). However, in practice, the stability of the resulting ROM is not generally guaranteed. In this work, we develop a novel framework that can be used to obtain $H_2$-optimal models of single-input single-output LTI systems while enforcing stability of the ROM. We use Malmquist-Takenaka orthogonal expansions in the corresponding $H_2$-spaces and their connection to hyperbolic geometry to develop an equilibrium function. We show that the poles of an $H_2$-optimal ROM transfer function are defined by the zeros of this equilibrium function. The resulting nonlinear root-finding problem is solved using a Newton-type method formulated on the products of Poincaré disk manifolds, where updates are performed along hyperbolic geodesics ensuring that the stability constraint is satisfied at every iteration. We present numerical experiments on benchmark systems for both discrete- and continuous-time LTI MOR problems, including examples where IRKA produces unstable reduced models while the proposed method preserves stability throughout the iteration.

</details>


<div id='nlin.PS'></div>

# nlin.PS [[Back]](#toc)

### [224] [Selection of Marginally Stable Patterns by Boundary Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15201)
*Jacob Daniels,Simon Eskin,Tel Fahrendholz,Arnd Scheel*

Main category: nlin.PS

TL;DR: Boundary conditions restrict stable wavenumbers in pattern-forming systems to a narrow band; this work shows solutions evolve toward an extremal (edge) member of that band via boundary "snapping" events, verified in Swift-Hohenberg simulations and proved using a phase-diffusion approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that many boundary conditions only permit equilibria within a narrow wavenumber band, but which member of that band is actually selected by the dynamics remained unclear. The paper aims to determine the wavenumber that pattern-forming solutions converge to under general initial conditions.

Method: Analyzes wavenumber selection in pattern-forming systems via a phase-diffusion approximation, using comparison principles and carefully constructed subsolutions that mimic the observed snapping behavior. Complementary numerical simulations of the Swift-Hohenberg equation are performed after a parameter quench confines the pattern-forming region to a large spatial interval.

Result: For a broad class of initial conditions, solutions evolve toward an extremal member of the admissible wavenumber band. This extremal equilibrium is neutrally stable yet nonlinearly unstable, and convergence occurs through a sequence of snapping events at the boundary. These behaviors are observed in Swift-Hohenberg simulations and confirmed analytically.

Conclusion: The dynamics select the extremal, boundary-compatible wavenumber rather than an interior one, with convergence driven by successive boundary snapping events. This selection principle is established rigorously in the phase-diffusion approximation and supported by simulations of the Swift-Hohenberg equation.

Abstract: We study the effect of boundary conditions on the selection of wavenumbers in pattern-forming systems. Prior work established that, for a large class of boundary conditions, equilibria only within a narrow band of wavenumbers are compatible with the boundary. We demonstrate here that for a large class of initial conditions, solutions evolve towards an extremal member of this narrow band of equilibria. In fact, this extremal member is neutrally stable, nonlinearly unstable, and the convergence is realized through a series of snapping events at the boundary. We observe this behavior in simulations of the Swift-Hohenberg equation when the pattern forming region is confined to a large spatial interval through a parameter quench. We prove the convergence in a phase-diffusion approximation using comparison principles and astutely chosen subsolutions that mimic the snapping behavior.

</details>


<div id='q-bio.QM'></div>

# q-bio.QM [[Back]](#toc)

### [225] [Data-Driven Discovery of a Hyperelastic Strain-Energy Function for Veins Using Constitutive Artificial Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15229)
*Marisa Bazzi,Divya Adil,Gavin Mays,Ellen Kuhl,Christopher K. Breuer,Jay D. Humphrey,Alison L. Marsden*

Main category: q-bio.QM

TL;DR: A constitutive artificial neural network (CANN) framework discovers a compact, interpretable 8-term hyperelastic model from biaxial data across three vein types, outperforming established venous constitutive models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Veins experience altered mechanical loading in clinical contexts (vein grafting, arteriovenous fistula creation, tissue-engineered grafts), so accurate constitutive models are clinically important. However, venous behavior differs from arterial, and existing models fail to simultaneously fit circumferential and axial stress-stretch responses across vein types.

Method: A CANN framework discovers a compact, interpretable hyperelastic constitutive model from biaxial mechanical data of three vein types (saphenous, jugular, inferior vena cava). It uses an expanded candidate function library with structurally motivated terms for progressive fiber recruitment and fiber-family interactions, then applies Sobol sensitivity analysis, correlation-based pruning, and response-level family collapse to reduce the basis to a compact strain-energy density function.

Result: The resulting eight-term strain-energy density function achieved significantly higher fitting accuracy than established venous constitutive models across all three vein types (R² > 0.9, p < 0.01).

Conclusion: The work establishes a framework for constitutive model discovery in veins and proposes a candidate constitutive form for further validation across larger, independent cohorts.

Abstract: Veins are commonly subjected to altered mechanical loading in clinical settings such as vein grafting, arteriovenous fistula creation, and tissue-engineered vascular graft implantation, making accurate constitutive models of venous tissue clinically important. Venous mechanical behavior differs characteristically from that of arteries, and existing constitutive models struggle to simultaneously fit the circumferential and axial stress-stretch responses across vein types. Here, we present a constitutive artificial neural network (CANN) framework that discovers a compact, interpretable hyperelastic constitutive model from biaxial mechanical data across three types of veins (saphenous, jugular, and inferior vena cava). The framework incorporates an expanded candidate function library with structurally motivated terms representing progressive fiber recruitment and fiber-family interactions. Sobol sensitivity analysis, correlation-based pruning, and response-level family collapse were then used to reduce the discovered basis to a compact strain-energy density function (SEF). The resulting eight-term SEF achieved significantly higher fitting accuracy than established venous constitutive models across all three types of veins $(R^2>0.9, p<0.01)$. These findings establish a framework for constitutive model discovery in veins and propose a candidate constitutive form for further validation across larger, independent cohorts.

</details>


<div id='math.FA'></div>

# math.FA [[Back]](#toc)

### [226] [(Super-)fast convergence rates for Chernoff approximations via centered differences and Richardson extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16094)
*Comlan Edmond Koudjinan*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: Constructs Chernoff families via centered differences, uses Richardson extrapolation to cancel polynomial error terms and raise convergence order, achieving super-fast rates for parabolic, Schrödinger-type, and dispersive operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To constructively answer Remizov's challenge (Problem 30, arXiv:2410.00416) of super-fast Chernoff approximation, i.e., achieving higher-order and even super-fast convergence rates for approximating operator semigroups.

Method: Use centered finite differences to build Chernoff families, derive even asymptotic expansions of their iterated products, then apply Richardson extrapolation to cancel the polynomial part of the error and boost convergence order. Constructions are explicit in coefficients of the differential operators and do not rely on the target semigroup.

Result: At extrapolation depth m, obtains rates O(n^{-2m}) on Sobolev scales H^{2m+δ}, H^{4m}, and H^{5m} (any δ>0) for uniformly parabolic, Schrödinger-type second-order, and third-order dispersive operators respectively. In the analytic category, all-order estimates allow depth growing with base resolution, giving super-fast rates e^{-cn^2} (second-order models) and e^{-cn} (third-order).

Conclusion: Provides a constructive positive solution to the super-fast Chernoff approximation problem, with explicit, semigroup-free constructions yielding high-order and super-fast convergence rates.

Abstract: We use centered differences to construct Chernoff families and derive even asymptotic expansions for their iterated products. Richardson extrapolation is then used to cancel polynomial part and thereby raise the convergence order. As illustrations, we apply the construction to uniformly parabolic, Schrödinger-type second-order operators and to a third-order dispersive operator, obtaining, at extrapolation depth $m$, the rates $ O(n^{-2m}) $ on the Sobolev scales $H^{2m+δ}$, $H^{4m}$, and $H^{5m}$, respectively, with arbitrary $δ>0$. In the analytic category, quantitative all-order estimates allow the extrapolation depth to grow with the base resolution and yield the super-fast rates $e^{-cn^2}$ for the two second-order models and $e^{-cn} $ for the third-order model. The constructions are explicit in terms of the coefficients of the underlying differential operators and do not use the target semigroup in their definition. This provides a constructive positive answer to the challenge of super-fast Chernoff approximation formulated by Remizov in Problem 30 of arXiv:2410.00416.

</details>


### [227] [Dimensional transition and spectral instability in lattice Hardy inequalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14950)
*Carlos Lizama*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: Studies how discretization changes the sharp Hardy inequality threshold on the integer lattice in dimensions ≥3, which matches the continuum value up to dimension 8 and drops from dimension 9, and analyzes the resulting negative eigenvalue created by local attraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discretization can alter a sharp stability threshold even when the continuum threshold is recovered at infinity, so the authors want to understand this threshold-shift phenomenon for the Hardy inequality on the integer lattice.

Method: They introduce the total critical defect (summable error from testing the lattice operator against the critical continuum power), use a theta-function representation, explicit positive Hardy remainders, and an exact energy identity linking the defect to the energy saved by optimizing outside the origin's neighbours. For the general case they analyze lattice operators with critical inverse-square tails via zero-energy solutions.

Result: Best lattice Hardy coefficient equals the continuum value through dimension 8 and is smaller from dimension 9; the defect is negative and strictly decreasing from dimension 9 (positive in dimensions 3-8 via explicit remainders). Local attraction produces a negative eigenvalue, exponentially small near the threshold, with rate and prefactor set by zero-energy solutions; the emerging state's spread gives a two-dimensional return scale for the associated Markov chain in every ambient dimension.

Conclusion: The threshold transition is characterized by the total critical defect, with the first loss of positivity located in the coupling of the stable exterior to the origin's neighbours; these techniques extend to general lattice operators with critical inverse-square tails, finite modifications, and nonradial corrections.

Abstract: Discretization can change a sharp stability threshold even when the continuum threshold is recovered at infinity. We study this phenomenon for the Hardy inequality on the integer lattice in dimensions at least three: its best coefficient agrees with the continuum value through dimension eight and is smaller from dimension nine onwards. We characterize this transition by the total critical defect, the summable error obtained by testing the lattice operator against the critical continuum power. A theta-function representation proves that the defect is negative and strictly decreasing from dimension nine; explicit positive Hardy remainders establish its positivity in dimensions three through eight. An exact energy identity relates this defect to the energy saved by optimizing the profile outside the neighbours of the origin. This exterior remains stable in dimensions eight and nine, locating the first loss of positivity in its coupling to those neighbours. We give self-contained proofs of both dimensional ranges. We then describe how local attraction creates a negative eigenvalue in a general class of lattice operators with critical inverse-square tails. Its magnitude is exponentially small near the threshold, with both the rate and the multiplicative prefactor determined by zero-energy solutions. The result allows connected finite modifications of the lattice and nonradial corrections to the tail. The same analysis controls the spread of the emerging state and gives a two-dimensional return scale for the associated Markov chain in every ambient dimension.

</details>


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [228] [AI Meets Turbulence Modeling: Complex Systems Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14355)
*Sharath S. Girimaji*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Proposes a turbulence taxonomy map parameterized by Reynolds number and degree of non-equilibrium to guide optimal, situation-aware AI/ML deployment in turbulence modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI/ML turbulence modeling has yielded only modest progress due to a lack of situation-aware deployment. ML-RANS closures fail to generalize across flow regimes and are often misapplied outside their validity, while scale-resolving simulations (SRS) remain computationally prohibitive and are unnecessarily used where RANS suffices. A principled way to decide which method to deploy is needed.

Method: The authors propose a turbulence taxonomy map parameterized by degrees of freedom (Re) and the degree of non-equilibrium (SK/ε). Applying Weaver's complexity framework, they classify turbulence into distinct physical regimes.

Result: The classification identifies where local ML-RANS surrogates can generalize, clarifies where explicit scale resolution is physically mandatory, and highlights where physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) are most effective.

Conclusion: The perspective establishes a physics-grounded framework for flow-aware, successful deployment of AI/ML in turbulence modeling.

Abstract: Despite widespread enthusiasm, applying artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML) to turbulence modeling has yielded only modest progress, primarily due to a lack of situation-aware deployment. Data-driven Reynolds-averaged Navier--Stokes (ML-RANS) closures frequently fail to generalize across diverse flow regimes, while scale-resolving simulations (SRS) remain computationally prohibitive. As a result, ML-RANS is often misapplied outside its domain of validity, and ML-SRS is unnecessarily deployed on flows fully amenable to RANS. To guide optimal AI/ML deployment, we propose a turbulence taxonomy map parameterized by degrees of freedom ($Re$) and the degree of non-equilibrium ($SK/ε$). By applying Weaver's well-recognized complexity framework, we classify turbulence into distinct physical regimes. This categorization identifies where local ML-RANS surrogates can generalize, clarifies where explicit scale resolution is physically mandatory, and highlights where physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) are most effective. Ultimately, this perspective establishes a physics-grounded framework for the flow-aware, successful deployment of AI/ML in turbulence.

</details>


### [229] [Nonlocal generalization of the Chapman-Enskog method and hydrodynamic equations. Nonlocal vorticity transport in a turbulent medium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16014)
*A. B. Kukushkin*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper extends the generalized Chapman-Enskog method to account for arbitrary gradients of velocity and temperature, yielding a nonlocal viscous stress tensor and a vorticity transport equation analogous to the Biberman-Holstein equation, with a power-law velocity distribution function that reproduces Richardson's t^3 law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prior work (Fluid Dyn 61, 20 (2026)) only treated arbitrary gradients of hydrodynamic velocity; here the authors seek to extend the generalized Chapman-Enskog method and nonlocal viscous stress tensor to arbitrary gradients of temperature as well, in order to better describe tangential discontinuities and separated flows.

Method: Generalized Chapman-Enskog method extended to arbitrary gradients of both hydrodynamic velocity and temperature, producing a nonlocal (spatially integral, non-diffusive) viscous stress tensor; for an incompressible medium, a nonlocal vorticity transport equation is derived and compared structurally to the Biberman-Holstein equation for resonant radiation transport.

Result: The resulting equations simplify the description of tangential discontinuities and separated flows. For incompressible media, the nonlocal vorticity transport equation is structurally identical to the Biberman-Holstein equation. A power-law form for the velocity distribution function of long-free-path vorticity carriers is determined that satisfies the empirical Richardson t^3 law for the mean square relative separation of particle pairs in turbulent liquid or gaseous media.

Conclusion: The generalized Chapman-Enskog framework, extended to arbitrary velocity and temperature gradients, yields a nonlocal vorticity transport equation formally matching the Biberman-Holstein equation and a vorticity-carrier distribution reproducing Richardson's t^3 law, linking kinetic theory to turbulent dispersion.

Abstract: The results from [Fluid Dyn 61, 20 (2026)], concerning the generalized Chapman-Enskog method and a nonlocal (i.e., non-diffusive, spatially integral) viscous stress tensor in the Navier-Stokes equation, are extended to the case of arbitrary gradients of not only hydrodynamic velocity but also temperature. The resulting equations simplify the description of tangential discontinuities and separated flows. For the specific case of an incompressible medium, a nonlocal vorticity transport equation is derived that is structurally identical to the Biberman-Holstein equation for resonant radiation transport in plasmas and gases. A power-law form has been determined for the velocity distribution function of long-free-path vorticity carriers that satisfies empirical Richardson t^3 law for the mean square of relative separation of a pair of particles in a turbulent liquid or gaseous medium.

</details>


### [230] [A ghost-cell immersed boundary method for the shallow water equations on Cartesian grids: mass conservation and wetting and drying over variable topography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16148)
*Yuta Yaegashi*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A ghost-cell immersed boundary method for 2D shallow water equations on Cartesian grids over variable topography, made mass-conservative via a cut-cell form of the continuity equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ghost-cell immersed boundary methods leak mass through solid walls, and a full cut-cell treatment requires cumbersome handling of momentum equations (wall pressure, cell merging) on cut cells. The paper seeks a robust, conservative, yet simple alternative.

Method: Explicit Godunov-type finite volume scheme (unsplit second-order MUSCL, HLLC flux, hydrostatic reconstruction) on Cartesian grids. Eight ghost-value constructions plus a cut-cell continuity equation are tested on seven problems (wetting/drying on sloping beds, laboratory dam break). In the final scheme, ghost values are bilinearly interpolated at the image point with solid and dry nodes excluded and remaining weights renormalized; mass conservation comes from solving the continuity equation in cut-cell form while momentum equations are left unchanged.

Result: The scheme is about as accurate as the unmodified ghost-cell method, conserves mass, and preserves a lake at rest whose shoreline crosses an immersed wall.

Conclusion: Modifying only the continuity equation yields a conservative ghost-cell immersed boundary method, avoiding the momentum-equation complications (wall pressure, cell merging) of a full cut-cell approach.

Abstract: A ghost-cell immersed boundary method is proposed for the two-dimensional shallow water equations with a variable bed (topography), solved with an explicit Godunov-type finite volume scheme (unsplit second-order MUSCL, HLLC flux, hydrostatic reconstruction) on Cartesian grids. Ghost-cell methods leak mass through the wall. To find a robust and conservative treatment, eight constructions of the ghost values and a cut-cell form of the continuity equation were applied to seven test problems, which include wetting and drying on sloping beds and a laboratory dam break. In the resulting scheme, the ghost values are interpolated bilinearly at the image point; solid and dry nodes are excluded, and the weights of the remaining nodes are renormalized. Mass is conserved by solving the continuity equation in cut-cell form, while the momentum equations are left unchanged. The proposed scheme is about as accurate as the ghost-cell method without this modification and preserves a lake at rest whose shoreline crosses an immersed wall. Because only the continuity equation is modified, it avoids the treatment of the momentum equations on cut cells (wall pressure, cell merging) that a full cut-cell method requires.

</details>


### [231] [One-way pressure-independent valves with hysteresis and chattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18242)
*Fabio Bagagiolo,Andrea Corli,Stefan Moreti,Massimiliano D. Rosini*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A 1D isothermal gas flow model with a valve-controlled pipe, formulated as two conservation laws with coupling conditions, is studied via the Riemann problem; non-classical shocks, valve chattering, and stabilizing hysteresis are characterized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding oscillatory behaviors in valve-controlled pipe flows (chattering and hysteresis) requires a tractable yet physically meaningful model.

Method: Proposes a 1D isothermal gas flow model through a valve, described by two conservation laws with coupling conditions. The corresponding Riemann problem is studied, where non-classical shocks appear. States leading to chattering are characterized as functions of model parameters.

Result: Non-classical shocks emerge in the Riemann problem. Two oscillatory behaviors are identified: chattering of the valve (visible in numerical simulations) and stabilizing hysteresis induced by valve operation. Chattering states and their parameter dependence are characterized, and the interplay between chattering and hysteresis is studied numerically.

Conclusion: The simple model effectively captures key oscillatory phenomena of valve-controlled pipe flow—chattering and hysteresis—and their interaction, offering insights into parameter-dependent behavior.

Abstract: We propose a model for the one-dimensional isothermal gas flow through a valve in a pipe. The valve aims at keeping the outgoing flow in an assigned interval; moreover, its opening and closing depend on a further parameter. The model is realized by a system of two conservation laws with suitable conditions coupling the ingoing and the outgoing flow. We study the corresponding Riemann problem, where non-classical shocks show up. Although simple, the model allows to study two key oscillatory behaviors of the flow, which are due, respectively, to the chattering of the valve, especially seen in the numerical simulations, and to the stabilizing hysteresis induced by the valve operation. We characterize the states leading to chattering and show how they depend on the parameters of the model. Through several numerical simulations, we study the interplay between the two above phenomena.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [232] [Finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory: a unified framework for i-DMFT and TAO-DFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14706)
*Jingang Han,Huanjing Gong,Kai Luo,Hongming Weng,Xinguo Ren*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT), a unified framework showing that i-DMFT and TAO-DFT are distinct reductions of the same theory, and demonstrates improved bond-breaking and strongly correlated solid-state behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Describing chemical bond breaking requires capturing strong/static electron correlation, which single-reference methods like Hartree-Fock and Kohn-Sham DFT struggle with. Fractional occupations help (as in i-DMFT and TAO-DFT), but these methods are formulated in different frameworks, somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship is unclear.

Method: Introduce FT-AC-RDMFT. The natural-orbital eigenvalue equation and Fermi-Dirac occupations derive from the variational principle, and a thermal adiabatic connection links i-DMFT's empirical entropy coefficient to a characteristic temperature of the auxiliary non-interacting system. i-DMFT is the exact-exchange reduction; TAO-DFT arises when exact exchange is replaced by a density-based exchange-correlation functional.

Result: i-DMFT coincides with the exact-exchange realization of the framework (up to a constant). Improved dissociation behavior from finite-temperature occupations extends beyond exact exchange to hybrid and semilocal functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially reduce the overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, matching experiment.

Conclusion: FT-AC-RDMFT provides a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature occupation-based electronic-structure methods in molecular and solid-state systems.

Abstract: Accurately describing chemical bond breaking requires capturing strong/static electron correlation, a major challenge for single-reference self-consistent-field methods such as Hartree--Fock and Kohn--Sham density-functional theory (DFT). Fractional occupations substantially improve this situation, as demonstrated by information-density-matrix-functional theory (i-DMFT) and thermally-assisted-occupation DFT (TAO-DFT). Although useful, they are formulated within rather different theoretical frameworks, are somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship remains unclear. Here we introduce finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT), in which i-DMFT and TAO-DFT emerge as different reductions of a unified framework.
