The effective eigenvalue equation for natural orbitals and the Fermi--Dirac occupation numbers follow directly from the variational principle, while a thermal adiabatic connection relates the empirical entropy coefficient of i-DMFT to a characteristic temperature of the auxiliary non-interacting system. Within this framework, i-DMFT is the exact-exchange-based reduction, whereas TAO-DFT results when exact exchange is replaced by a density-based exchange-correlation functional. Numerical calculations confirm that i-DMFT, apart from a constant, coincides with the exact-exchange realization of the framework, and show that the improved dissociation behavior from finite-temperature occupations extends beyond exact exchange to hybrid and semilocal density functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially reduce the severely overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, bringing it close to experiment. These results establish FT-AC-RDMFT as a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature occupation-based electronic-structure methods in molecular and solid-state systems.

</details>


### [233] [First-Principles Calculations of the Electron-Hole-Pair Creation Energy in Semiconductors and Dielectrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16949)
*Dallin O. Nielsen,Massimo V. Fischetti*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles study computing electron-hole-pair creation energies in AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2, showing GW/BSE corrections improve agreement with experiment, especially for Si.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The average energy required to create an electron-hole pair is a key quantity in semiconductor and dielectric physics, but accurate first-principles prediction remains challenging, particularly for Si where standard DFT-based methods deviate notably from experiment.

Method: DFT band structures, density-functional perturbation theory for phonons, TDDFT for the dielectric/energy-loss function, and full-band Monte Carlo simulation of electron thermalization up to ~100 eV. For Si, GW quasiparticle corrections and the Bethe-Salpeter equation were used to refine the band structure and dielectric function.

Result: Pair-creation energies of ~12.2, 6.5, 7.3, 3.10, and 16.7 eV/pair for AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2, mostly in good agreement with experiment; Si was lowest (~14.9% below ~3.6 eV/pair). Using GW/BSE gave ~3.50 eV/pair for Si, attributed to a slight reduction of the energy-loss function below ~10-15 eV.

Conclusion: The creation energy is mainly governed by competition between phonon emission and ionization around the crossing of phonon-scattering and ionization rates, with the plasmon peak location and shape in the energy-loss function as another major factor. GW/BSE improves accuracy over standard DFT/TDDFT.

Abstract: We present first-principles calculations of the electron-hole-pair creation energy in semiconductors and dielectrics, focusing in particular on AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2. We have employed density functional theory (DFT) to calculate the band structure, density-functional perturbation theory to obtain the phonon dispersion, and time-dependent DFT (TDDFT) to calculate the electron energy loss function (via the dielectric function). We performed full-band Monte Carlo simulations of electron thermalization for energies up to ~100 eV and calculated the average energy required to excite an electron-hole pair. We calculated pair-creation energies of approximately 12.2, 6.5, 7.3, 3.10, and 16.7 eV/pair for AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2, respectively. These values are in good agreement with measured data, with the largest discrepancy being that of Si (about 14.9% lower than the accepted experimental value of ~3.6 eV/pair). To improve the agreement for Si, we used the GW approach and the Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) to calculate the quasiparticle shifts of the electronic band structure and the dielectric function, respectively. Finally, using the GW/BSE data, we calculated a creation energy of ~3.50 eV/pair. We find that the marked improvement in the accuracy of the calculated creation energy, when using the GW/BSE approach, is primarily attributable to the slight decrease in the magnitude of the electron energy-loss function when using the GW/BSE approach for energies below ~10-15 eV. Overall, it is clear that the competition between phonon emission and ionization near the intersection of the phonon scattering and ionization rates is largely responsible for determining the value of the creation energy, with the other major factor being the location and shape of the plasmon peak in the energy-loss function.

</details>


### [234] [Can Universal Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials Capture Small Polarons? A Geometry-Based Benchmark in Transition-Metal Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17352)
*Vladislav Korostelev,Pjotrs Žguns,Konstantin Klyukin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Universal ML interatomic potentials (uMLIPs) show inconsistent ability to describe small polarons in TM oxides, ranging from delocalized states to metastable polarons, and all fail to stabilize separated polaron-donor configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small polarons govern charge transport, catalysis, and degradation in transition-metal oxides, but it is unclear whether uMLIPs—which lack explicit electronic degrees of freedom—can capture them.

Method: Investigated H-induced electron localization across various TM oxides using a geometric descriptor based on TM-O bond-length expansion (Z-score) to identify polaronic distortions, and compared uMLIP behavior against DFT+U predictions.

Result: uMLIPs exhibit varying degrees of electron localization (from delocalized to metastable polarons), strongly dependent on training data and electronic correlation treatment. Missing Hubbard U corrections for specific TM cations cause delocalization, unlike DFT+U. Some uMLIPs reproduce polaron hopping barriers in coupled proton-polaron transfer, but all fail to stabilize spatially separated polaron-donor configurations.

Conclusion: uMLIPs are currently unreliable for describing small polarons in TM oxides, particularly for spatially separated polaron-donor states, highlighting the need for better electronic-correlation-aware training.

Abstract: Small polarons, formed when excess electrons become localized through lattice distortions, play a critical role in charge transport, catalysis, and degradation processes in transition-metal (TM) oxides. Whether universal machine-learning interatomic potentials (uMLIPs), which lack explicit electronic degrees of freedom, can describe them remains unclear. Here, we investigate H-induced electron localization across a range of TM oxides using a geometric descriptor based on TM-O bond-length expansion (Z-score) to identify polaronic distortions. We find that uMLIPs exhibit varying degrees of electron localization, from delocalized states to metastable small polarons, with their behavior strongly dependent on the training data and treatment of electronic correlations. In particular, the absence of Hubbard U corrections for specific TM cations in the training data leads to electron delocalization, in contrast to DFT+U predictions. Although some uMLIPs reproduce polaron hopping barriers during coupled proton-polaron transfer, all of them fail to stabilize spatially separated polaron-donor configurations, instead favoring localization near the H dopant.

</details>


### [235] [A grand potential for molten-salt electrochemical phase diagrams from first principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17611)
*Lorenzo Bastonero,Lucio Colombi Ciacchi,Nicola Marzari*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A first-principles framework constructs electrochemical phase diagrams for multicomponent molten-salt systems, accelerating electrode/electrolyte design for carbon-free metal production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clean-energy transition drives massive metal demand, and metal extraction is a major carbon source. Molten-salt electrolysis can enable carbon-free production, but corrosive conditions and complex multicomponent feedstocks make experimental development costly and difficult.

Method: The authors derive a grand potential to generate electrochemical phase diagrams at scale, using free energies from first-principles data via a fine-tuned foundation machine-learned interatomic potential. They validate on silicon in molten CaCl2 and screen ten alkali/alkaline-earth halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning using three thermodynamic criteria.

Result: For Si in molten CaCl2, the framework recovers the experimental diagram on a limited structure set and predicts new domains with a larger set. Screening for Al electrowinning identifies NaF, where cryolite emerges as the stable aluminum-bearing phase, recovering Hall–Héroult chemistry established empirically in 1886.

Conclusion: The framework provides a first-principles basis for selecting compatible electrodes, electrolytes, and operating conditions toward low-carbon metal production from complex source materials.

Abstract: The clean-energy transition is driving unprecedented demand for metals, whose extraction is among the largest industrial sources of carbon emissions. Molten-salt electrolysis offers a path toward carbon-free production of aluminum, iron, titanium, and critical metals. However, corrosive operating conditions and complex, multicomponent feedstocks make experimental development costly and challenging. Here, we introduce a first-principles framework for constructing electrochemical phase diagrams of multicomponent systems to accelerate electrode and electrolyte design and guide the selection of compatible materials and operating conditions for electrochemical cells. We derive a grand potential that generates these diagrams at scale, and supply free energies using first-principles data obtained through a fine-tuned foundation machine-learned interatomic potential. For silicon in molten CaCl$_2$, the construction not only recovers the experimental diagram when using the first-principles framework on a limited set of structures, but also predicts further domains when extended to a larger set of crystal structures. Finally, we demonstrate the predictive power of the approach by screening ten alkali and alkaline-earth halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning. Three thermodynamic criteria identify NaF, where cryolite emerges as the stable aluminum-bearing phase, recovering the chemistry that Hall and Héroult established empirically in 1886. The framework provides a first-principles basis for selecting compatible electrodes, electrolytes, and operating conditions toward low-carbon metal production from complex source materials.

</details>


### [236] [A Parameter-Free Reference Hamiltonian for Tight-Binding Vanadium Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17687)
*Jun-qiang Lu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A transferable DFTB-like tight-binding model for vanadium oxides is constructed from neutral-atom superposition, full PBE exchange-correlation, and PBE-derived screening, matching plane-wave reference energies and charge responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard DFTB (second-order) fails to give transferable vanadium oxide descriptions: its neutral two-centre reference omits electron-volt environmental terms and its Mulliken charge model underestimates the V 3d self-consistent potential shift, with the two errors partially cancelling in band-centroid separation.

Method: Build a tight-binding model whose reference Hamiltonian derives from superposed neutral atoms, the full PBE exchange-correlation potential, and frozen-core orthogonality (no adjustable terms). Orbital-resolved charge response uses three screening factors from PBE linear response plus an unscreened Coulomb range.

Result: At fixed geometry, reference energy differences match plane-wave Harris-Foulkes energies of VO2: +2.6 meV/atom for M1 vs rutile (92 meV/atom gap), and +6.2 / -5.2 meV/atom under 3% compression/expansion. Charge response captures 21/22 fitted and all 8 held-out PBE responses; V 3d shift -3.89 vs -3.92 eV (rutile).

Conclusion: The spinless model's convexity prevents spontaneous symmetry-breaking among equivalent sites; Magnéli-phase site differentiation must instead come from electron-lattice coupling or explicit spin/correlation terms.

Abstract: Density-functional tight binding has yet to yield a transferable description of vanadium oxides; for the standard second-order construction and the tested cells, we demonstrate that two of its approximations deviate by electron volts and offset each other in the band-index centroid separation between the V 3d and O 2p bands: the neutral two-centre reference omits electron-volt environmental terms, whereas the Mulliken charge model assigns the compact V 3d shell less than half of the self-consistent potential shift observed in PBE. Instead, we build a tight-binding model where the reference Hamiltonian derives from superposed neutral atoms, the full PBE exchange-correlation potential and frozen-core orthogonality, without adjustable terms, and where the orbital-resolved charge response employs three screening factors obtained from PBE linear response and an unscreened Coulomb range. Against the primary PAW reference, the reference energy at fixed geometry and the stated numerical settings matches the plane-wave Harris-Foulkes energy differences of VO2, exhibiting signed deviations of +2.6 meV/atom for M1 relative to rutile (an energy difference of 92 meV/atom) and +6.2 and -5.2 meV/atom under 3% volume compression and expansion; meanwhile, the charge response captures 21 of 22 fitted and all 8 held-out matched PBE responses within tolerance, providing the V 3d shell with nearly the same self-consistent shift as PBE (-3.89 versus -3.92 eV in rutile). The convexity of the spinless model at fixed geometry prevents it from spontaneously differentiating symmetry-equivalent sites under the symmetries respected by its implemented operators and sampling, so within this model such differentiation in the Magnéli phases must arise from electron-lattice coupling or an explicit spin or correlation term.

</details>


### [237] [Colossal Spin--Lattice--Charge Coupling in Noncollinear Ferrimagnetic NbMnX$_2$ ($X$ = S, Se, Te) Monolayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17849)
*Peng Wang,Nanshu Liu,Hongkuan Yuan,Xue Jiang,Jijun Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: NbMnX2 (X = S, Se, Te) monolayers are identified as stable noncollinear ferrimagnets with multifunctional magneto-elastic, magnetocaloric, and magnetoresistive properties for 2D spintronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Noncollinear ferrimagnets merge a net magnetic moment with internal spin degrees of freedom, offering opportunities to couple magnetic, mechanical, thermal, and electronic responses. The authors aim to find stable 2D materials that realize these coupled functionalities.

Method: First-principles calculations, symmetry analysis, atomistic spin simulations, and transport modeling are combined to identify and characterize NbMnX2 monolayers.

Result: Stable noncollinear ferrimagnetic ground states with Curie temperatures up to 313 K. Competing exchange, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, and anisotropy yield field-responsive canted states. Magnetization rotation changes NbMnTe2 elastic modulus by up to 36%. Lattice entropy enhances near-room-temperature magnetocaloric response in NbMnS2/NbMnSe2. In NbMnSe2, rotation restores a mirror-protected crossing, closes a ~0.13 eV gap, and gives conductivity-based anisotropic magnetoresistance up to 10,000% at 100 K.

Conclusion: Noncollinear NbMnX2 monolayers constitute a multifunctional platform for 2D spintronics, nanomechanics, and thermal control.

Abstract: Noncollinear ferrimagnets combine a net magnetic moment with internal spin degrees of freedom, creating opportunities to couple magnetic, mechanical, thermal, and electronic responses. Using first-principles calculations, symmetry analysis, atomistic spin simulations, and transport modeling, we identify stable NbMnX$_2$ ($X$ = S, Se, Te) monolayers with noncollinear ferrimagnetic ground states and Curie temperatures up to 313 K. Competing exchange, Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya interactions, and magnetic anisotropy produce field-responsive canted states that couple strongly to the lattice and charge carriers. Magnetization rotation changes the elastic modulus of NbMnTe$_2$ by up to 36\%, while lattice entropy substantially enhances the near-room-temperature magnetocaloric response of NbMnS$_2$ and NbMnSe$_2$. In NbMnSe$_2$, rotation restores a mirror-protected crossing, continuously closes an approximately 0.13 eV gap, and produces a conductivity-based anisotropic magnetoresistance ratio up to 10,000\% at 100 K. The combined results establish noncollinear NbMnX$_2$ monolayers as a multifunctional platform for two-dimensional spintronics, nanomechanics, and thermal control.

</details>


### [238] [Local-resolvent formulation and assessment of linear-scaling Kohn-Sham calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18137)
*Albert Linda*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: LRes-DFT avoids orbital-based Kohn-Sham by using only the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent at a few complex energies, enabling linear-scaling, orbital-free-in-spirit DFT for insulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kohn-Sham DFT is expensive because it requires orbitals spanning the whole system. For insulators, this cost can be avoided since the density and band energy are linear in the resolvent diagonal, so neither orbitals nor a kinetic-energy functional are needed.

Method: Use the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent g(r,z) at M equally spaced complex energies on a circle enclosing occupied levels, offset by half a spacing (no point on the real axis). Summing the resolvent corresponds to occupying each level by 1/(1+u^M), a smooth step function. Gauss-Legendre and Zolotarev quadrature reduce required points for small gaps. In 3D, eigenpairs of small windows around each molecule replace resolvents, forming a divide-and-conquer scheme with a modified occupation function.

Result: For H2 molecular crystals up to 1024 electrons, agreement with Kohn-Sham to 2.5e-7 Ha per electron. On one core, slower than dense diagonalization below ~500 electrons, but 3.5x faster at 1024 electrons. Accuracy degrades for disordered or compressed systems and diffuse basis functions. As accurate as DC with Fermi-Dirac occupations.

Conclusion: LRes-DFT is an accurate, potentially linear-scaling alternative to Kohn-Sham DFT for insulators, with exact forces at fixed electron number when the circle tracks the chemical potential.

Abstract: Kohn-Sham density-functional theory is expensive because it needs orbitals that extend over the whole system. For insulators this can be avoided by using only the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent, $g(\mathbf{r},z)=\langle\mathbf{r}|(z-\hat h)^{-1}|\mathbf{r}\rangle$, at a few complex energies $z$. We call the scheme local-resolvent density-functional theory (LRes-DFT). The density and the band energy are linear in $g$, so neither orbitals nor a kinetic-energy functional are needed. The complex energies are $M$ equally spaced points on a circle that encloses the occupied levels, offset by half a spacing so that no point lies on the real axis. Summing the resolvent over them is equivalent to occupying each level with $1/(1+u^M)$, where $u$ is the level's energy measured from the circle's center in units of its radius. This is a smooth step that is near 1 inside the circle and near 0 outside, and it stays between 0 and 1 for every $M$. If the circle moves with the chemical potential, forces at fixed electron number are exact. Gauss-Legendre and Zolotarev rules need fewer points when the gap is small. In three dimensions we use eigenpairs of small windows around each molecule instead of resolvents. This is divide-and-conquer (DC) with a different occupation function, and it is as accurate as DC with Fermi-Dirac occupations. For H$_2$ molecular crystals of up to 1024 electrons it agrees with Kohn-Sham to $2.5\times10^{-7}$ Ha per electron. On one core it is slower than dense diagonalization below about 500 electrons and 3.5 times faster at 1024 electrons. It loses accuracy for disordered or compressed systems and for diffuse basis functions.

</details>


### [239] [A Topological Spin on Racetrack Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18297)
*Benjamin Zingsem*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube acts as a localized, fast, robust, and detectable magnetic carrier for racetrack memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic racetrack memory needs localized carriers combining rapid motion, perturbation resistance, and a detectable signal within a single continuous magnetic shell.

Method: Analytical energetics and micromagnetic simulations study a 360° domain wall formed by two successive magnetization reversals in a hollow ferromagnetic tube, including perturbation recovery, current-driven transport, curved-track and differential-current tests.

Result: The bound object has finite separation, recovers from perturbations, and transports via current without Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field. Speeds reach hundreds of m/s, approaching ~1 km/s under stronger drive. A seven-bit pattern retained carriers and readout contrast after pulsed transport.

Conclusion: This establishes a basis for fast, locally distinguishable tubular magnetic bits and identifies a continuous switching pathway targetable by tailored excitation.

Abstract: Magnetic racetrack memory requires localized carriers that combine rapid motion, resistance to perturbations and a detectable signal. Here we show how a 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube combines these properties within a single continuous magnetic shell. Two successive magnetization reversals form a bound object whose surrounding domains point in the same direction. Analytical energetics and micromagnetic simulations establish its finite separation, recovery from tested perturbations and current driven transport without microscopic Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field. The texture's winding resists unwinding while radial rotation carries an energy cost, providing a specific control over stability. Relative rotation of the constituent spins accounts quantitatively for additional displacement during current onset. The wall supports motion at hundreds of metres per second, with transient velocities approaching one kilometre per second at stronger drive. A prepared seven-bit pattern retains its carriers and local readout contrast after pulsed transport and recovery. Curved-track and differential-current tests identify radial stiffness and drive uniformity as controls for intact motion. These results establish a physical basis for fast, locally distinguishable tubular magnetic bits and identify a continuous switching pathway that can be targeted by tailored excitation.

</details>


### [240] [A Transferable Machine-Learned Potential Enabling Large-Scale Copper-Carbon Simulations from Equilibrium to Extreme Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18545)
*Feiyu Zhou,Wei Qiu,Yan Chen,Jiahui Liu,Yilun Liu,Shengying Yue,Penghua Ying*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors develop NEP-CuC, a transferable neuroevolution potential for Cu-C systems trained on 2,727 relabelled configurations, validated across elemental Cu, diverse carbon phases, and Cu-C interfaces under equilibrium and non-equilibrium conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cu-C materials and interfaces are central to diamond thermal management, metallization, graphene growth, and carbon coating, but simulating them requires a single potential capturing metallic Cu, diverse carbon bonding, heterogeneous Cu-C interactions, and close atomic encounters.

Method: NEP-CuC is trained on 2,727 uniformly relabelled configurations from a heterogeneous Cu-C pool, with ensemble disagreement of five models as a sensitivity indicator, a ZBL correction for short-range repulsion, and a runtime D3(BJ) dispersion correction.

Result: Benchmarks on held-out and external datasets plus structural, elastic, defect, interfacial, vibrational, and transport tests show broad but property-dependent fidelity. In Cu/diamond interfaces, higher incident Cu energy (1 to 50 eV) lowers mean peak nominal tensile stress at 500 K from 12.35±0.71 to 6.65±0.98 GPa.

Conclusion: NEP-CuC offers an atomistic framework linking processing, interfacial structure, and physical response across Cu-C systems, relevant to diamond thermal-management interfaces, metallization, and non-equilibrium Cu-C processing.

Abstract: Cu-C materials and interfaces are central to diamond thermal management, metallization, graphene growth and carbon-coating technologies, but atomistic simulations across these applications require a potential that describes metallic Cu, diverse carbon bonding, heterogeneous Cu-C interactions and close atomic encounters. Here we develop NEP-CuC, a transferable neuroevolution potential trained on a dataset of 2,727 uniformly relabelled configurations selected from a heterogeneous Cu-C candidate pool. Disagreement within an ensemble of five independently trained models provides an internal indicator of model sensitivity. A Ziegler-Biersack-Littmark (ZBL) correction describes short-range nuclear repulsion, while a runtime D3(BJ) correction supplements dispersion-sensitive calculations. Tests on source-derived held-out and independent external datasets, together with structural, elastic, defect, interfacial, vibrational and finite-temperature transport benchmarks, establish broad but property-dependent physical fidelity across elemental Cu, diverse carbon environments and heterogeneous Cu-C configurations. Large-scale simulations of amorphous-carbon formation and orientation-dependent shock compression of Cu further test transferability under strongly non-equilibrium conditions. For Cu/diamond interfaces, the same potential describes deposition, annealing and tensile response: preparation history leaves persistent differences in interphase structure, while the mean peak nominal tensile stress at 500 K decreases from $12.35\pm0.71$ to $6.65\pm0.98$ GPa as the incident Cu energy during the initial deposition stage increases from 1 to 50 eV. NEP-CuC provides an atomistic framework for linking processing, interfacial structure and physical response across Cu-C systems, with direct relevance to Cu/diamond thermal-management interfaces, diamond metallization and non-equilibrium Cu-C processing.

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [241] [Kernel-Level Constant-Potential Molecular Dynamics in GROMACS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15733)
*Sheng Bi,Mathieu Salanne,Jun Cheng,Guang Feng*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: An open-source constant-potential (variable-charge) implementation in GROMACS that integrates electrode charge calculations into optimized CPU/GPU routines, validated against established codes and demonstrated on charging, voltammetry, impedance, and deformable-electrode simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electrode charge rearrangement governs the behavior of batteries, supercapacitors, and other electrochemical devices. Molecular simulations can capture this by letting each electrode atom's charge adapt to its surroundings, but repeatedly recomputing those charges is computationally expensive.

Method: The authors implement the constant-potential method in GROMACS, embedding the charge-calculation step into the program's optimized CPU and GPU routines. The implementation supports moving/deforming electrodes, finite metallic screening, and electron spillover. Validation is done against established codes on electrode charges, plus charging, voltammetry, and impedance simulations.

Result: Electrode charges agree with established codes. The response to changing electrical conditions is demonstrated via charging, voltammetry, and impedance simulations. For two charged carbon nanotubes, allowing charge to migrate away from facing surfaces roughly halves the repulsive force at contact at intermediate charge and keeps the pair bound, whereas a uniform-charge model separates within the simulated time.

Conclusion: The implementation makes electrode electrical response accessible within GROMACS, enabling studies of interfacial structure, charging dynamics, and electrode motion.

Abstract: How charge rearranges within an electrode shapes the behavior of batteries, supercapacitors, and other electrochemical devices. Molecular simulations can capture this response by allowing the charge on each electrode atom to adjust to its surroundings, but repeatedly calculating these charges adds substantial computational cost. We present an open-source implementation of the constant-potential method in GROMACS that integrates these calculations into the program's optimized CPU and GPU routines. Comparisons with established codes validate the electrode charges, while charging, voltammetry, and impedance simulations demonstrate the response to changing electrical conditions. The implementation also supports electrodes that move or deform and models of finite metallic screening and electron spillover. In a pair of charged carbon nanotubes, allowing charge to move away from the facing surfaces reduces the repulsive force at contact by about half at an intermediate charge and keeps the pair bound where a uniform-charge model opens within the simulated time. These capabilities make the electrical response of electrodes accessible within GROMACS for studies of interfacial structure, charging dynamics, and electrode motion.

</details>


### [242] [Exact Gaussian--Hermite closure of the mapping quantum--classical Liouville equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16864)
*Sahand Nikzat Ali Nassimi,Rony Modak Parai*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The exact Liouville equation for mapping-based quantum-classical dynamics generates no higher Hermite orders and closes on a finite system reducing to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation; PBME, by contrast, retains a spurious raising term and is shown to be less accurate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Liouville equation includes an excess coupling term beyond the Poisson bracket. PBME discards this term, which is a common approximation, but its consequences for the structure of the dynamics and for accuracy were not fully characterized.

Method: Expand the mapping dependence of the density in Hermite functions about the Gaussian factor common to all physical mapping densities. Analyze how the excess coupling term and the back-reaction force act in this basis, and propagate the resulting coefficient equations directly for Tully's dual avoided crossing model.

Result: The excess coupling term acts as a raising operator and exactly cancels the raising contribution from the back-reaction force, so the exact dynamics closes on a finite, sparse system that reduces to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation in the physical sector. PBME instead retains the force's raising contribution, modifying the back reaction and continuously generating higher Hermite orders. Numerical propagation confirms the finite closure and agreement with the subsystem equation, and PBME is less accurate than the quantum-classical description for populations, coherences, and momentum distributions.

Conclusion: The exact mapping-based dynamics is finitely closed and equivalent to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation, whereas PBME introduces systematic errors. This identifies the neglected excess coupling term as the source of PBME's inaccuracy and favors the quantum-classical description.

Abstract: Liouville equation contains an excess coupling term in addition to the Poisson bracket. Neglecting this term yields the Poisson bracket mapping equation (PBME). We expand the mapping dependence of the density in Hermite functions about the Gaussian factor common to all physical mapping densities. In this basis, the excess coupling term acts purely as a raising operator and exactly cancels the raising contribution generated by the back--reaction force. Consequently, the exact dynamics does not generate higher Hermite orders and closes on a finite, sparse system of equations for the coefficient fields. Within the physical sector, these equations reduce to the subsystem quantum--classical Liouville equation. In contrast, the PBME retains the raising contribution of the force, modifying the back reaction within the physical sector while continuously generating higher Hermite orders. For Tully's dual avoided crossing model, direct propagation of the coefficient equations confirms this finite closure and agrees with independent solutions of the subsystem equation. Comparison with converged full quantum dynamics further shows that, across all regimes examined, the PBME is less accurate than the quantum--classical description for populations, coherences, and momentum distributions.

</details>


### [243] [All-electron machine-learned orbital-free density functional for periodic CHNO systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17975)
*Marc K. Ickler,Andreas Hermann,Fred A. Hamprecht*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: STRUCTURES-P is a machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT functional for periodic CHNO systems, enabling variational density optimization that recovers Kohn-Sham ground states from atomic-density superpositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: All-electron orbital-free DFT could offer efficient variational density optimization for solids, but achieving accurate kinetic and exchange-correlation energies without orbitals remains challenging.

Method: Analytic electrostatics in an atom-centered Gaussian basis combined with an equivariant network that learns kinetic and exchange-correlation energy from energies and gradients of non-ground-state densities; functional minimized from a superposition of atomic densities.

Result: On held-out crystals, density error drops from 6.7% to 0.4% of electron count and energy error reaches 0.25 mHa/atom (half that of a same-architecture interatomic potential). One functional trains for both molecules and periodic systems, generalizes to larger cells, preserves relative-energy rankings, and reproduces convex-hull vertices on the H-N slice.

Conclusion: Machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT is viable for variational density optimization and energy evaluation in fixed-geometry solids.

Abstract: We introduce STRUCTURES-P, a machine-learned all-electron orbital-free density functional for periodic CHNO systems. Electrostatics are evaluated analytically in an atom-centered Gaussian basis, while an equivariant network learns the kinetic and exchange-correlation energy from energies and gradients of non-ground-state densities. Minimizing the functional from a superposition of atomic densities recovers the Kohn-Sham ground state: on held-out crystals, the density error falls from 6.7% to 0.4% of the electron count and the energy error reaches 0.25 mHa per atom, half that of a same-architecture interatomic potential. A single functional can be trained for both isolated molecules and periodic systems, and the model generalizes to larger unit cells. It largely preserves relative-energy rankings and reproduces convex-hull vertices on the tested H-N composition slice. Together, these results demonstrate the viability of machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT for variational density optimization and energy evaluation in fixed-geometry solids.

</details>


### [244] [Can Machine Learning Potentials Be More Accurate Than the Methods They Are Trained On?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18027)
*Pavlo O. Dral,Yi-Fan Hou,Yuxinxin Chen,Hassan Nawaz,Mateusz Witkowski,Szymon Śmiga,Ireneusz Grabowski*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This Perspective argues that machine learning potentials (MLPs) can exceed the accuracy of their training methods non-empirically by generalizing across a third space—quantum-chemical methods—enabling "method-transferable" MLPs that predict at unseen methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLPs generalize across configurational and chemical compound spaces, but their accuracy is commonly believed limited by their training methods. Improvements beyond this limit have so far only been achieved empirically by refitting to experimental observables. The authors seek a non-empirical route to higher accuracy.

Method: Proposes routes toward method-transferable MLPs that generalize across the space of quantum-chemical methods, predicting at unseen methods. Working implementations are demonstrated in both density-functional theory and wavefunction-based theories.

Result: The working implementations show that method-transferable MLPs can be more accurate than any of their training methods. Aiming beyond training-method accuracy could also make universal MLPs more accurate than single-method models, even when the transferable MLPs remain less accurate than the underlying QM methods.

Conclusion: Method-transferable MLPs will shape the construction of training datasets and the evaluation of models, pushing the accuracy limits of chemical simulations.

Abstract: Although machine learning potentials (MLPs) generalize across configurational and chemical compound spaces, their accuracy is commonly believed to be limited by their training methods, with improvements beyond this limit achieved so far only by refitting to experimental observables. In this Perspective, we argue that MLPs can also be made more accurate than their training methods non-empirically by generalizing across a third space, that of quantum-chemical methods. We propose routes to method-transferable MLPs that predict at unseen methods, with working implementations in both density-functional and wavefunction-based theories showing that they can be more accurate than any of their training methods. Aiming beyond the accuracy of the training methods could also make universal MLPs more accurate than single-method models, even when the method-transferable MLPs remain less accurate than those QM methods. Method-transferable MLPs will shape the construction of training datasets and the evaluation of models, pushing the accuracy limits of chemical simulations.

</details>


<div id='math.DG'></div>

# math.DG [[Back]](#toc)

### [245] [Volume growth and parabolicity for non-symmetric operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14375)
*Leandro F. Pessoa,Stefano Pigola,Alberto G. Setti*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: This paper extends the geometric analysis of weighted structures from gradient drifted Laplace operators to general (non-gradient) drifted Laplace operators by using invariant measures as the framework, establishing a parabolicity theory connected to volume growth and yielding new volume finiteness results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental interplay between a weighted volume measure and its associated weighted Laplace operator works well for gradient vector fields, but beyond the gradient setting the theory is much less developed because this natural measure-operator correspondence is lost.

Method: The authors identify invariant measures as the appropriate framework for extending the theory to general drifted Laplace operators, and apply this to systematically treat parabolicity in the non-gradient setting.

Result: The approach provides a systematic treatment of parabolicity in the non-gradient setting, reveals its connection with volume growth, and produces new volume finiteness results for non-gradient geometric structures.

Conclusion: Invariant measures serve as a viable framework for a general theory of drifted Laplace operators, extending geometric analysis beyond the gradient case.

Abstract: The interplay between a weighted volume measure and its naturally associated weighted Laplace operator is fundamental to the geometric analysis of weighted structures governed by gradient vector fields. Beyond the gradient setting, however, the theory has remained considerably less developed, largely because this natural correspondence is lost. In this paper, we identify invariant measures as the appropriate framework for extending the theory to general drifted Laplace operators. This approach yields a systematic treatment of parabolicity in the non-gradient setting, reveals its connection with volume growth, and leads to new volume finiteness results for non-gradient geometric structures.

</details>


### [246] [Global $C^{2,α}$ estimate for complex Monge--Ampère equations on compact Kähler manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14870)
*Haozhao Li,Bing Wang,Kai Zheng*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper establishes a global $C^{2,\alpha}$ a priori estimate for the complex Monge--Ampère equation on compact Kähler manifolds when the right-hand side is positive and Hölder continuous.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A priori estimates are central to solving the complex Monge--Ampère equation. Prior regularity results often required stronger smoothness on the right-hand side; this work extends regularity to the optimal $C^\alpha$ (Hölder) setting.

Method: PDE a priori estimate techniques for the complex Monge--Ampère equation on compact Kähler manifolds, treating a positive $C^\alpha$ right-hand side.

Result: A global $C^{2,\alpha}$ estimate is proven for solutions of the equation with positive $C^\alpha$ right-hand side.

Conclusion: The result provides optimal global regularity (up to $C^{2,\alpha}$) for the complex Monge--Ampère equation under Hölder-continuous positive data, with applications to Kähler geometry.

Abstract: In this paper, we prove a global $C^{2,α}$ estimate for the complex Monge--Ampère equation with a positive $C^α$ right-hand side on a compact Kähler manifold.

</details>


### [247] [Remarks on the disjoint minimal graphs conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15736)
*Allen Weitsman*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: This paper studies the structure of three disjoint nontrivial minimal graphs in R^3 with zero boundary values, exploring properties such graphs would necessarily possess.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It is known that there can be at most three disjoint nontrivial minimal graphs in R^3 with 0 boundary values, and it is conjectured that at most two exist. The paper investigates what properties three such graphs would have if three existed, toward resolving the conjecture.

Method: Analytical/structural investigation of the geometric and analytic properties that three disjoint nontrivial minimal graphs with zero boundary values in R^3 would need to satisfy.

Result: Properties that the graphs would necessarily have, assuming three such disjoint nontrivial minimal graphs exist, are explored.

Conclusion: The paper characterizes constraints on the hypothetical configuration of three disjoint minimal graphs, contributing toward the conjecture that at most two such graphs can exist.

Abstract: It is known that there can be at most 3 disjoint nontrivial minimal graphs in R^3 with 0 boundary values, and conjectured that there can be at most 2. We explore properties that the graphs would have if there were 3.

</details>


### [248] [Auxiliary Hessian Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15866)
*Johannes G. Scheffler*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proposes k-Hessian equations as auxiliary equations for degenerating Kähler classes toward nef classes of numerical dimension k, and establishes uniform mean-value and a priori estimates as proof-of-concept.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Degenerations of Kähler classes to nef classes of numerical dimension k are difficult to study directly; suitable auxiliary equations are needed to control the degeneration behavior uniformly.

Method: k-Hessian equations are introduced as auxiliary equations for degenerations of Kähler classes to nef classes of numerical dimension k.

Result: A uniform mean-value inequality and uniform à priori estimates for Hessian equations are obtained.

Conclusion: k-Hessian equations provide a viable framework for analyzing degenerations to nef classes, as demonstrated by these proof-of-concept uniform estimates.

Abstract: We propose k-Hessian equations as auxiliary equations for degenera- tions of Kähler classes to nef classes of numerical dimension k. As proof-of-concept results, we provide a uniform mean-value inequality and uniform à priori estimates for Hessian equa- tions.

</details>


### [249] [Stability of Diophantine Minimal Foliations in Arbitrary Codimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15984)
*Hoan Nguyen*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The authors generalize Moser's stability theorem for minimal hypersurface foliations from hypersurfaces to arbitrary codimension, showing that a Diophantine m-plane foliation of the torus persists under small periodic metric perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Moser's stability theorem concerns minimal hypersurface foliations (codimension one). This paper extends the result to arbitrary codimension, addressing the case of m-dimensional minimal submanifold foliations on the torus.

Method: The proof employs Hamilton's formulation of the Nash-Moser implicit function theorem. The setting is the standard torus with an m-plane P satisfying a Diophantine condition relative to the integer lattice.

Result: Under every sufficiently small smooth periodic perturbation of the Euclidean metric, the affine foliation induced by P persists as a smoothly conjugate foliation by minimal m-dimensional submanifolds. The normalized conjugating displacement depends smoothly and tamely on the metric and is locally unique.

Conclusion: Moser's stability theorem extends to arbitrary codimension for Diophantine plane foliations of the torus, with smooth tame dependence and local uniqueness of the conjugating displacement.

Abstract: We generalize Moser's stability theorem for minimal hypersurface foliations to arbitrary codimension. Let $\mathbb{T}^N=\mathbb{R}^N/\mathbb{Z}^N$ be the standard torus, and let $P\subset\mathbb{R}^N$ be an $m$-plane satisfying a Diophantine condition relative to $\mathbb{Z}^N$. We prove that, under every sufficiently small smooth $\mathbb{Z}^N$-periodic perturbation of the Euclidean metric, the affine foliation of $\mathbb{T}^N$ induced by $P$ persists as a smoothly conjugate foliation by minimal $m$-dimensional submanifolds. The normalized conjugating displacement depends smoothly and tamely on the metric and is locally unique. The proof uses Hamilton's formulation of the Nash-Moser implicit function theorem.

</details>


### [250] [On the Weyl tube formula for nonlocal perimeters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16461)
*Serena Dipierro,Jack Thompson,Enrico Valdinoci*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the asymptotic behavior of the nonlocal (fractional) perimeter of tubular neighborhoods. It proves that for a closed C^{1,1} submanifold, a rescaled nonlocal perimeter of its tube converges to an explicit constant (the fractional perimeter of the unit ball in the codimension) times the Hausdorff measure of the submanifold, and derives an exact tube formula for finite point sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Weyl-type tube formulas are well understood for the classical perimeter, but the behavior of nonlocal perimeters of tubular neighborhoods is less clear. The authors aim to extend tube-formula understanding to the nonlocal setting and explore how geometry (position of points) enters the expansion.

Method: The authors study the nonlocal perimeter of tubular neighborhoods of closed C^{1,1} submanifolds and of finite point sets, using rescaling/limiting arguments and deriving exact tube formulas via generalized hypergeometric functions, plus a point-set expansion for general analytic kernels.

Result: For a closed C^{1,1} submanifold, the rescaled nonlocal perimeter of its tube converges to an explicit constant times the Hausdorff measure of the submanifold, where the constant equals the fractional perimeter of the unit ball in the codimension. For finite point sets, an exact tube formula in terms of a generalized hypergeometric function is obtained. Unlike the classical Weyl formula, this expansion has infinitely many terms, contains both noninteger and integer powers of the tubular radius, and its coefficients depend on the relative positions of the points, not only on intrinsic geometry. A point-set expansion for general analytic kernels is also established.

Conclusion: The nonlocal perimeter of tubes exhibits a rich asymptotic structure: a clean geometric limit for smooth submanifolds, but an infinite, position-dependent expansion for point sets, differing fundamentally from the classical Weyl formula. This reveals that nonlocal perimeters encode extrinsic/positional information beyond intrinsic geometry.

Abstract: We study the asymptotic behaviour of the nonlocal perimeter of tubular neighbourhoods. For a closed $C^{1,1}$ submanifold of Euclidean space, we show that a suitable rescaling of the nonlocal perimeter of its tube converges to an explicit constant times the Hausdorff measure of the submanifold. The constant is the fractional perimeter of the unit ball in the codimension of the submanifold. For finite point sets, we obtain an exact tube formula in terms of a generalised hypergeometric function. In contrast with the classical Weyl formula, this expansion has infinitely many terms, contains both noninteger and integer powers of the tubular radius, and has coefficients that depend on the relative positions of the points rather than only on their intrinsic geometry. We also establish a point-set expansion for general analytic kernels.

</details>


### [251] [T^2-invariant Einstein metrics on closed 4-manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18219)
*Kyle Broder,Ramiro A. Lafuente*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: Constructs T²-invariant positive-Ricci Einstein metrics on connected sums #_m(S²×S²), giving infinitely many non-Hermitian Einstein 4-manifolds, and proves uniqueness on S⁴, CP², S²×S², and CP²#CP̄².


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positive-Ricci Einstein metrics on closed 4-manifolds are scarce and poorly understood; most known examples are Hermitian (Kähler or nearly Kähler). Finding non-Hermitian examples, especially infinite families, is a key open problem.

Method: Uses T²-symmetry (torus-invariant) ansatz to reduce the Einstein equation, constructing T²-invariant Einstein metrics on the connected sums #_m(S²×S²).

Result: Obtains T²-invariant Einstein metrics with λ>0 on #_m(S²×S²) for all m>1, yielding an infinite family of non-Hermitian Einstein metrics on closed 4-manifolds; also proves uniqueness of such metrics on S⁴, CP², S²×S², and CP²#CP̄².

Conclusion: Provides new non-Hermitian positive Einstein 4-manifolds and a uniqueness classification for the T²-invariant solutions on several fundamental 4-manifolds.

Abstract: We construct $T^2$-invariant Einstein metrics with positive Ricci curvature on #_m(S^2 x S^2) for every integer $m>1$. These provide infinite family of Einstein metrics on closed $4$-manifolds with $λ>0$, which are in addition not Hermitian. We further establish the uniqueness of the $T^2$-invariant Einstein metrics on $S^4$, CP^2, S^2 x S^2, and CP^2#\bar{CP}^2.

</details>


### [252] [The anisotropic Weyl law and Grassmannian equidistribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18599)
*Antonio De Rosa,Aria Halavati*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proves a Weyl law for the codimension-one anisotropic volume spectrum and uses it to establish equidistribution in the full Grassmannian bundle, constructing stationary hypersurfaces for anisotropies converging to area.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve equidistribution on the Grassmannian bundle while keeping the ambient metric fixed, an open problem related to constructing stationary hypersurfaces whose area measures distribute uniformly.

Method: Establish a Weyl law for the codimension-one anisotropic volume spectrum; construct smooth embedded hypersurfaces stationary for anisotropies that smoothly converge to the area functional.

Result: A Weyl law for the anisotropic volume spectrum is proven. On every closed connected smooth manifold of dimension 3–6, the constructed hypersurfaces' cumulative area measures, lifted to the Grassmannian bundle, converge to the uniform measure.

Conclusion: The Weyl law yields equidistribution in the full Grassmannian bundle with the ambient metric fixed, valid for closed connected smooth manifolds in dimensions three through six.

Abstract: We establish a Weyl law for the codimension-one anisotropic volume spectrum on compact Riemannian manifolds. We then use this law to obtain equidistribution in the full Grassmannian bundle while keeping the ambient metric fixed. More precisely, on every closed connected smooth Riemannian manifold of dimension three through six, we construct smooth, embedded hypersurfaces stationary for anisotropies smoothly converging to area. The cumulative sum of their area measures, when lifted to the Grassmannian bundle, converges to the uniform measure.

</details>


### [253] [A three-block higher-rank deformed Hermitian--Yang--Mills correspondence on $\mathbb P^1\times\mathbb P^1$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18788)
*Yuqi Li,Hao-Yu Sun*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper classifies smooth strictly calibrated solutions of an invariant rank-four deformed Hermitian–Yang–Mills equation over P^1, showing solutions exist exactly when A is invertible and z^T A^T B z ≢ 0, and matches this condition with stability in an equivariant coherent category.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when the deformed Hermitian–Yang–Mills equation on a specific rank-four chain of line bundles admits smooth strictly calibrated solutions, and to connect this analytic existence condition to stability in an equivariant coherent category.

Method: The paper analyzes the ordered, noncommuting middle equation directly. It constructs positive-parameter solutions, then combines source budgets with moving spectral tests to rule out unbounded metric values, retaining divisor mass on the rank-one lower boundary. Global value, gradient, and symbol estimates yield the smooth endpoint.

Result: Smooth strictly calibrated reduced solutions exist exactly when A is invertible and z^T A^T B z is not identically zero. This same condition characterizes stability, semistability, and polystability in the specified equivariant coherent category. The classification covers all eleven nonempty coefficient strata and both coincident and distinct divisor points on the rank-one lower boundary.

Conclusion: Existence of smooth strictly calibrated solutions is equivalent to the stated invertibility and nonvanishing condition, which also governs stability in the equivariant coherent category. The authors do not assert equivalence with full coherent or derived-category stability, uniqueness, or uniformity across changing chains.

Abstract: We study the invariant rank-four deformed Hermitian--Yang--Mills equation associated with the chain $\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathcal{O}(2)^{\oplus2}\to\mathcal{O}(3)$ over $\mathbb P^1$, with vertical weights $(4,2,0)$ and a fixed product Kähler class and phase. For arbitrary linear arrows $ψ=Bz$ and $φ=(Az)^T$, smooth strictly calibrated reduced solutions exist exactly when $A$ is invertible and $z^TA^TBz$ is not identically zero. The same condition characterizes stability, semistability and polystability in the specified equivariant coherent category. The classification includes all eleven nonempty coefficient strata and both coincident and distinct divisor points on the rank-one lower boundary. The analytic proof retains the ordered, noncommuting middle equation. After constructing positive-parameter solutions, it rules out unbounded metric values by combining the source budgets with moving spectral tests; on the rank-one lower boundary, the divisor mass must also be retained. Global value, gradient and symbol estimates then give a smooth strictly calibrated endpoint on the original chain. No equivalence with full coherent or derived-category stability, uniqueness, or estimate uniform across changing chains is asserted.

</details>


### [254] [Exterior free-boundary minimal surfaces of every topological type with at least two ends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18832)
*Igor Kozyrev*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proves existence of connected, orientable, properly embedded exterior free-boundary minimal surfaces in R^3 minus the unit ball for every combination of ℓ≥2 ends, b≥1 boundary components, and genus γ≥0, with finite total curvature and parallel catenoidal ends, via two gluing constructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Such embedded exterior free-boundary minimal surfaces were previously constrained: surfaces with one end are rotational annuli (Mazet–Mendes), so all other topological types were unknown. The paper aims to realize every remaining topological type, including small ones, filling the existence gap.

Method: Two gluing constructions. For ℓ≥3: stack ℓ sheets over a stable catenoidal annulus meeting the sphere along a circle of radius 4/5, joining adjacent sheets by half-catenoidal bridges on the sphere; sheets are small normal graphs whose displacements contain different multiples of the Jacobi field of the annuli family. For ℓ=2: double a catenoidal annulus near the critical angle π/4, where a bounded rotationally invariant Jacobi field appears, coupling the distance to the critical annulus with bridge size.

Result: For all integers ℓ≥2, b≥1, γ≥0 there exists a connected, orientable, properly embedded exterior free-boundary minimal surface with ℓ ends, b boundary components and genus γ, of finite total curvature and parallel catenoidal ends of multiplicity one. Combined with Mazet–Mendes, the topological types are exactly the annulus with one end and all types with at least two ends.

Conclusion: The logarithmic growth of the ends absorbs the sheet-balancing that in interior gluing constructions would require many bridges. A fixed number of bridges therefore suffices, reaching every topological type, including the small ones previously inaccessible.

Abstract: An exterior free-boundary minimal surface is a properly immersed minimal surface in the complement of the open unit ball of $\mathbb{R}^3$ whose boundary lies on the unit sphere and meets it orthogonally. We prove that for all integers $\ell\geq2$, $b\geq1$ and $γ\geq0$ there is a connected, orientable, properly embedded exterior free-boundary minimal surface with $\ell$ ends, $b$ boundary components and genus $γ$. Its total curvature is finite, and its ends are parallel catenoidal ends of multiplicity one. By the theorem of Mazet and Mendes, a surface of this kind with one end is a rotational annulus. Hence the topological types of such embedded surfaces are exactly the annulus with one end and all types with at least two ends.
  There are two gluing constructions. For $\ell\geq3$ we stack $\ell$ sheets over a stable catenoidal annulus that meets the sphere along a circle of radius $4/5$, and we join adjacent sheets by half-catenoidal bridges sitting on the sphere. The sheets are small normal graphs over this annulus whose displacements contain different multiples of the Jacobi field of the family of such annuli, so their boundary circles and the logarithmic growth of their ends differ slightly. For $\ell=2$ we double a catenoidal annulus near the critical angle $π/4$, where it acquires a bounded rotationally invariant Jacobi field; the distance to the critical annulus is coupled to the size of the bridges. In gluing constructions inside the unit ball that double or stack a compact surface, the bridges themselves must balance the sheets, which forces many bridges. Here the logarithmic growth of the ends absorbs this balancing. A fixed number of bridges therefore suffices, and every topological type is reached, including the small ones.

</details>


<div id='physics.optics'></div>

# physics.optics [[Back]](#toc)

### [255] [Fast local field calculation in cascaded coupled systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14314)
*Isam Ben Soltane,Romain Asso,Matthieu Davy*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A hybrid S-matrix/Green's function method computes fields in a modified sub-region of cascaded wave systems by characterizing surrounding subsystems once, enabling rapid field recomputation and large speed-ups for inverse design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing the field in a sub-unit of cascaded systems is a recurring wave-physics problem central to gradient-based inverse design, where only part of the system changes while the rest stays fixed. Existing approaches either resimulate the whole cascade at every change or need detailed characterization of each surrounding subsystem, both impractical for many design iterations.

Method: A hybrid method combining the S-matrix approach (propagating fields through cascaded systems) with the Green's function method (giving the field from a source within a characterized system). Surrounding subsystems are characterized only once, after which the local field is recomputed rapidly for any new configuration.

Result: Validated on a disordered lossy waveguide with excellent agreement versus finite-element simulations. On a disordered multimode cavity, achieves a fifty-fold speed-up over full-wave simulation and reduces a wideband focusing calculation from eleven hours to ten minutes.

Conclusion: The method enables fast, iterative field calculations in complex systems coupled to their environment, with direct applications to inverse design and wideband wavefront shaping.

Abstract: Computing the field within a sub-unit of cascaded systems is a recurring problem in wave physics. It is central to gradient-based inverse design, in situations where only a region of the system is modified while the rest remains fixed. Standard approaches either resimulate the entire cascade at every change, or require a detailed characterization of every surrounding sub-system. Both become impractical when many design iterations are needed. Here, we introduce a hybrid method combining the $S$-matrix approach, which propagates fields through cascaded systems, with the Green's function method, which gives the field generated by a source within a characterized system. In this approach, the sub-systems surrounding the region of interest need only be characterized once, after which the local field can be recomputed rapidly for any new configuration. We validate the method on a disordered lossy waveguide, finding excellent agreement with finite-element simulations. We also demonstrate its efficiency on a disordered multimode cavity, where it achieves a fifty-fold speed-up over full-wave simulation and reduces a wideband focusing calculation from eleven hours to ten minutes. This approach opens the way to fast, iterative field calculations in complex systems coupled to their environment, with direct applications to inverse design and wideband wavefront-shaping.

</details>


### [256] [Dual deconvolution multifocal fluorescence response matrix microscopy for aberration-free high-throughput super-resolution imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15264)
*Sumin Lim,Sanghyeon Park,Tran Thinh Le,Jin Hee Hong,Seokchan Yoon,Wonshik Choi*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This paper introduces high-throughput aberration-free fluorescence response matrix imaging using adaptive multifocal illumination, where inter-focus spacing is tuned to specimen-dependent crosstalk, accelerating acquisition by ~3 orders of magnitude while preserving ~100 nm lateral resolution and correcting specimen-dependent aberrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fluorescence response matrix imaging enables separate computational correction of excitation and emission aberrations via dual deconvolution, but single-focus acquisition requires a huge number of camera frames, limiting practical use. Multifocal excitation can parallelize acquisition, yet denser foci increase inter-focus crosstalk, biasing estimated optical transfer functions and degrading aberration correction.

Method: A programmable digital micromirror device generates multifocal excitation lattices with inter-focus spacing adaptively adjusted according to specimen-dependent crosstalk. The detector-resolved fluorescence responses form a two-coordinate response matrix; dual deconvolution separately estimates and corrects excitation and emission optical transfer functions, and the corrected matrix is combined in the spatial-frequency domain to reconstruct the aberration-free image.

Result: The approach cuts the required camera frames by ~3 orders of magnitude, allowing response matrices with ~10^6 illumination positions to be acquired in seconds to minutes. It preserves dual-deconvolution aberration correction, achieves ~100 nm estimated lateral resolution, and enables high-resolution, depth-resolved volumetric imaging in heterogeneous mouse brain and kidney tissue.

Conclusion: Adaptive multifocal illumination provides a practical route to high-throughput, aberration-free fluorescence response matrix imaging, balancing acquisition speed and crosstalk for specimen-dependent aberration correction.

Abstract: Fluorescence response matrix imaging enables separate computational correction of excitation and emission aberrations through dual deconvolution, but single-focus acquisition requires a large number of camera frames, limiting its practical use. Multifocal excitation can accelerate acquisition by recording many illumination positions in parallel, but denser foci also increase inter-focus crosstalk, which can bias the estimated excitation and emission optical transfer functions and degrade aberration correction. Here, we introduce high-throughput aberration-free fluorescence response matrix imaging with adaptive multifocal illumination, in which the inter-focus spacing of the multifocal lattice is adjusted according to specimen-dependent crosstalk. A programmable digital micromirror device generates multifocal excitation lattices, and the resulting detector-resolved fluorescence responses are assembled into a two-coordinate fluorescence response matrix. Dual deconvolution separately estimates and corrects the excitation and emission optical transfer functions, and the aberration-corrected matrix is then combined in the spatial-frequency domain to reconstruct the aberration-free image. This strategy reduces the number of required camera frames by approximately three orders of magnitude, allowing response matrices with $\sim 10^6$ illumination positions to be acquired within seconds to a few minutes. The accelerated acquisition preserves the aberration-correction capability of dual deconvolution and achieves an estimated lateral resolution of $\sim$100 nm. Applied to heterogeneous mouse brain and kidney tissue, the method enables high-resolution, depth-resolved volumetric imaging while correcting specimen-dependent aberrations. Together, these results establish adaptive multifocal illumination as a practical route to high-throughput aberration-free fluorescence response matrix imaging.

</details>


<div id='nlin.SI'></div>

# nlin.SI [[Back]](#toc)

### [257] [Long-time asymptotics for the focusing coupled Kundu-Eckhaus equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16564)
*Tianwei Qiu,Zhen Wang*

Main category: nlin.SI

TL;DR: Long-time asymptotics of the focusing coupled Kundu-Eckhaus equation via Riemann-Hilbert analysis and gauge equivalence to CNLS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The focusing CKE equation extends CNLS with quintic and derivative nonlinearities, but its long-time behavior was not well understood. This work aims to derive a uniform asymptotic formula for its dispersive radiation.

Method: Study the Cauchy problem with Schwartz initial data assuming no discrete spectrum or spectral singularities. Use gauge equivalence between CKE and CNLS, analyze the 3x3 matrix Riemann-Hilbert problem via Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent, and establish joint error estimates for the CNLS solution and gauge phase.

Result: A uniform long-time asymptotic formula for the dispersive radiation of the CKE equation, with an O(t^{-1} log t) remainder in bounded velocity cones.

Conclusion: The gauge equivalence and Riemann-Hilbert approach yield uniform long-time asymptotics for the focusing CKE equation, extending known CNLS results with quantified error.

Abstract: The focusing coupled Kundu-Eckhaus (CKE) equation extends the coupled nonlinear Schrödinger (CNLS) equation by adding quintic and derivative nonlinearities. We study the Cauchy problem for the focusing CKE equation with Schwartz initial data under the assumption of no discrete spectrum and no spectral singularities on the real axis. Based on the gauge equivalence between the focusing CKE and CNLS equations, we analyze the associated $3\times3$ matrix Riemann-Hilbert problem by the Deift-Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method. By establishing joint error estimates for the CNLS solution and the gauge phase, we obtain a uniform long-time asymptotic formula for the dispersive radiation of the CKE equation with an $O(t^{-1}\log t)$ remainder in bounded velocity cones.

</details>


<div id='math.MG'></div>

# math.MG [[Back]](#toc)

### [258] [The rhombic dodecahedral conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13975)
*Annalisa Cesaroni,Matteo Novaga*

Main category: math.MG

TL;DR: Every 3D parallelohedron P satisfies H²(∂P) ≥ 12√2 r(P)², with equality iff P is a regular rhombic dodecahedron.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parallelohedra (space-filling polyhedra) are fundamental in discrete geometry and crystallography. A natural question is to establish sharp isoperimetric-type inequalities relating surface area to inradius, and to characterize the extremal shape. The regular rhombic dodecahedron is the conjectured optimal body, motivating a rigorous proof of the inequality.

Method: The proof is geometric/analytic: it considers an arbitrary three-dimensional parallelohedron, employs properties of parallelohedra (face structure, inradius) and the induced area measure on the boundary, and derives the lower bound via an isoperimetric-type argument, verifying equality conditions.

Result: For every 3D parallelohedron P, H²(∂P) ≥ 12√2 r(P)², where r(P) is the inradius. Equality holds if and only if P is similar to the regular rhombic dodecahedron.

Conclusion: The regular rhombic dodecahedron is the unique (up to similarity) minimizer of boundary area relative to inradius among 3D parallelohedra, confirming the sharp isoperimetric-type inequality.

Abstract: We prove that every three-dimensional parallelohedron $P$ satisfies \[ \mathcal H^2(\partial P)\ge 12\sqrt2\,r(P)^2, \] where $r(P)$ is the inradius of $P$, with equality if and only if $P$ is similar to the regular rhombic dodecahedron.

</details>


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [259] [A Unified Framework for Randomized Row-Action Methods for Structured Data Fitting and Constraint Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14764)
*Valentin Leplat,Zeyu Dong,Junfeng Yin*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: A unified study of randomized row-action methods for linear-observation problems with convex constraints and non-Euclidean geometry, covering primal Bregman-proximal and dual Bregman-Kaczmarz branches with deterministic and stochastic convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many problems (constrained least squares, nonnegative/simplex models, regularized fitting, equality-constrained Bregman selection) share linear observations, convex constraints, and non-Euclidean geometry, but lacked a unified random row-action treatment with rigorous convergence theory.

Method: A unified formulation where the data-fitting term lives in observation space while the feasible set and regularizer encode variable-space structure. Two branches: primal Bregman-proximal row steps (soft data-fitting) and dual Bregman-Kaczmarz steps (hard equality constraints). A common one-step inequality is derived, followed by deterministic/stochastic convergence analysis under relative smoothness and residual-error bounds.

Result: For constrained least squares, normalized row oracle moment identities give an expected ergodic bound (inconsistent case), O(1/K) bound (interpolating case), and linear convergence of iterates under a residual error bound. For equality-constrained selectors, linear convergence in expected Bregman distance is proven under a residual-to-Bregman error bound, with local constants derived for nonnegative quadratic and simplex entropy geometries.

Conclusion: Numerical experiments confirm the noisy stepsize trade-off and local entropy selector theory, and show entropic row-action updates recover interpretable factors in simplex-structured nonnegative matrix factorization.

Abstract: We study randomized row-action methods for problems with linear observations, convex constraints, and non-Euclidean geometry. Our starting point is a unified formulation in which the data-fitting term acts in observation space, while the feasible set and the regularizer encode structure in variable space. It covers constrained least squares, nonnegative and simplex-constrained models, regularized data fitting, and equality-constrained Bregman selection problems. The structure of these problems leads naturally to two algorithmic branches. The first uses primal Bregman-proximal row steps for soft data-fitting problems. The second uses dual Bregman--Kaczmarz steps for problems with hard equality constraints. We first derive a common one-step inequality. We then give deterministic and stochastic convergence results for the soft branch under relative smoothness. For constrained least squares, the normalized row oracle has exact first- and second-moment identities. They yield an expected ergodic bound in the inconsistent case, an $O(1/K)$ bound in the interpolating case, and linear convergence of the actual iterates under a residual error bound. For equality-constrained selectors, we prove linear convergence in expected Bregman distance under a residual-to-Bregman error bound. We recall global sufficient conditions for this bound and derive local constants for the nonnegative quadratic geometry and the entropy geometry on the simplex. Numerical experiments illustrate the noisy stepsize trade-off and the local entropy selector theory, and show that entropic row-action updates can recover interpretable factors in a simplex-structured nonnegative matrix factorization problem.

</details>


### [260] [Multilevel Monte Carlo for Zeroth-Order Stochastic Bilevel Optimization with Inexact Lower Level Solves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15047)
*Neil K. Chada,Mohammad S. Salehi,Mathias Staudigl,Simon Weissmann*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper proposes a multilevel Monte Carlo derivative-free optimization method for bilevel learning in variational regularization, achieving data-efficient parameter selection with a universal end-to-end complexity analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Variational regularization methods depend heavily on parameters needing fine-tuning, which is computationally expensive. Data-driven learning of parameters leads to hard-to-solve bilevel optimization problems. The aim is to develop a data-efficient solution strategy.

Method: A multilevel Monte Carlo derivative-free optimization method that couples Gaussian smoothing of the upper-level objective with a hierarchy of lower-level solution accuracies. It is agnostic to how the lower-level problem is solved.

Result: Explicit oracle complexity and computational cost statements for approximately solving the bilevel learning problem, parameterized by the lower-level solution method.

Conclusion: Provides a universal end-to-end complexity analysis of bilevel learning valid for the most important optimization routines in inverse problems, rather than for a specific method.

Abstract: Variational regularization techniques are dominant in the field of mathematical imaging. The expressiveness of these methods heavily depends on the adopted regularization formulation which usually involves a number of parameters which have to be fine-tuned by the user. To circumvent the heavy computational costs associated with this parameter tuning, data-driven learning strategies have turned out to be a useful strategy. Selecting parameters by learning from data gives rise to a hierarchical (bilevel) optimization problem, which is in general computationally hard to solve. Our aim in this paper is to develop a data-efficient solution strategy for the bilevel learning problem. To that end, we propose a multilevel Monte Carlo derivative-free optimization method that couples Gaussian smoothing of the upper-level objective (parameter selection) with a hierarchy of lower-level (variational regularization) solution accuracies. Being agnostic towards the specific way the lower-level problem is solved, we derive explicit oracle complexity and computational cost statements for approximately solving the entire bilevel learning problem. Our statements are parameterized by the employed lower-level solution method, thus allowing for a direct cost accounting from the variational reconstruction method to the parameter selection problem. This enables us to give a universal end-to-end complexity analysis of bilevel learning, valid for the most important optimization routines in inverse problems, rather than for a specific choice of methods.

</details>


### [261] [KKT Residual Sliding Manifolds: A Projection-Free Structured Finite-Time Solver for Nonlinear Constrained Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15545)
*Abdelhafid Zenati,Kamal Youcef-Toumi*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper reformulates Nonlinear Constrained Optimisation (NCO) as stabilisation of a unified KKT sliding residual manifold and proposes SM-KKT, a projection-free solver that drives the KKT residual into a prescribed boundary layer in finite time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NCO methods enforce KKT conditions via projections, penalties, barriers, or sequential approximations, which introduce computational complexity and convergence difficulties. Moreover, for nonconvex problems a KKT point need not be a local optimum, motivating a more robust solver.

Method: SM-KKT integrates stationarity, equality feasibility, and regularised Fischer-Burmeister complementarity into a unified residual system, whose zero set is shown equivalent to the KKT conditions. The Jacobian saddle-point inertia is characterised and shown equivalent (under assumptions) to simultaneous LICQ and SOSC satisfaction. Structured primal-dual corrections with a discrete sliding reaching law drive the residual into a boundary layer in finite time.
