Result: Four nonlinear benchmarks recover reference constrained optima with KKT residuals below 10^-6, achieving earlier boundary-layer reaching and favourable KKT accuracy versus projected primal-dual, augmented-Lagrangian, quadratic-penalty, and log-barrier baselines.

Conclusion: The inertia characterisation establishes local stability of the correction algorithm, distinguishes nondegenerate locally optimal KKT solutions from points failing second-order optimality, and yields a projection-free solver with strong empirical accuracy.

Abstract: Nonlinear Constrained Optimisation (NCO) requires simultaneous satisfaction of the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions. Existing methods enforce these conditions through projections, penalties, barriers, or sequential approximations, potentially introducing computational complexity and convergence difficulties. For nonconvex problems, a KKT point need not be a local optimum. This paper reformulates NCO as stabilisation of a unified KKT sliding residual manifold and develops SM-KKT, a projection-free solver. Stationarity, equality feasibility, and regularised Fischer-Burmeister complementarity are integrated into a unified residual system, with complementarity encoding inequality feasibility, dual feasibility, and complementary slackness. The manifold's zero set is shown to be equivalent to the KKT conditions. The required Jacobian saddle-point inertia is characterised and shown, under stated assumptions, to be equivalent to the simultaneous satisfaction of the Linear Independence Constraint Qualification (LICQ) and Second-Order Sufficient Condition (SOSC). This characterisation establishes local stability of the structured correction algorithm under these conditions and distinguishes nondegenerate locally optimal KKT solutions from points failing second-order optimality. SM-KKT computes structured primal--dual corrections and applies a discrete sliding reaching law to drive the KKT residual into a prescribed boundary layer in finite time. Four nonlinear benchmarks show recovery of reference constrained optima with KKT residuals below $10^{-6}$, earlier boundary-layer reaching, and favourable KKT accuracy compared with projected primal-dual, augmented-Lagrangian, quadratic-penalty, and log-barrier baselines.

</details>


### [262] [Preconditioned Latent Variable Proximal Point Method for Optimal Control Problems with Control Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16678)
*Nico Haaf,Roland Herzog*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: LVPP solves pointwise-constrained optimization in function spaces via smooth strictly feasible subproblems, applied to linear-quadratic elliptic optimal control with convergence, multiplier recovery, and parameter-independent preconditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pointwise-constrained optimization in function spaces is hard to solve directly; the paper seeks an iteration with strictly feasible iterates and robust numerical behavior for linear-quadratic elliptic optimal control problems.

Method: Apply LVPP, decomposing the problem into smooth subproblems; interpret the Newton step as a linear-quadratic optimal control problem with spatially varying control cost; solve Newton systems by MINRES with a block-diagonal preconditioner.

Result: Subproblems are well posed; the iteration converges for any proximity-parameter sequence with divergent sum; it yields the Lagrange multiplier as a by-product. Newton's method converges locally quadratically, and the preconditioned Newton operator's spectrum is bounded independently of proximity parameter, iterate, mesh, and observation/control domains. Confirmed numerically.

Conclusion: A complete LVPP-based algorithm for linear-quadratic elliptic optimal control is established, with convergence theory, multiplier recovery, locally quadratic Newton convergence, and mesh- and parameter-independent preconditioning.

Abstract: The latent variable proximal point (LVPP) method solves optimization problems in function spaces with pointwise constraints by a sequence of smooth subproblems whose iterates are strictly feasible. We apply it to linear-quadratic elliptic optimal control problems. Control and observation are distributed but may be restricted to subdomains. We prove that the LVPP subproblems are well posed in appropriate function spaces, that the iteration converges for every sequence of proximity parameters with divergent sum, and that it delivers the Lagrange multiplier of the constraint as a by-product. We interpret the Newton step for the LVPP subproblem as a linear-quadratic optimal control problem with a spatially varying control cost, and we show that Newton's method converges locally quadratically. The Newton systems are solved by the minimal residual method with a block-diagonal preconditioner. We prove that the spectrum of the Newton operator with respect to the inner product defined by the preconditioner is bounded independently of the proximity parameter, the iterate, the mesh and the observation and control domains. The results are combined into a complete algorithm, and tested in numerical experiments.

</details>


### [263] [Failure Modes of Interior-Point SDP Solvers on Moment--SOS Hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17261)
*Michal Kocvara*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: Standard double-precision SDP solvers can fail on high-order moment-SOS problems despite plausible-looking output. The paper identifies false convergence and stagnation failure patterns, and proposes an effective condition number as a warning indicator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Double-precision SDP solvers used in high-order moment-SOS hierarchies can produce unreliable results even when termination messages and convergence curves look regular, so practitioners need ways to detect numerical deterioration.

Method: Three polynomial optimization problems are analyzed by comparing standard double-precision computations against reference solutions from the extended-precision solver Loraine 64x4. The authors introduce an empirical effective condition number based on positive spectral components of primal and dual solution matrices, and also examine primal-dual spectral conditioning imbalance and a Cholesky inverse residuum as diagnostics.

Result: Two distinct failure patterns are observed: false convergence (smooth sequence approaching the limit at the wrong rate) and stagnation (plateau or oscillations not reflecting hierarchy convergence). The effective condition number grows alongside numerical deterioration, and the additional diagnostics are assessed as potential indicators.

Conclusion: Solver termination messages and apparently regular convergence curves are insufficient to establish reliability of high-order moment-SOS bounds; condition-number-based indicators can serve as useful warnings.

Abstract: Standard semidefinite programming (SDP) solvers working in double precision can become unreliable on difficult problems, even when their output appears plausible. We study this issue for SDP problems arising from high-order moment-SOS hierarchies. Three polynomial optimization problems are analysed by comparing standard double-precision computations with reference solutions obtained using the extended-precision solver \mbox{Loraine\,64x4}. We observe two distinct patterns of numerical failure. In \emph{false convergence}, the computed bounds form a smooth sequence but approach the limit at the wrong rate. In \emph{stagnation}, the sequence develops a plateau or oscillations and no longer reflects the convergence of the underlying hierarchy. We introduce an empirical effective condition number based on the positive spectral components of the primal and dual solution matrices. It serves as a warning indicator rather than an error bound, and its growth accompanies numerical deterioration in our examples. We also examine the imbalance between primal and dual spectral conditioning and a Cholesky inverse residuum as potential diagnostic indicators. The results show that solver termination messages and apparently regular convergence curves may be insufficient to establish the reliability of high-order moment-SOS bounds.

</details>


### [264] [Beyond periodicity: Fourier methods for optimal control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17386)
*Milena Ispiryan,Vahagn Makaryan,Lusine Poghosyan,Tigran Bakaryan*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: A Fourier-based spectral framework for finite-horizon optimal control problems that uses Eckhoff-type endpoint correction to preserve spectral convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fourier spectral methods offer compact global approximations and spectral convergence for smooth periodic functions, but their application to finite-horizon OCPs is hindered because state and control trajectories are generally nonperiodic. Endpoint mismatch creates artificial discontinuities that can destroy spectral convergence.

Method: The authors leverage Eckhoff-type endpoint-correction techniques to build a Fourier-based spectral framework for OCPs with possibly nonlinear dynamics and constraints. The correction improves convergence without enlarging the decision-variable set, giving an efficient finite-dimensional numerical method. Selected dynamics and obstacle-avoidance constraints are formulated through Sobolev residual bounds that use residual information over the whole horizon.

Result: Numerical experiments on benchmark problems show competitive computational performance, lower residual errors, and improved solution accuracy.

Conclusion: Eckhoff-type endpoint correction enables effective Fourier spectral methods for nonperiodic finite-horizon OCPs, and Sobolev residual bounds improve constraint satisfaction.

Abstract: Fourier-based spectral methods provide compact global approximations, permit simple differentiation and integration, and achieve spectral convergence for smooth periodic functions. Their use in finite-horizon optimal control problems (OCPs) is nevertheless challenged by the generally nonperiodic state and control trajectories: endpoint mismatch induces artificial discontinuities and can destroy spectral convergence. Leveraging Eckhoff-type endpoint-correction techniques, we develop a Fourier-based spectral framework for OCPs with possibly nonlinear dynamics and constraints. The correction improves convergence without enlarging the set of decision variables, yielding an efficient finite-dimensional numerical method. Furthermore, we formulate selected dynamics and obstacle-avoidance constraints through Sobolev residual bounds, which incorporate information about the residuals over the entire time horizon and improve constraint satisfaction. Numerical experiments on benchmark problems demonstrate competitive computational performance, lower residual errors, and improved solution accuracy.

</details>


### [265] [A Projection Anderson Acceleration Algorithm for Non-quasi-monotone Variational Inequalities with Hölder Continuous Terms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18370)
*Xiaojun Chen,C. T. Kelley,Lei Wang*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper introduces Projection Anderson Acceleration (PAA) for variational inequality problems with finite sums of Hölder continuous functions under σ-star strong monotonicity, establishing evaluation complexity O(log(ε⁻¹)ε^{2(ᾱ-1)}).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for VI problems typically rely on Lipschitz continuity or quasi-monotonicity assumptions. This paper addresses settings where these assumptions fail, tackling star strongly monotone VIs with Hölder continuous (rather than Lipschitz) functions.

Method: The authors propose a Projection Anderson Acceleration (PAA) algorithm where the objective function is a finite sum of Hölder continuous functions satisfying the σ-star strongly monotone condition (including σ=1 case). The analysis avoids requiring quasi-monotonicity and Lipschitz continuity.

Result: For star strongly monotone VI problems, PAA needs at most O(log(ε⁻¹)ε^{2(ᾱ-1)}) function and projection evaluations to find an ε-approximate solution, where ᾱ ∈ (0,1] is the smallest Hölder continuity exponent of the component functions. Theoretical results are validated via numerical examples.

Conclusion: PAA provides complexity guarantees for VI problems under weaker assumptions (no quasi-monotonicity or Lipschitz continuity), demonstrated on non-quasi-monotone complementarity problems and non-monotone semilinear elliptic equations with non-Lipschitz terms.

Abstract: This paper proposes a Projection Anderson Acceleration (PAA) algorithm for variational inequality (VI) problems, where the underlying function is expressed as a finite sum of Hölder continuous functions and satisfies the $σ$-star strongly monotone condition that includes the star strong monotonicity with $σ= 1$ as a special case. In the absence of quasi-monotonicity and Lipschitz continuity, we establish the evaluation complexity of the PAA algorithm for finding an approximate solution. Specifically, for the star strongly monotone VI problems, the PAA algorithm needs at most $O (\log (ε^{-1}) ε^{2 (\underlineα - 1)})$ evaluations of the function and projection for finding an $ε$-approximate solution, where $\underlineα \in (0, 1]$ is the smallest exponent of H{ö}lder continuity of the component functions. We illustrate our theoretical results by numerical examples of non-quasi-monotone complementarity problems and non-monotone semilinear elliptic equations with non-Lipschitz terms.

</details>


### [266] [Intrinsic Contraction Theory and Stabilization of Probability Measures on the Wasserstein Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14164)
*Min Kim,Joshua D. Ibrahim,Soon-Jo Chung*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper develops an intrinsic differential contraction theory for pushforward flows on the 2-Wasserstein space, introducing the Intrinsic Wasserstein Contraction Condition (IWCC) that generalizes classical contraction LMIs and yields an incremental Wasserstein ISS theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic flows can contract Wasserstein distances between probability laws even when they are not contractive in point-state space, so classical contraction theory is insufficient to capture law-level contraction.

Method: The authors build a differential contraction theory on forward-invariant subsets of the 2-Wasserstein space, quotienting out the gauge freedom of the continuity equation. They use weighted Helmholtz-Hodge decomposition and the Wasserstein contingent cone to define the weighted horizontal tangent cone (whtc), and require the contraction quadratic form to be nonpositive only on the whtc (IWCC). Sufficiency for intrinsic Wasserstein contraction is proven via an almost-everywhere Euler approximation based on the superposition principle and a Banach-valued Lebesgue differentiation theorem.

Result: IWCC generalizes the classical contraction LMI and recovers it on Dirac measures. A rotationally symmetric shear example illustrates law contraction without point-state contraction. A new comparison lemma yields an incremental Wasserstein ISS theorem; for Brockett's integrator, exponential stabilization of a class of axisymmetric laws to the uniform law on a circle is proven via a smooth feedback.

Conclusion: The paper establishes intrinsic Wasserstein contraction as a rigorous framework that captures contraction at the level of probability laws, with applications to incremental ISS and feedback stabilization of Brockett's integrator.

Abstract: Deterministic flows can contract Wasserstein distances between probability laws without being contractive in point-state space. We develop a differential contraction theory for pushforward flows on forward-invariant subsets of the 2-Wasserstein space that quotients out the gauge freedom of the continuity equation. Weighted Helmholtz-Hodge decomposition and the Wasserstein contingent cone define the weighted horizontal tangent cone (whtc). Requiring the contraction quadratic form to be nonpositive only on the whtc yields the Intrinsic Wasserstein Contraction Condition (IWCC), which generalizes the classical contraction LMI and recovers it on Dirac measures. An almost-everywhere Euler approximation based on the superposition principle and Banach-valued Lebesgue differentiation theorem proves that IWCC is sufficient for intrinsic Wasserstein contraction. Law contraction without point-state contraction is illustrated by a rotationally symmetric shear example. Finally, a new comparison lemma yields an incremental Wasserstein ISS theorem; for Brockett's integrator, we prove exponential stabilization of a class of axisymmetric laws to the uniform law on a circle via a smooth feedback.

</details>


### [267] [Sharp Damping Location for Stability of a Locally Damped Wave Equation and its Galerkin Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17539)
*Kangsheng Liu,Lihui Lu,Qiong Zhang*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper analyzes energy decay for a variable-coefficient wave equation with viscous strip damping, showing exponential stability when the damping strip touches x=0 and sharp logarithmic decay otherwise, and proves that spectral Galerkin approximations fail to be uniformly exponentially stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how the location of a localized damping region affects energy decay in wave equations with variable coefficients, particularly when some rays never enter the damped region (geometric control fails).

Method: Combines separation of variables, one-dimensional resolvent estimates, a reflection identity for the harmonic oscillator, and Gaussian quasimodes; also studies a spectral Galerkin approximation based on Dirichlet eigenfunctions of the constant-coefficient Laplacian.

Result: The semigroup is exponentially stable when the damping strip's closure meets x=0 (|η|≤δ). When the strip is strictly away from x=0, the decay rate is sharp and equals (log(2+t))^{-1}. The Galerkin-approximation semigroups converge but are not uniformly exponentially stable.

Conclusion: The damping location relative to x=0 precisely determines the energy decay rate, and standard Galerkin approximation can destroy uniform exponential stability despite convergence.

Abstract: We study the stability of the variable coefficient wave equation $u_{tt}-u_{xx}-(1+x^{2})u_{yy}+χ_{\mathbb G}u_{t}=0$ on the rectangle $(-1,1)\times(0,π)$ with Dirichlet boundary conditions, where the viscous damping acts on a vertical strip $\mathbb G=(η-δ,η+δ)\times(0,π)$. We establish a precise relationship between the location of the damping region and the rate of energy decay. We prove that the associated semigroup is exponentially stable whenever the closure of the damping strip meets the line $x=0$, i.e., $|η|\leqδ$, despite the existence of rays that never enter the damped region. When the damping region is strictly bounded away from $x=0$, the semigroup decays at the sharp rate $(\log(2+t))^{-1}$. The proofs combine separation of variables, one-dimensional resolvent estimates, a reflection identity for the harmonic oscillator, and Gaussian quasimodes. We then consider the spectral Galerkin approximation based on the Dirichlet eigenfunctions of the constant coefficient Laplacian and show that, although the sequence of semigroups resulting from the approximation converges to the continuous one, it fails to be uniformly exponentially stable.

</details>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [268] [Toward a Multimodal Foundation Model for Plasma State Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15076)
*Bin Xia,Chuanfei Dong,Boting Li,Yilan Qin,Zikang Xie,Sung Hyun Son,Adam J. Stanier,Jongsoo Yoo,Hantao Ji,Ahmed Diallo,Han Ding,John Wise*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A 5.5M-parameter multimodal 3D U-Net reconstructs magnetic fields and electron density from incomplete plasma diagnostics in synthetic VPIC reconnection simulations, showing bidirectional cross-modal predictive coupling via masking-based training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasma experiments observe coupled physical states only through sparse, heterogeneous, and incomplete diagnostics. A foundation model that reconstructs full plasma states from partial observations is needed, motivated by the FLARE facility.

Method: Trained a 5.5M-parameter residual 3D U-Net on 250 synthetic VPIC simulations (225 train / 25 disjoint validation) at fixed electron beta 0.2. Combines spatial-only pooling (preserving all 24 temporal frames) with global spatiotemporal self-attention and rotary positional encoding. Independently masks magnetic and density modalities across random, grid, block, and temporal patterns, enabling sparse sensing, cross-modal completion, super-resolution, and temporal completion with one weight set and no task-specific retraining.

Result: With density fully hidden, 0.1% magnetic spatial visibility cuts median density NRMSE from ~0.9 to 0.1. Observed density improves current-density reconstruction when magnetic data are absent or sparse. With no magnetic observations, ~1% density visibility nears the density accuracy from fully observed magnetic fields, though current structure remains more dependent on direct magnetic measurements. Full magnetic conditioning stabilizes completion of missing future density frames.

Conclusion: Bidirectional cross-modal predictive coupling exists within this simulation regime, demonstrating a proof of concept for multimodal plasma state reconstruction. Generalization across plasma regimes or to experimental measurements remains untested.

Abstract: Plasma experiments observe coupled physical states through sparse, heterogeneous, and incomplete diagnostics. We present a proof of concept toward a multimodal foundation model for plasma state reconstruction during magnetic reconnection, motivated by the Facility for Laboratory Reconnection Experiments (FLARE). Using 250 synthetic VPIC simulations at fixed reference electron beta $β_{e0}=0.2$, with 225 training runs and 25 disjoint validation runs, we train a 5.5M-parameter residual 3D U-Net to reconstruct three magnetic-field components and electron density from incomplete observations. The model combines spatial-only pooling, which preserves all 24 temporal frames, with global spatiotemporal self-attention and rotary positional encoding. Independently masking magnetic and density modalities across random, grid, block, and temporal patterns enables sparse sensing, cross-modal completion, density super-resolution, and temporal completion with a single set of weights and no task-specific retraining. With density fully hidden, just 0.1% magnetic spatial visibility reduces median density NRMSE from approximately 0.9 to 0.1. Conversely, observed density improves current-density reconstruction when magnetic data are absent or extremely sparse. With no magnetic observations, roughly 1% density visibility approaches the density reconstruction accuracy achieved with fully observed magnetic fields, although current structure remains more dependent on direct magnetic measurements. Full magnetic conditioning also stabilizes completion of missing future density frames. These results demonstrate bidirectional cross-modal predictive coupling within this simulation regime. Generalization across plasma regimes or to experimental measurements remains untested.

</details>


### [269] [MIRRA: Fast, High-Fidelity Molecular Generation with Chemistry-Informed Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17244)
*Ge Sun,Juan J. de Pablo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MIRRA is a discrete flow-matching molecular generator that keeps full chemical building-block vocabularies available while computing identity probabilities only when needed, achieving faster and higher-fidelity generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast, high-fidelity molecular generation needs accurate chemical choices and efficient sampling. Chemical building blocks shrink graph size but turn each node into a choice among tens of thousands of related identities, making full-vocabulary sampling costly.

Method: MIRRA (Molecular Identity Representation, Refresh and Assembly) is a discrete flow-matching generator that learns from chemical knowledge, shares information across related blocks via chemical features, applies learned identity corrections in molecular context, and decides which positions to refresh before computing their probabilities—evaluating identity probabilities only when needed while keeping the entire vocabulary available.

Result: Versus strongest per-task baselines: chemical and graph distribution errors on ZINC250k drop 29-31% and 53-60%, with generation up to 8.6x faster. Property targeting gives an estimated 7.8-20.8x more distinct novel hits per second. On a held-out polymer test set, MIRRA reaches 100% periodic validity with known chain topology versus 57.0% for a polymer VAE.

Conclusion: MIRRA demonstrates fast, high-fidelity molecular generation while keeping the full chemical vocabulary available throughout sampling.

Abstract: Fast, high-fidelity molecular generation requires accurate chemical choices and efficient sampling. Chemical building blocks reduce graph size, but make each node a choice among tens of thousands of related identities. We introduce MIRRA (Molecular Identity Representation, Refresh and Assembly), a discrete flow-matching generator that learns from chemical knowledge and evaluates identity probabilities only when needed, while keeping every identity in its vocabulary available. Chemical features share information across related blocks, and learned identity corrections distinguish them in molecular context. By deciding which positions will refresh before computing their probabilities, the sampler avoids unused predictions while preserving each sampling transition. Against the strongest baselines in each task, MIRRA reduces chemical and graph distribution errors on ZINC250k by 29-31% and 53-60%, respectively, with generation up to 8.6 times faster. Property targeting yields an estimated 7.8-20.8 times more distinct novel hits per second. On a held-out polymer test set, MIRRA achieves 100% periodic validity with known chain topology, versus 57.0% for a polymer variational autoencoder. These results demonstrate fast, high-fidelity generation with the full chemical vocabulary available throughout sampling.

</details>


### [270] [Structure-preserving fidelity diagnostics for partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17483)
*Kyriakos Flouris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ML surrogates of PDEs trained on numerical solver data may learn discretization error, not physics, and test loss cannot detect this. The authors propose structure-preserving fidelity diagnostics—simple scores checking conserved quantities, symmetries, or Hamiltonian structure without needing training data or the governing equation—which correlate with true-dynamics error up to 0.94 rank correlation and distinguish schemes that test loss treats as equivalent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training data for PDE surrogates come from numerical solvers, so they obey the modified equation of the scheme rather than the true PDE. Test loss on same-solver data cannot tell whether a model learned physics or just the discretization error, creating a hidden fidelity problem.

Method: Proposes a suite of structure-preserving fidelity diagnostics: simple scores measuring whether a learned model respects known physical properties (conserved quantity, symmetry, Hamiltonian structure), requiring no training data or governing equation.

Result: Across five systems, diagnostics track error vs. true dynamics with rank correlation up to 0.94, while test loss is uncorrelated or anti-correlated. For Burgers, Korteweg-de Vries, and Navier-Stokes, diagnostics separate numerical schemes that test loss ranks as equivalent, including same-order schemes.

Conclusion: Structure-preserving diagnostics reveal model fidelity invisible to standard test loss, offering a practical tool to distinguish physics-learning from discretization-error-learning in PDE surrogates.

Abstract: Machine learning surrogates of partial differential equations (PDEs) are commonly trained on data generated by numerical solvers. Therefore, the training data obey not the PDE but the modified equation of the numerical scheme, and the test loss, computed on data from the same solver, cannot distinguish a model that learned the physics from one that learned the discretization error. Here, we show that the difference is nevertheless visible in the trained model. We propose a suite of structure-preserving fidelity diagnostics, simple scores that measure whether a learned model respects one known property of the physics, such as a conserved quantity, a symmetry or a Hamiltonian structure, without access to the training data or the governing equation. Over five systems, the diagnostics track the error with respect to the true dynamics with a rank correlation of up to $0.94$, while the test loss is uncorrelated or anti-correlated with it. For the Burgers, Korteweg-de Vries and Navier-Stokes equations, the diagnostics separate numerical schemes that the test loss ranks as equivalent, including schemes of the same order. order.

</details>


### [271] [Deep Mesoscopic Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17885)
*Yue Zhao,Shiqi Wu,Yihang Ouyang,Sergey Grebenchuk,Magdalena Grzeszczyk,Xiaoxu Zhao,Kostya S. Novoselov,Maciej Koperski,Qianxiao Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepMesoD learns effective mesoscopic field equations from microscopic trajectories at a prescribed coarse-graining scale using neural networks, validated on Ising-Kac, Heisenberg ferromagnet, and CrBr3 systems with large speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microscopic simulations capture spatial patterns but are limited to small domains and short timescales, while bulk descriptions average out these patterns. Mesoscopic fields preserve spatial organization, but effective equations exist only for selected microscopic systems and scaling limits.

Method: DeepMesoD uses neural networks to learn an effective mesoscopic field equation from microscopic trajectories at a prescribed coarse-graining scale. For nonconserved dissipative dynamics, it learns a free-energy functional with a spatial interaction kernel and a state-dependent dissipation operator constrained by the fluctuation-dissipation relation.

Result: Validated on Ising-Kac (recovers known hydrodynamic limit, stable rollouts beyond training horizon). For a 2D Heisenberg ferromagnet, predictions match atomistic references up to 1 ns and remain bounded to 25 ns with unseen initial conditions. For layered CrBr3, the learned kernel captures suppression and sign reversal of interlayer coupling at a stacking fault. Achieves 3-4 orders of magnitude reduction in wall-clock time versus atomistic spin dynamics.

Conclusion: DeepMesoD can learn effective mesoscopic descriptions for systems lacking closed-form mesoscopic equations, capturing emergent physics like stacking-fault effects and delivering major computational speedups.

Abstract: Microscopic simulations resolve spatial patterns but are feasible only over small domains and short timescales, whereas bulk descriptions erase those patterns through spatial averaging. Mesoscopic fields preserve this spatial organisation, but their effective equations are known only for selected microscopic systems and scaling limits. We introduce Deep Mesoscopic Dynamics (DeepMesoD), which uses neural networks to learn an effective mesoscopic field equation from microscopic trajectories at a prescribed coarse-graining scale. For the nonconserved dissipative dynamics studied here, DeepMesoD learns the free-energy functional with a spatial interaction kernel and a state-dependent dissipation operator constrained by the fluctuation-dissipation relation. We first validate the construction in the Ising-Kac model, where the learned mesoscopic equation recovers the known hydrodynamic limit and rollouts remain stable far beyond the training horizon. We then treat two systems without closed-form mesoscopic descriptions. For a two-dimensional Heisenberg ferromagnet governed by atomistic spin dynamics, DeepMesoD learns a mesoscopic model whose predictions match atomistic references up to 1 ns, and a rollout with an unseen initial condition remains bounded up to 25 ns. For layered CrBr$_3$, the learned kernel captures the suppression and sign reversal of interlayer coupling at a stacking fault whose location and strength are not supplied as inputs. Controlled changes to the learned interaction show how fault number and placement shape multistep magnetic responses, complementing our magneto-photoluminescence measurements. Across the two atomistic systems, DeepMesoD achieves a three-to-four-order-of-magnitude reduction in wall-clock time compared with atomistic spin dynamics.

</details>


### [272] [Generalized PINN-Based Inverse Estimation of Spatially Varying Heat Transfer Coefficients with Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14008)
*Pradipan Maitra,Jariful Hassan,Arijit Hazra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores generalized boundary representations and transfer learning for PINN-based inverse estimation of spatially varying convective heat transfer coefficients (HTCs) in jet impingement cooling, comparing PWL and neural-network estimators against CFD-CHT references and an Adjoint-FEM benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional PINN formulations assume a prescribed quadratic form for the HTC profile, which cannot accommodate general spatially varying HTC distributions. Inverse estimation is also computationally costly when repeated across many targets, motivating the need for more flexible representations and efficient reuse via transfer learning.

Method: Piecewise-linear (PWL) and neural-network boundary estimators are inferred from transient interior-temperature measurements at Re = 5000, 10000, 15000, and evaluated against CFD-CHT reference HTC profiles. A discrete Adjoint-FEM formulation with H^1-seminorm gradient regularization serves as an independent inverse-method comparison. Transfer learning is tested under two conditions: noise-level transfer at fixed Reynolds number, and Gaussian-profile-to-CFD-CHT transfer across Reynolds numbers.

Result: For Re = 5000 with 5% noise, quadratic, PWL, and NN PINNs yield HTC-profile relative errors of 5.08%, 5.31%, and 5.19%. Generalized representations cut the Re = 15000 error from 16.76% (quadratic) to 14.41% (PWL) and 14.46% (NN). Transfer learning reduces target-training time by 57.86-81.71% (fixed-Re transfer) and 50.59-84.94% (Gaussian-to-CHT transfer). Accuracy effects are target dependent: PWL fixed-profile transfer stays within 0.30 pp of scratch training, while Gaussian-to-CHT differences range 0.10-1.52 pp.

Conclusion: Generalized PINN representations can capture spatially varying HTC profiles without a prescribed quadratic form, and transfer learning substantially lowers target-training cost for repeated inverse estimation, with reconstruction accuracy depending on source-target compatibility.

Abstract: This study investigates generalized boundary representations and transfer learning for physics-informed neural-network (PINN) estimation of spatially varying convective heat transfer coefficients (HTCs) in jet impingement cooling. Piecewise-linear (PWL) and neural-network boundary estimators are inferred from transient interior-temperature measurements and evaluated against CFD-CHT reference HTC profiles at Reynolds numbers of 5000, 10000 and 15000. A discrete Adjoint-FEM formulation with gradient regularization based on the $H^1$-seminorm provides an independent inverse-method comparison. Transfer learning is investigated under two source-target conditions: transfer between noise levels at the same Reynolds number, and transfer from a controlled Gaussian-profile problem to CFD-CHT-derived target problems at different Reynolds numbers. For the representative Re = 5000 target with 5% measurement noise, quadratic, PWL, and neural-network PINNs achieve relative HTC-profile errors of 5.08, 5.31 and 5.19%, respectively, while generalized representations reduce the error for the Re = 15000 target from 16.76% for the quadratic PINN to 14.41% and 14.46%. Transfer learning reduces target-training time by 57.86-81.71% for fixed-Reynolds-number transfer and by 50.59-84.94% for Gaussian-to-CHT transfer. Its effect on reconstruction accuracy is target dependent: fixed-profile PWL transfer remains within 0.30 percentage points of scratch training, whereas Gaussian-to-CHT transfer produces differences of 0.10-1.52 percentage points across targets and representations. These results demonstrate that generalized PINN representations can accommodate spatially varying HTC profiles without a prescribed quadratic form, while transfer learning can substantially reduce target-training cost for repeated inverse estimation, with the resulting reconstruction accuracy depending on source-target compatibility.

</details>


<div id='math-ph'></div>

# math-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [273] [Dynamical BEC formation and vanishing entropy for trapped bosons in non-relativistic QED via nonlinear resonance cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17923)
*Thomas Chen,Ali Mezher*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper rigorously proves dynamical formation of a complete Bose-Einstein condensate and asymptotic purification of trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously establish that radiative relaxation irreversibly depletes all excited trap modes, driving even genuinely mixed initial states to a pure condensate, despite global unitary evolution.

Method: Track the macroscopic one-particle density matrix κ_T through a nonlinear resonance cascade, expressed as an average over de Finetti measure of mean-field particle-field orbits. Derive the Hartree hierarchy from the finite-N BBGKY hierarchy, taking limits successively: N→∞, weak-coupling, then T→∞.

Result: Under a non-vanishing initial ground state component and strictly positive downward Fermi-Golden-Rule rates: strong trace-norm convergence to a pure condensate, lim_{T→∞} ‖κ_T − |χ_0⟩⟨χ_0|‖ = 0, and asymptotic vanishing von Neumann entropy lim_{T→∞} S[κ_T] = 0, with no monotonicity claim. Any initially mixed, positive-entropy state is purified to the ground state.

Conclusion: Although the global particle-field evolution remains unitary, energy and correlations are continuously radiated into the dispersive field, so the macroscopic one-particle state converges to a pure state.

Abstract: The dynamical formation of a complete Bose-Einstein condensate and the asymptotic purification of trapped bosons coupled to a quantized radiation field are rigorously established. By tracking the macroscopic one-particle density matrix $κ_T$ through a nonlinear resonance cascade, it is demonstrated that radiative relaxation irreversibly depletes all excited trap modes, even when the initial macroscopic state $κ_0$ is genuinely mixed. Here $κ_T$ is an average, with respect to a de Finetti measure $μ_0$, over mean-field particle-field orbits, so that the mixedness of $κ_0$ reflects a statistical ensemble of orbits. Under the assumptions of a non-vanishing initial ground state component and strictly positive downward Fermi-Golden-Rule rates, strong trace-norm convergence to a pure condensate is proven, $$
  \lim_{T\to\infty} \| κ_T-|χ_0\rangle\langleχ_0| \|_{\mathfrak{S}_1(\mathfrak{h})} = 0 \, . $$ Furthermore, the von Neumann entropy of the macroscopic one-particle density matrix is shown to vanish asymptotically, $$
  \lim_{T\to\infty}S[κ_T]=0 \, , $$ without any claim of monotonicity in $T$. Thus, any initially mixed, positive-entropy state ($0<S[κ_0]<\infty$) is purified to the ground state $|χ_0\rangle\langleχ_0|$. For the proof, we derive the Hartree hierarchy of the coupled system of bosons and photons from the finite-$N$ BBGKY hierarchy of the microscopic quantum many-body system, establishing trace-norm convergence in the mean-field limit as $N\to\infty$. The limits are taken successively; first $N\to\infty$, then the weak-coupling limit, and finally $T\to\infty$. Although the global particle-field evolution remains unitary, the macroscopic one-particle state converges to a pure state as energy and correlations are continuously radiated into the dispersive field.

</details>

# cs.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [274] [Orientation Estimation of Cryo-EM Images for Molecules with Arbitrary Symmetry by Semidefinite Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18691)
*Yunpeng Shi,Amit Singer,Jinxin Wang,Ruiyi Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Extends the non-unique games framework to symmetric structures, yielding a unified SDP relaxation solved via ADMM for all point group symmetries, applied to cryo-EM orientation estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for symmetric problems (e.g., cryo-EM orientation estimation) are tailored to specific symmetry groups, lacking a general method. The paper seeks a unified framework to handle all point group symmetries.

Method: Formulates a minimization over group elements with a finite symmetry subgroup S. A semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxation turns it into a convex program, solved using the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM).

Result: Demonstrated on cryo-EM orientation estimation for both simulated and experimental datasets, generating high-quality ab initio models from only a few class averages of experimental images.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides a unified computational framework for all point group symmetries, generalizing prior symmetry-specific approaches and proving effective in practice.

Abstract: This paper extends the non-unique games framework introduced in Bandeira et al. (2020) to address symmetric structures. Precisely, we consider the problem \[ \begin{aligned}
  \underset{g_1,\ldots,g_n\in \mathcal{G}}{\operatorname{min}}\,\, \frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i,j=1}^n \sum_{s\in S} f_{ij}(g_i s g_j^{-1}), \end{aligned} \] where $\mathcal{G}$ is a compact group, $S\subset \mathcal{G}$ is a finite subgroup of symmetry elements, and $f_{ij}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is a loss function. A notable example is common lines based orientation estimation of cryo-EM images for molecules with point group symmetry $S$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is the rotation group $\operatorname{SO}(3)$. With a semidefinite programming relaxation, we turn the problem into a convex program that is solved with the alternating direction method of multipliers. Unlike existing approaches which are tailored to specific symmetry groups, ours provides a unified computational framework for addressing all point group symmetries. We demonstrate the applicability of the proposed algorithm on the cryo-EM orientation estimation problem on both simulated and experimental datasets. In particular, our algorithm is effective in generating high-quality ab initio models from only a few class averages computed from experimental images.

</details>

### [275] [Magpie: A Graphical Magnon Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15790)
*Tobias Weber*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Magpie is an open-source, graphical linear spin-wave software focused on 3D visualization of magnetic models and magnon dispersion relations, built in portable C++ with a modular architecture for extensibility and interoperability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic models and magnon dispersion relations are typically handled by non-graphical or non-interoperable tools. There is a need for a fully graphical, open-source software that visualizes magnetic models and magnon dispersions in 3D.

Method: The software is written in high-performance, portable C++ with a modular architecture enabling rapid development of new calculation modules, integration into external applications, and code generators for interoperability with other linear spin-wave libraries.

Result: Magpie provides full graphical 3D visualization of magnetic models and magnon dispersion relations, supporting modular extension and seamless interoperability with other linear spin-wave libraries.

Conclusion: Magpie is a portable, open-source, modular graphical tool that advances linear spin-wave calculations by combining 3D visualization, easy extensibility, and interoperability.

Abstract: Magpie is a fully graphical linear spin-wave software which has its main focus on 3-d visualisation of magnetic models and magnon dispersion relations. The software is distributed under an open-source license and is written in high-performance, portable C++. Its modular architecture allows for a rapid development of new calculation modules and permits the inclusion into external applications. Included code generators ensure seamless interoperability with other linear spin-wave libraries.

</details>


### [276] [Honeycomb Crystal in a Mass-Asymmetric Electron-Hole Bilayer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17030)
*Shengdu Chai,Wangqian Miao,Yuqiang Li,Lei Wang,Xi Dai,X. C. Xie*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Correlated mobile electrons in a strongly mass-asymmetric electron-hole bilayer can stabilize a honeycomb electron-hole crystal over the usual triangular Wigner crystal, with transitions tunable by density and accessible in a proposed BLG/hBN/WS2-WSe2 platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In a uniform positive background, electrons form a triangular Wigner crystal at low density. In mass-asymmetric electron-hole bilayers, localized heavy holes create a nonuniform positive background enabling crystallization at much higher densities. The paper asks whether correlated mobile electrons can overturn the preference for triangular order and stabilize other lattices like honeycomb.

Method: Real-space neural-network variational Monte Carlo in the Born-Oppenheimer limit with fixed holes. Mapping to a half-filled Hubbard model to interpret spin and transport states.

Result: Increasing density drives a transition from a triangular electron-hole crystal to a honeycomb crystal, which eventually melts into itinerant electrons coexisting with honeycomb or triangular hole lattices at fixed interlayer separation. Honeycomb order emerges when kinetic energy and electron-hole attraction gains outweigh higher intralayer Coulomb repulsion—a threshold unreachable in the rigid point-dipole model. The honeycomb hole lattice supports paramagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and stripe-ordered spin states; complex polarization indicates a metal-insulator transition.

Conclusion: States on the honeycomb hole lattice are consistent with a stripe-ordered Dirac semimetal and paramagnetic or antiferromagnetic Mott insulators. A BLG/hBN/WS2-WSe2 heterostructure is proposed as a platform for realizing the honeycomb crystal at experimentally feasible density n~10^11 cm^-2.

Abstract: A two-dimensional electron gas in a uniform neutralizing background forms a triangular Wigner crystal at low density. In a strongly mass-asymmetric electron-hole bilayer, localized heavy holes instead provide a nonuniform positive background that supports an electron crystal at densities two orders of magnitude higher than for a homogeneous electron gas in the same material. Here we show that correlated mobile electrons can overturn the preference for triangular order, stabilizing a honeycomb electron-hole crystal. Using the powerful real-space neural-network variational Monte Carlo in the Born-Oppenheimer limit with fixed holes, we find that increasing the density drives a transition from a triangular electron-hole crystal to a honeycomb crystal, which eventually melts into itinerant electrons coexisting with the honeycomb or the triangular hole lattice at fixed interlayer separation. Relative to triangular order, honeycomb order emerges when gains in electronic kinetic energy and electron-hole attraction outweigh its higher intralayer Coulomb repulsion, a threshold never reached in the rigid point-dipole model. Moreover, the honeycomb hole lattice supports paramagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and stripe-ordered spin states. Complex polarization indicates a metal-insulator transition, while the states on the honeycomb hole lattice are consistent with a stripe-ordered Dirac semimetal and paramagnetic or antiferromagnetic Mott insulators via mapping to the half-filled Hubbard model. We further propose a BLG/hBN/WS$_2$-WSe$_2$ heterostructure as a platform for the predicted honeycomb crystal, at an experimentally feasible density $n\sim10^{11}~\text{cm}^{-2}$.

</details>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [277] [Walk-on-Ambient-Patch: Solver-Adapted Isometric Embeddings for Projected Walk on Spheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15573)
*Yunfeng Liao,Xiucheng Li*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: WoAP improves Projected Walk on Spheres for solving surface PDEs by treating the embedding as a solver variable, using edge-isometry constraints and tangent-point repulsion to separate folded regions, with analysis of the geometry-sampling tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PWoS solves surface PDEs via ambient sampling and closest-point projection, but tightly folded embeddings force short steps or ambiguous projections even when the intrinsic geometry is simple.

Method: Walk-on-Ambient-Patch (WoAP) treats the embedding as a solver variable. It applies edge-isometry constraints and tangent-point repulsion to separate intrinsically distant regions, optionally in higher codimension. Patch states track residual projection branches without introducing PDE boundaries.

Result: Analysis of a geometry-sampling tradeoff: extra dimensions give separation freedom but reduce uniform tangent progress, while strong tangent-biased proposals incur large importance-weight moments. Folded-surface experiments show reduced projection conflicts, a hard-constraint pilot verifies metric preservation, and an internal fixed-RMSE comparison favors higher dimensions at stricter accuracy targets.

Conclusion: The embedding and projection state should be chosen jointly with the stochastic solver.

Abstract: Projected Walk on Spheres (PWoS) solves surface PDEs through ambient sampling and closest-point projection, but tightly folded embeddings can force short steps or ambiguous projections despite simple intrinsic geometry. We introduce Walk-on-Ambient-Patch (WoAP), which treats the embedding as a solver variable. Edge-isometry constraints and tangent-point repulsion separate intrinsically distant regions, optionally in higher codimension; patch states track residual projection branches without introducing PDE boundaries. We analyze the resulting geometry--sampling tradeoff: additional dimensions provide separation freedom but reduce uniform tangent progress, while strong tangent-biased proposals incur large importance-weight moments. Folded-surface experiments demonstrate reduced projection conflicts, a separate hard-constraint pilot verifies metric preservation, and an internal fixed-RMSE comparison favors higher dimensions at stricter accuracy targets. Together, these results motivate choosing the embedding and projection state jointly with the stochastic solver.

</details>
